### [1] [Breakdown of material-time scaling in the microscopic dynamics of metallic glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02426)
*Till Böhmer,Jie Shen,Jan Philipp Gabriel,Rolf Zeißler,Federico Zontone,Fan Yang,Eloi Pineda,Beatrice Ruta,Thomas Blochowicz*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Material time describes the evolution of average structural properties of a metallic glass during physical aging, but the underlying microscopic dynamics violate material-time scaling, revealing a transient stress-mediated relaxation that disappears upon equilibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical aging in glasses is conventionally described by the concept of material time, which assumes the relaxation mechanism stays identical to equilibrium while only its rate changes. However, it is unclear whether this concept captures the microscopic dynamics of metallic glasses, whose dynamics differ qualitatively between the glassy and equilibrium supercooled liquid states.

Method: Simultaneous time-resolved X-ray diffraction and X-ray photon-correlation spectroscopy (XPCS) were used to follow the full equilibration of a metallic glass after small temperature jumps (as small as 4 K) starting from equilibrium.

Result: The first sharp diffraction peak position shows the up-/down-jump asymmetry characteristic of material-time aging and is quantitatively captured by the Tool-Narayanaswamy-Moynihan (TNM) model. In contrast, the microscopic density-density autocorrelation functions strongly violate material-time scaling. Compressed-exponential relaxation emerges after temperature changes as small as 4 K, independent of jump direction, and continuously evolves into stretched-exponential relaxation as the average structure equilibrates.

Conclusion: Material time can describe the evolution of average structural properties even when the corresponding microscopic dynamics do not obey material-time scaling. The observed behavior is consistent with a transient stress-mediated contribution linked to volumetric frustration that vanishes upon equilibration.

Abstract: Physical aging in glasses is often described using the concept of material time, which assumes the underlying relaxation mechanism to remain the same as in equilibrium, but with a rate that changes during aging. Whether material time also captures the microscopic dynamics of metallic glasses, which differ qualitatively between the glass and the equilibrium supercooled liquid, is unclear. Using simultaneous time-resolved X-ray diffraction and X-ray photon-correlation spectroscopy, we follow the full equilibration of a metallic glass after small temperature jumps starting from equilibrium. The time evolution of the first sharp diffraction peak position exhibits the asymmetry between temperature up- and down-jumps characteristic of material-time aging and is quantitatively described by the Tool-Narayanaswamy-Moynihan model, which is based on this concept. By contrast, the microscopic density-density autocorrelation functions strongly violate material-time scaling. Compressed-exponential relaxation emerges even after temperature changes as small as 4K, irrespective of the direction of the temperature jump, and continuously evolves into a stretched-exponential relaxation shape following the equilibration of the average structure. This behavior is consistent with a transient stress-mediated contribution associated with volumetric frustration that disappears upon equilibration. Our results show that a material time can describe the evolution of average structural properties even when the corresponding microscopic dynamics do not obey material-time scaling.

</details>


### [2] [Universal energy barrier for plastic flow in a network glass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02454)
*Ibrahim Ghanem,Richard Jana,Wolfram G. Nöhring,Michael Moseler,Lars Pastewka*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Using a machine-learned interatomic potential, the authors show that a shear transformation in amorphous carbon reduces to breaking/forming a single covalent bond, whose bond length serves as the reaction coordinate. Extracted energy landscapes collapse onto a universal barrier shape, with the barrier height set by a single bond-specific scale (bond-jump distance) via a cubic law, enabling prediction of plastic flow stress from bond-level quantities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shear transformations are elementary events of plasticity in glasses and underpin mesoscopic models of plastic flow, but their atomic origin and energetics are difficult to establish.

Method: Machine-learned interatomic potential applied to amorphous carbon as a representative network glass; identification of the shear transformation reaction coordinate (bond length); extraction of energy landscapes and barriers; analysis of barrier-shape collapse and activation statistics.

Result: A shear transformation corresponds to breaking or formation of a single covalent bond. All energy landscapes collapse onto a universal barrier shape, with a single bond-specific scale, the bond-jump distance, fixing barrier height through a cubic law. Combined with activation statistics, this yields a model predicting plastic flow stress from independently determined bond-level quantities.

Conclusion: The findings provide microscopic support for phenomenological models of plastic flow in glasses and suggest a route toward a quantitative description of amorphous plasticity.

Abstract: Shear transformations are the elementary events of plasticity in glasses and underpin many mesoscopic models of plastic flow, yet their atomic origin and energetics remain difficult to establish. Here we use a machine-learned interatomic potential to show that, in amorphous carbon as a representative network glass, a shear transformation corresponds to the breaking or formation of a single covalent bond. The simplicity of the associated reaction coordinate, the bond length, enables us to extract energy landscapes and barriers. Despite the intrinsic structural disorder, we find that all energy landscapes collapse onto a universal barrier shape. What remains bond-specific is a single scale, the bond-jump distance, which fixes the barrier height through a cubic law. Combined with the activation statistics of shear transformations, this yields a model that predicts the plastic flow stress from independently determined bond-level quantities. Our results provide microscopic support for phenomenological models of plastic flow in glasses and suggest a route towards a quantitative description of amorphous plasticity.

</details>


### [3] [Quantum transmission in 1D disordered stealthy hyperuniform Kronig--Penney-like models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02501)
*Shaobing Yuan,Carlo Vanoni,Salvatore Torquato*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: First perturbation theory of 1D quantum transport through a continuum of identical point scatterers with stealthy hyperuniform (SHU) positional disorder, showing exact cancellation of lower-order Lyapunov exponent terms and suppression of the fourth-order term.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disordered SHU systems are emerging platforms for controlling classical and quantum transport because single-scattering contributions vanish in the stealthy regime, yet no perturbation theory existed for 1D quantum transport through a continuum of identical point scatterers with SHU disorder.

Method: A perturbative treatment of the Lyapunov exponent λ(k) for 1D quantum transport through a continuum of identical point scatterers whose positions follow SHU disorder, exploiting the Fourier-space stealthy condition S(q)=0 and the real-space hyperuniform constraint lim_{D→∞} σ_D^2 = c < ∞.

Result: All lower-order terms cancel exactly due to the stealthy condition S(q)=0, and the lowest non-vanishing fourth-order contribution to the Lyapunov exponent λ(k) is further suppressed by the real-space hyperuniform constraint.

Conclusion: The findings reveal universal implications for general wave-propagation phenomena in disordered SHU systems.

Abstract: Disordered stealthy hyperuniform (SHU) systems are emerging as platforms for controlling classical and quantum transport due to vanishing single-scattering contributions in the stealthy regime. Here, to our knowledge, we present the first perturbation theory of one-dimensional quantum transport through a continuum of identical point scatterers with SHU positional disorder. Our perturbation theory indicates the exact cancellation of all the lower-order terms due to the Fourier-space stealthy condition $S(q)=0$, and the further suppression of the lowest non-vanishing fourth order in the Lyapunov exponent $λ(k)$ due to the real-space hyperuniform constraint $\lim\limits_{D\to\infty}σ_D^2=c<\infty$. Our findings presents universal implications of the general wave-propagation phenomena in disordered SHU systems.

</details>


### [4] [Cup and Cap Topological Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03169)
*Marta Niedostatek,Ferran Hernandez Caralt,Runyue Wang,Federica Baccini,Lorenzo Giambagli,Pietro Lió,Ginestra Bianconi*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Cup and Cap Topological Neural Network (CCNN), which uses cup and cap products to overcome the layer-wise dimensional limitation of boundary-operator- and Hodge-Laplacian-based Topological Deep Learning, enabling direct cross-dimensional interactions. CCNN learns node-based 0-cochains by capturing many-body interactions such as triangles, and achieves competitive performance on TopoBench against other simplicial complex neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most Topological Deep Learning (TDL) methods rely on boundary operators and Hodge Laplacians. This design restricts each layer so that features and signals cannot be lifted or lowered by more than one dimension at a time, limiting the modeling of interactions spanning multiple dimensions.

Method: The authors adopt cup and cap products and formulate the Cup and Cap Topological Neural Network (CCNN), a topological deep learning architecture that learns node-based variables (0-cochains) while accounting for many-body interactions (e.g., triangles) in the data.

Result: CCNN is validated on the TopoBench datasets, where it demonstrates competitive performance relative to other simplicial complex neural networks.

Conclusion: Using cup and cap products provides an effective way to bypass the one-dimension-per-layer restriction of existing TDL approaches, and CCNN is a competitive simplicial neural network architecture for learning higher-order interactions.

Abstract: Topological Deep Learning (TDL) is designed to learn features associated with higher-dimensional simplices including not only nodes but also edges, triangles and so on. However, most TDL approaches, being based on boundary operators and Hodge Laplacians, have the limitation that features and signals cannot be lifted or lowered across more than one dimension per layer. To overcome this limitation, in this work, we propose the adoption of the cup and cap products. Specifically, we formulate the Cup and Cap Topological Neural Network (CCNN), a topological deep learning architecture designed to learn node-based variables (or 0-cochains) by taking into account their many-body interactions (e.g. triangles) present in the data. We validate CCNN by assessing its performance on the TopoBench datasets, revealing its competitiveness with respect to other simplicial complex neural networks.

</details>


### [5] [Discrete Scale Invariance of Ising Mesons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35940)
*Denis V. Vasilyev,Abbas Ali Saberi*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Discrete scale invariance (DSI) is shown to emerge from the internal motion of a composite excitation (a meson) in a marginal 1/r^4 transverse-field Ising chain, rather than from resonant few-body dynamics. The inverse-square kink attraction strength and the two-kink threshold curvature fix the scale-anomaly exponent, which governs the meson hierarchy, binding-energy/size ratios, logarithmic level accumulation, and log-periodic scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete scale invariance conventionally originates from resonant few-body or singular scale-invariant dynamics. The paper seeks an alternative, many-body microscopic origin—specifically whether DSI can arise in the internal motion of a composite excitation in an ordered quantum magnet.

Method: Study of the marginal 1/r^4 transverse-field Ising chain, where long-range bonds generate an asymptotic inverse-square attraction between dressed domain walls (kinks). The analysis uses the dressed two-kink threshold curvature to set the relative kinetic scale, supported by full-spin exact diagonalization and a fourth-order weak-field Hamiltonian.

Result: The inverse-square attraction strength (fixed by spontaneous magnetization) and the two-kink threshold curvature independently determine the scale-anomaly exponent. In the supercritical regime this exponent controls geometric binding-energy and size ratios, logarithmic level accumulation, and log-periodic threshold scattering. Exact diagonalization and the fourth-order Hamiltonian confirm coupled energy–size scaling within the finite window between core and ring effects.

Conclusion: The work provides a microscopic many-body realization of quantum limit-cycle physics, with universal scaling fixed by magnetic order and kink mobility, establishing an emergent route to discrete scale invariance in ordered quantum magnets.

Abstract: Discrete scale invariance often arises from resonant few-body or singular scale-invariant dynamics. We show that it can instead emerge in the internal motion of a composite excitation in an ordered quantum magnet. In the marginal $1/r^4$ transverse-field Ising chain, long-range bonds generate an asymptotic inverse-square attraction between dressed domain walls, with strength fixed by the spontaneous magnetization. The dressed two-kink threshold curvature sets the relative kinetic scale. Together, these independently accessible quantities determine the scale-anomaly exponent and hence the meson hierarchy. In the supercritical regime, the same exponent governs geometric binding-energy and size ratios, logarithmic level accumulation, and log-periodic threshold scattering. Full-spin exact diagonalization and a fourth-order weak-field Hamiltonian support the coupled energy--size scaling in the finite window between core and ring effects. This provides a microscopic many-body realization of quantum limit-cycle physics, with its universal scaling fixed by magnetic order and kink mobility.

</details>

### [6] [Premonoidal Semantics and Scalable Diagrammatics of Fermionic Quantum Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02287)
*Thomas Perez,Titouan Carette*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives a categorical account of Local Fermionic Mode (LFM) quantum computation, showing that LFM processes form a symmetric premonoidal category whose centre is the even, parity-preserving subcategory, and develops "pronaps" (non-natural permutations) to organize a hierarchy of fermionic ZW calculus fragments with scalable diagrammatic notation and normal forms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LFM-based quantum computation has pure state spaces isomorphic to qubit state spaces via the Jordan-Wigner representation, but its compositional structure differs subtly: the algebraic formalism for embedding fermionic systems defines a parallel composition for which the usual interchange law of monoidal categories generally fails. The paper aims to explain and formalize this phenomenon categorically.

Method: Starting from the CAR-algebraic semantics of fermionic gate application, the authors show LFM processes form a symmetric premonoidal category whose centre is the even, parity-preserving subcategory. Alternatively, processes are organized with the ordinary tensor product and a fermionic presymmetry natural exactly on even maps. They introduce pronaps (a relaxation of props where permutations need not be natural) and extend scalable diagrammatic notation to them, defining syntactic sugars inspired by SZX and GSA, where matrix arrows encode minors and determinants and graph triangles encode pfaffians of principal submatrices.

Result: These constructions yield elegant normal forms and completeness proofs for presentations of the EoW, FoW, EW, and FW fragments. In particular, the FW presentation gives a new normal form for the matchgate fragment, distinct from the rWGS-X normal form of the planar-W (pW) presentation of the same category.

Conclusion: The resulting framework connects fermionic circuit semantics, diagrammatic rewriting with scalable notations, and the algebra of determinants and pfaffians.

Abstract: Local fermionic mode (LFM) based quantum computation has pure state spaces that are isomorphic, via the Jordan-Wigner representation, to qubit state spaces, but its compositional structure is subtly different. Indeed, the algebraic formalism specifying how to embed fermionic systems underlies a notion of parallel composition, for which, in general, the usual interchange law of monoidal categories fails. We give a categorical account of this phenomenon. Starting from the CAR-algebraic semantics of fermionic gate application, we show that LFM processes form a symmetric premonoidal category whose centre is precisely the even, parity-preserving subcategory. The same processes can alternatively be organized with the ordinary tensor product and a fermionic presymmetry that is natural exactly on even maps. To reason diagrammatically in this latter presentation, we introduce pronaps, a relaxation of the notion of prop, in which permutations are not-necessarily-natural, and use them to organize a hierarchy of fragments of the fermionic ZW calculus relevant to the study of fermionic circuits on physically motivated gate-sets. We then extend scalable diagrammatic notation to pronaps. In this setting, syntactic sugars inspired by SZX and GSA are defined, with the specificity that matrix arrows encode minors and determinants, while graph triangles encode pfaffians of principal submatrices. These constructions yield elegant normal forms and completeness proofs for our presentations of the EoW, FoW, EW, and FW fragments. In particular, the FW presentation gives a new normal form for the matchgate fragment, distinct from the rWGS-X normal form of the planar-W (pW) presentation of the same category. The resulting framework connects fermionic circuit semantics, diagrammatic rewriting with scalable notations, and the algebra of determinants and pfaffians.

</details>


### [7] [Determining the Number of Symmetry Sectors in Composite Quantum Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03108)
*Nisarg Vyas,S. Harshini Tekur,M. S. Santhanam*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work proposes a Best Subset Technique to determine the number of symmetry sectors in a composite quantum spectrum by comparing its spectral fluctuations to an appropriate random matrix ensemble, without requiring de-symmetrization; validated on Gaussian, Wishart, and Ginibre ensembles and many-body quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral fluctuations in complex quantum systems match random matrix theory only when the spectrum is pure; a composite spectrum mixing unequal symmetry sectors breaks this agreement, and prior detection methods required equal-dimension sectors and known symmetries.

Method: A Best Subset Technique is introduced that determines the number of symmetry sectors from a composite spectrum by comparing its spectral fluctuations to those of an appropriate random matrix ensemble, avoiding de-symmetrization. It applies to both Hermitian and non-Hermitian settings.

Result: The technique successfully recovers the number of symmetry sectors using spectra from Gaussian, Wishart, and Ginibre ensembles, as well as from many-body quantum systems.

Conclusion: The Best Subset Technique provides a general, de-symmetrization-free method to identify the number of unknown or partially known symmetry sectors in composite spectra, extending higher-order spacing ratio statistics to the unequal-dimension case.

Abstract: The spectral fluctuations in complex quantum systems are modeled in terms of spectra from random matrices. Quantitative agreement with random matrices holds only when the quantum spectrum is a pure sequence, and not a composite spectrum that mixes levels from different symmetry sectors. It was shown earlier that discrete symmetries in composite spectra can be detected using higher-order spacing ratio statistics provided the symmetry sectors are of equal dimensions. The general case is when the symmetry sectors have unequal dimensions, and the symmetries of the Hamiltonian system are unknown or only partially known. In this work, we address how the number of symmetry sectors can be determined from a composite spectrum arising in Hermitian and non-Hermitian settings. A Best Subset Technique is proposed for determining the number of the symmetry sectors by comparing the spectral fluctuations to that of an appropriate random matrix ensemble without requiring de-symmetrization. It is demonstrated using spectra from Gaussian, Wishart and Ginibre ensembles, and also for spectra drawn from many-body quantum systems.

</details>


### [8] [Homological Thresholds in Randomly Monitored Quantum Error-Correcting Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02310)
*Yoshito Watanabe,Daiki Sasamoto,Bo Han,Simon Trebst*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Stabilizer codes under a single round of random single-qubit Pauli measurements exhibit two distinct threshold universality classes: geometric percolation (toric code) and homological percolation (color code), distinguished via logical-support criteria and critical exponents from million-qubit simulations; the framework extends to non-CSS, subsystem, higher-dimensional, and Floquet codes and informs learning and maximum-likelihood decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random projective measurements effectively model monitoring, inferring, and decoding entangled states such as quantum error-correcting codes, while monitored dynamics has revived critical-phenomena questions in disordered statistical mechanics (percolation, random-bond models, Nishimori physics). The robustness and threshold behavior of stabilizer codes under such measurement dynamics, and whether distinct codes fall into separate universality classes, remained to be clarified.

Method: The authors analyze the logical-support criteria of stabilizer quantum error-correcting codes subject to one round of independently sampled single-qubit Pauli measurements. They perform optimized stabilizer simulations of large-scale systems exceeding one million qubits to numerically estimate critical exponents, and generalize the framework to non-CSS, subsystem, higher-dimensional, and Floquet stabilizer codes to derive threshold phase diagrams.

Result: Threshold behavior falls into two broad categories: geometric percolation and homological percolation transitions, with the toric code and color code as principal examples. The two threshold theories are shown to be distinct, established through logical-support criteria and numerical critical exponents. The framework yields threshold phase diagrams for the broader code families and provides quantitative and qualitative guidance for tunable learning and maximum-likelihood decoding, illustrated practically on the gross code.

Conclusion: Stabilizer code robustness under random Pauli measurements is governed by two distinct percolation-type threshold universality classes whose character is set by the codes' logical-support structure, providing a unified framework linking code thresholds to computationally demanding learning and decoding tasks.

Abstract: Random projective measurements are an effective tool to model the interlinked phenomenology of monitoring, inferring, and decoding highly entangled states such as quantum error-correcting codes. At the same time, the study of such monitored dynamics has opened a new arena for critical phenomena, renewing longstanding questions in disordered statistical mechanics, including percolation, random-bond models, and Nishimori physics. Here we investigate the robustness of stabilizer quantum error-correcting codes under a single round of independently sampled single-qubit Pauli measurements and demonstrate that their threshold behavior falls into two broad categories - geometric and homological percolation transitions, with the toric code and color code being the principal examples for each class. We establish the distinct character of these two threshold theories by analyzing the logical-support criteria of these codes and by providing numerical estimates of their respective critical exponents obtained from optimized stabilizer simulations of large-scale systems with over one million qubits. Our framework extends to non-CSS, subsystem, higher-dimensional, and Floquet stabilizer codes and allows us to determine their respective threshold phase diagrams. We exemplify how the latter provide quantitative and qualitative guidance for the computationally more demanding problems of tunable learning and maximum-likelihood decoding. As a practical application of our framework, we discuss the robustness of the gross code.

</details>


### [9] [Counterdiabatic Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02312)
*Ankit Wenju Shrestha,Budhaditya Bhattacharjee,Adolfo del Campo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a Hamiltonian-free theory of the adiabatic gauge potential (AGP) for parameter-dependent unitary operators, enabling shortcuts to adiabaticity and AGP-based chaos diagnostics for Floquet systems and quantum circuits where no effective Hamiltonian exists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard counterdiabatic driving offers a universal route to fast quantum control, but its conventional formulation requires a Hamiltonian generator. This assumption excludes Floquet systems and quantum circuits that are defined purely by parameter-dependent unitary operators, creating a need for an AGP framework that does not rely on a Hamiltonian.

Method: The authors construct a Hamiltonian-free formulation of the AGP for parameter-dependent unitary operators U_λ. Exact and regularized AGP variants are derived from a Hermitian, banded linear system, solved at a cost comparable to the Lanczos construction in the Hermitian case, and requiring neither diagonalization nor matrix logarithm. A truncated Krylov approximation is used and tested on the kicked top and an integrable brickwork circuit built from unitary R-check matrices.

Result: The truncated Krylov approximation shows rapid convergence for both the kicked top and the integrable brickwork circuit composed of unitary R-check matrices—a setting with no local effective Hamiltonian.

Conclusion: The work extends shortcuts to adiabaticity and AGP-based diagnostics of chaos to Floquet systems and quantum circuits, providing a Hamiltonian-free tool for fast quantum control and chaos detection in unitary-circuit settings.

Abstract: Counterdiabatic driving provides a universal route to fast quantum control, but its standard formulation presupposes a Hamiltonian generator. We develop a Hamiltonian-free theory of the adiabatic gauge potential (AGP) for parameter-dependent unitary operators $U_λ$. Exact and regularized versions of the AGP follow from a Hermitian, banded linear system at a cost comparable to the Lanczos construction in the Hermitian case, requiring neither diagonalization nor a matrix logarithm. We demonstrate rapid convergence of the truncated Krylov approximation for the kicked top and for an integrable brickwork circuit built from unitary $\check{R}$ matrices, a setting in which no local effective Hamiltonian is available. Our results extend shortcuts to adiabaticity, and AGP-based diagnostics of chaos, to Floquet systems and quantum circuits.

</details>


### [10] [Gap-Protected Heisenberg-Limited Squeezing with Locally Interacting Multi-Level Spins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02314)
*Sakshi Bahamnia,Sagar Chaudhary,Arman Duha,Thomas Bilitewski*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a general mechanism for generating scalable, metrologically useful entanglement (generalized spin squeezing) in multi-level (qudit) spin systems with power-law interactions, using local su(D) exchange interactions to open an energy gap that protects the collective manifold and achieves Heisenberg scaling in 1–3 dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin squeezing in multi-level (qudit) spin systems with power-law interactions is promising for metrology, but in the absence of all-to-all connectivity, the dynamics suffers from leakage into non-collective finite-momentum modes, which destroys Heisenberg scaling. A mechanism is needed to preserve collective behavior and scalable entanglement in realistic spatially extended lattice geometries.

Method: The authors study spin-changing dynamics generalizing spin-nematic squeezing from spinor BECs to spatially extended lattice geometries. They introduce local su(D) exchange interactions that establish an energy gap protecting the fully symmetric collective manifold and suppressing non-collective excitations. They provide an analytical understanding via analysis of the Bogoliubov excitation spectrum and verify effectiveness through numerical simulations of the quantum many-body dynamics.

Result: Local su(D) exchange interactions open an energy gap that suppresses leakage into non-collective finite-momentum modes, enabling Heisenberg scaling ξ² ∝ 1/N in one to three dimensions for any power-law exponent. The mechanism is confirmed analytically through the Bogoliubov excitation spectrum and numerically via quantum many-body simulations.

Conclusion: The findings more broadly apply to other spin-squeezing Hamiltonians and generalize to larger internal spin dimensions, providing a practical pathway towards scalable Heisenberg-limited qudit entanglement generation in experimental platforms such as arrays of (polar) molecules, magnetic atoms, and Rydberg atoms realizing multi-level spin systems.

Abstract: We demonstrate a general mechanism to generate scalable, metrologically useful entanglement in the form of generalized spin squeezing in multi-level (qudit) spin systems with power-law interactions. We demonstrate this for spin-changing dynamics, extending the physics of spin-nematic squeezing known from spinor BECs to spatially extended lattice geometries. In the absence of all-to-all connectivity, the dynamics generally suffers from leakage into non-collective finite-momentum modes, destroying Heisenberg scaling. We show that introducing local $\mathfrak{su}(D)$ exchange interactions establishes an energy gap that protects the fully symmetric collective manifold, suppressing non-collective excitations, and achieving Heisenberg scaling $ξ^2\propto1/N$ in one to three dimensions for any power-law exponent. We provide an analytical understanding of the mechanism via the analysis of the Bogoliubov excitation spectrum, and demonstrate its effectiveness via numerical simulations of the quantum many-body dynamics. Our findings more broadly apply to other spin-squeezing Hamiltonians, and directly generalize to larger internal spin dimensions. Our results provide a practical pathway towards scalable Heisenberg-limited qudit entanglement generation in experimental platforms such as arrays of (polar) molecules, magnetic atoms, and Rydberg atoms realizing multi-level spin systems.

</details>


### [11] [Halving the cost of QROM again via dense encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02321)
*Danial Motlagh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a dense encoding construction for QROM that stores two classical bits per dirty qubit using both Z and X bases, achieving up to ~4x Toffoli-count improvement over SelectSwap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QROM is a major source of non-Clifford (Toffoli) cost in fault-tolerant quantum algorithms. In qubit-constrained regimes, limited workspace restricts table-block size and table loading dominates cost. Prior dirty-qubit approaches (SelectSwap, sequential bit packets) reduce cost but store only one bit per dirty qubit, leaving room for further optimization.

Method: The authors develop a dense encoding construction of QROM that stores two bits of classical information per dirty qubit by exploiting both the Z and X bases, while ensuring unknown initial dirty-qubit states are restored. This construction is analyzed standalone and then combined with the sequential bit packet technique, with Toffoli counts derived for N entries of b bits and approximately bλ dirty qubits.

Result: Dense encoding alone reduces the leading Toffoli count from 2N/λ (SelectSwap) to N/λ, a 2x improvement. Combined with sequential bit packets, it achieves ½(1+1/b)N/λ, halving the sequential construction's leading cost and yielding ~4x improvement over SelectSwap in the large-N limit for practical b values.

Conclusion: Dense encoding is an effective technique for reducing QROM non-Clifford cost in qubit-constrained fault-tolerant settings, offering substantial Toffoli-count savings over SelectSwap and sequential bit packets, and is complementary to sequential bit packet methods.

Abstract: Quantum read only memory (QROM) is a substantial source of non-Clifford cost in fault-tolerant quantum algorithms. Borrowing dirty qubits can reduce this cost, provided their unknown initial states are restored. We consider the practically relevant qubit-constrained regime, where limited workspace restricts the table-block size and table loading dominates the cost. For $N$ entries of $b$ bits and approximately $bλ$ dirty qubits, SelectSwap has leading Toffoli count $2N/λ$. Recent work using sequential bit packets reduces this to $(1+1/b)N/λ$. In this work we introduce a dense encoding construction of QROM, where two bits of classical information are stored in each dirty qubit instead of one by utilizing both the $Z$ and $X$ bases. On its own, dense encoding provides a $2\times$ improvement over SelectSwap by reducing the leading Toffoli count to $N/λ$. Combining it with sequential bit packets gives $\tfrac12(1+1/b)N/λ$, a further halving of the sequential construction's leading cost, and an approximately $4\times$ improvement over SelectSwap in the large-$N$ limit for practical values of $b$.

</details>


### [12] [From Static Lindblad Stabilizability to Robust Physical Control Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02362)
*Yibin Wang,Ruifeng Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives checkable conditions for dissipative quantum stabilization under imperfect controls, with bounds on convergence, operational errors, and physical cost, and validates them via counterexamples and a finite-library search on a four-level cascade model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissipative dynamics can stabilize a quantum target without requiring a reliable sequence of finite control operations, but practical implementations face imperfect controls. The paper aims to establish verifiable conditions under which such stabilization succeeds despite implementation errors.

Method: Using a common Lyapunov measure of deviation from the target, the authors require a contraction bound uniformly below one over the prescribed uncertainty set after accounting for implementation errors. The analysis incorporates pulses, idle intervals, and all simultaneously acting background dynamics. Target-subspace preservation throughout each operation is imposed as a path condition, and a finite-library search is supported by the certification approach.

Result: Two single-qubit counterexamples show that a stabilizing average or a stabilizing generator obtained by unitary conjugation does not guarantee the required path conditions. For a specified four-level cascade model with finite pulses, calibration and timing errors, dephasing, and bounded pumping, the finite-library search yields a sequence with an explicit preparation-time bound, guaranteeing trace distance at most 0.1 from the target for every initial state at the end of preparation and throughout subsequent cycles.

Conclusion: Certified target-preserving paths give an explicit long-time error bound under bounded leakage, and the certification framework enables constructive finite-library searches for robust dissipative stabilization sequences under realistic imperfections.

Abstract: Dissipative dynamics can stabilize a quantum target without providing a reliable sequence of finite control operations. We derive checkable conditions for implementing such stabilization with imperfect controls, with bounds on convergence, errors during operations, and physical cost. The analysis includes pulses, idle intervals, and all simultaneously acting background dynamics. Using a common Lyapunov measure of deviation from the target, we require a contraction bound that remains uniformly below one over the prescribed uncertainty set after accounting for implementation errors. If all allowed paths preserve the target subspace throughout each operation, repeated cycles converge to that subspace from every initial state, even when errors vary between cycles. Comparison with these certified target-preserving paths gives an explicit long-time error bound under bounded leakage. Two single-qubit counterexamples show why a stabilizing average or a stabilizing generator obtained by unitary conjugation does not ensure these path conditions. The certification approach also supports a finite-library search. For a specified four-level cascade model with finite pulses, calibration and timing errors, dephasing, and bounded pumping, this search yields a sequence with an explicit preparation-time bound. For every initial state, it guarantees trace distance at most $0.1$ from the target at the end of preparation and throughout subsequent cycles.

</details>


### [13] [Singer-Difference-Set Qudit Stabilizer Codes from Non-Degenerate Quadrics in PG(d,q)PG(d,q): Construction, Structural Theorems, and Monte-Carlo Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02392)
*Michel Kulhandjian,Lajos Hanzo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Q(q,d), a family of q-ary non-CSS qudit stabilizer codes built from Singer difference sets and non-degenerate quadrics in PG(d,q), with parity check H = (A | M_Q A). Five structural theorems establish modular-constancy, CRT zero-count, a Clifford bridge to graph-state encodings, column-rigidity, and a distance lower bound. The flagship Q(p,2) = [[p^2+p+1, p(p+1)/2, d_min]]_p reaches asymptotic rate one-half, with a conjecture d_min = p+1 for odd primes proven at p=5 and lower-bounded to >=8 at p=7.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing qudit stabilizer code constructions, particularly CSS codes, are limited in achievable rate and structural flexibility. The paper aims to construct high-rate non-CSS qudit stabilizer codes with provable structural properties and strong distance behavior by exploiting Singer difference sets and quadrics in projective geometry.

Method: The construction couples the Singer-circulant incidence matrix A with a quadric circulant M_Q to form the parity check H = (A | M_Q A). A Cancellation Lemma uses the identity (k - lambda) = q^{d-1} = 0 mod p to make H symplectic-isotropic over F_q. Five structural theorems are proven: cross-correlation modular-constancy (nu = 1 mod q), a CRT zero-count identity, an explicit Clifford bridge identifying Q(q,2) as a graph-state encoding of the trivial Singer CSS code, paired column-rigidity (d_min = R_P + 1), and a lower bound d_min(Q(7,2)) >= 8 via exhaustive Singer-Frobenius search. A Hamada-Smith specialisation gives closed-form k(p,d).

Result: The flagship code Q(p,2) = [[p^2+p+1, p(p+1)/2, d_min]]_p attains asymptotic rate one-half. Conjecture d_min(Q(p,2)) = p+1 is proven at p=5 and lower-bounded to >=8 at p=7. Instances: Q(3,2) = [[13,6,3]]_3, Q(5,2) = [[31,15,6]]_5, Q(7,2) = [[57,28,d>=8]]_7, Q(3,4) = [[121,105,3]]_3. Monte-Carlo over 1.5x10^6 trials shows Q(5,2) achieves a 70-fold per-logical-qudit improvement over Steane [[7,1,3]]_2 at depolarising rate 10^-3 with zero silent logical errors.

Conclusion: Q(q,d) provides a structurally rich family of non-CSS qudit stabilizer codes with asymptotic rate one-half and strong distance properties. A textbook bounded-distance decoder is used, and Berlekamp-Massey-style cyclic decoding is proposed as the natural sequel.

Abstract: We propose Q(q,d), a family of q-ary non-CSS qudit stabilizer codes with prime power q and dimension d >= 2, constructed from Singer difference sets and non-degenerate quadrics in PG(d,q). The parity check H = (A | M_Q A) couples the Singer-circulant incidence matrix A with the quadric circulant M_Q, and a Cancellation Lemma uses the identity (k - lambda) = q^{d-1} = 0 mod p to make H symplectic-isotropic over F_q.
  Five structural theorems carry the novelty: a cross-correlation modular-constancy identity nu = 1 mod q at q odd, d >= 3, with a two-value pattern that proves a 2-torsion distance ceiling; a CRT zero-count identity; an explicit Clifford bridge identifying Q(q,2) as a graph-state encoding of the trivial Singer CSS code, with the 2021 low-density-spreading (LDS) sign-flip pattern realised by a symmetric circulant; a paired column-rigidity equality d_min = R_P + 1; and a lower bound d_min(Q(7,2)) >= 8 from an exhaustive Singer-Frobenius search.
  A Hamada-Smith specialisation yields the closed-form logical dimension k(p,d) = theta_d(p) - C(p+d-1, d) - 1, and the flagship Q(p,2) = [[p^2 + p + 1, p(p+1)/2, d_min]]_p attains asymptotic rate one-half. We conjecture d_min(Q(p,2)) = p + 1 for odd primes, proven at p = 5 and lower-bounded to >= 8 at p = 7. Instances include Q(3,2) = [[13,6,3]]_3, Q(5,2) = [[31,15,6]]_5, Q(7,2) = [[57,28, d>=8]]_7, and Q(3,4) = [[121,105,3]]_3. Monte-Carlo over 1.5 x 10^6 trials shows Q(5,2) achieves a 70-fold per-logical-qudit improvement over Steane [[7,1,3]]_2 at depolarising rate 10^-3 with zero silent logical errors. A textbook bounded-distance decoder is used; Berlekamp-Massey-style cyclic decoding is left as the natural sequel.

</details>


### [14] [Investigating entanglement dynamics with dynamical decoupling together with initial qubit-reservoir correlations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02453)
*Rifzah Shahid,Amna Qadeer,Ali Raza Mirza*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Exact entanglement dynamics of two qubits dephasing in a common bosonic bath is derived when qubits and bath are initially correlated; initial correlations accelerate entanglement sudden death (gap grows quadratically with coupling in weak-to-moderate regime), while periodic π-pulse sequences extend entanglement lifetime nearly sevenfold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most open-system treatments assume the qubits and reservoir start in a factorized (uncorrelated) state, but realistic experimental settings often involve pre-existing system-bath correlations. The authors ask how such initial correlations, established by prior joint equilibration, alter the entanglement dynamics of the two-qubit pair.

Method: The two qubits are coupled to a common bosonic reservoir via a pure dephasing interaction. Before preparing the qubit state, the system and reservoir are allowed to equilibrate into a joint state, and only then is the qubit pair projected onto the target state by a local measurement. For an Ohmic spectral density, the exact reduced two-qubit dynamics is obtained, and entanglement is quantified using the Wootters concurrence. The effect of a symmetric periodic π-pulse sequence on both qubits is also examined.

Result: Initial correlations always advance entanglement sudden death compared with the factorized case, and this gap grows quadratically with coupling strength in the weak-to-moderate coupling regime. A symmetric sequence of periodic π pulses applied to both qubits extends the entanglement lifetime by close to a factor of seven over the studied range of pulse numbers.

Conclusion: Pre-existing qubit-reservoir correlations are not benign: they systematically shorten entanglement lifetime by hastening sudden death, with a coupling-dependent penalty. Applying appropriate periodic π-pulse sequences offers a practical route to substantially prolong entanglement, extending its lifetime by nearly a factor of seven.

Abstract: We study the exact entanglement dynamics of two qubits coupled to a common bosonic reservoir through a pure dephasing interaction. We ask how this dynamics changes when the qubits and the reservoir already share correlations before the qubit state is prepared. In that case the system and reservoir first interact for a long time and settle into a joint equilibrium state. Only afterward is the qubit pair projected onto the desired state by a local measurement. For an Ohmic spectral density we obtain the exact reduced two qubit dynamics. Using the Wootters concurrence we show that initial correlations always speed up entanglement sudden death relative to the factorized case, and that this gap grows quadratically with the coupling strength in the weak-to-moderate coupling regime. Finally we show that a symmetric sequence of periodic π pulses applied to both qubits extends the entanglement lifetime close to a factor of seven over the range of pulse numbers studied.

</details>


### [15] [Robust constrained optimization of nonequilibrium Casimir repulsion in a biased-semiconductor cavity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02471)
*Mohamed R. Khodja,Belkacem E. Khodja*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper formulates robust constrained optimization of repulsive nonequilibrium Casimir–Lifshitz forces in a PEC–vacuum–biased-GaAs–vacuum–PEC stack, maximizing the smaller outward pressure under fabrication and drive uncertainties, and derives a design bound at 300 K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonequilibrium electromagnetic fluctuations can produce repulsive Casimir–Lifshitz forces, but designs optimized only at nominal conditions may be fragile to fabrication and drive uncertainties. There is a need for a robust design framework that guarantees repulsion under realistic perturbations.

Method: Repulsion in a planar PEC–vacuum–biased-GaAs–vacuum–PEC stack is cast as a robust constrained optimization problem in which the smaller of the outward pressures on the two conductors is maximized subject to fabrication and drive uncertainties. In the optically thick-slab limit, a symmetry argument proves that identical boundaries admit a symmetric optimizer, yielding equality of optimal gaps as a model outcome. Results are validated against a full finite-slab scattering calculation.

Result: At 300 K, with gap uncertainty of ±10 nm and drive uncertainty of ±0.005, the optimized design sustains a worst-case outward pressure of 2.257 mPa and exceeds the corresponding far-field radiation-pressure plateau by 0.1812 mPa. The full finite-slab scattering calculation agrees with the reduced model to within 1.60×10⁻³ % at both the nominal and active worst-case points.

Conclusion: The work provides a design bound for cavity-enhanced nonequilibrium Casimir–Lifshitz repulsion and demonstrates that robust optimization can distinguish genuine cavity enhancement from ordinary far-field radiation pressure.

Abstract: Nonequilibrium electromagnetic fluctuations offer a route to repulsive Casimir--Lifshitz forces. However, designs optimized only at nominal conditions may be fragile. We formulate repulsion in a planar PEC--vacuum--biased-GaAs--vacuum--PEC stack as a robust constrained optimization problem. The smaller of the outward pressures on the two conductors is maximized under fabrication and drive uncertainties. In the optically thick-slab limit, we prove that identical boundaries admit a symmetric optimizer, so equality of the optimal gaps emerges from the model rather than being imposed. At $300~\mathrm{K}$, with gap uncertainty of $\pm10~\mathrm{nm}$ and drive uncertainty of $\pm0.005$, the optimized design sustains a worst-case outward pressure of $2.257~\mathrm{mPa}$ and exceeds the corresponding far-field radiation-pressure plateau by $0.1812~\mathrm{mPa}$. At the nominal and active worst-case points, a full finite-slab scattering calculation agrees with the reduced model to within $1.60\times10^{-3}\%$. These results provide a design bound for cavity-enhanced nonequilibrium Casimir--Lifshitz repulsion and show how robust optimization can distinguish cavity enhancement from ordinary far-field radiation pressure.

</details>


### [16] [Single-photon addition to multimode quantum fields at telecom wavelengths using lithium niobate and its doped variants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02473)
*Naveen V,Nicolas Treps,Raghavan G,Valentina Parigi,Srinivasan K*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper theoretically analyzes single-photon addition (SPA) and mode-selective SPA (MSSPA) to multimode quantum fields at telecom wavelengths for quantum information processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimode spectro-temporal non-Gaussian quantum states of light are crucial for continuous-variable quantum information processing, and non-Gaussian states generated via SPA to multimode quantum fields at telecommunication wavelengths are a useful resource for quantum technologies.

Method: The authors conduct a detailed theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of SPA and MSSPA to a multimode quantum field at telecom wavelengths. They examine congruently grown lithium niobate (LN), 5% MgO-doped LN (MgO:LN), and various ZnO-doped LN (ZnO:LN) bulk crystals. MSSPA is investigated using type-II parametric down-conversion (PDC), and SPA using type-I PDC. They also present spectro-temporal mode-selective SPA at telecom wavelength using 5% MgO:LN for temperatures from 0°C to 15°C, and simulate SPA based on a noncollinear type-I PDC configuration.

Result: The theoretical results identify optimal experimental conditions and phase-matching parameters to achieve SPA and mode-selective SPA for LN-based crystals.

Conclusion: The work provides a potential platform for non-Gaussian quantum state engineering at telecommunication wavelengths, mainly at 1550 nm.

Abstract: Multimode spectro-temporal non-Gaussian quantum states of light play a crucial role in continuous-variable quantum information processing. In particular, the non-Gaussian quantum states generated through a single-photon addition (SPA) to multimode quantum fields at telecommunication wavelengths constitute a useful resource for quantum technologies. We present a detailed theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of single-photon addition (SPA) and mode-selective single-photon addition (MSSPA) to a multimode quantum field at telecommunication wavelengths. The analysis is performed for congruently grown lithium niobate (LN), $5\%$ MgO-doped lithium niobate (MgO:LN) and various concentrations of ZnO-doped lithium niobate (ZnO:LN) bulk crystals. We investigate MSSPA using type-II parametric down-conversion (PDC) and SPA using the type-I PDC processes. We further present spectro-temporal mode-selective SPA at the telecom wavelength using $5\%$ MgO:LN for temperatures ranging from $0^\circ C$ to $15^\circ C$. In addition, we perform simulations to investigate SPA based on the noncollinear type-I PDC configuration. These theoretical results identify optimal experimental conditions and phase-matching parameters to achieve SPA and mode-selective SPA for LN-based crystals, providing a potential platform for non-Gaussian quantum state engineering at telecommunication wavelengths, mainly at $1550$ nm.

</details>


### [17] [The complexity of entangled graph colouring via polymorphisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02565)
*Eric Culf*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work generalizes polymorphism-based CSP reductions to entangled CSPs via an entangled analogue of polymorphisms, yielding gap-preserving reductions that prove undecidability of entangled graph colouring with more than three colours.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CSPs with operator assignments give a structured framework for studying the decision complexity of the entangled value of nonlocal games. Classical CSP complexity is well understood through polymorphisms (symmetries of the underlying relational structure) via the CSP dichotomy theorem. The goal is to extend this algebraic, polymorphism-based understanding to the entangled setting, particularly to attack problems resistant to existing hardness techniques.

Method: The authors show that polymorphism-based reductions between CSPs can be generalized to gap-preserving reductions between entangled CSPs, relying on an entangled analogue of polymorphisms.

Result: Using the generalized gap-preserving reductions, the paper establishes the undecidability of entangled graph colouring with more than three colours — a problem that had resisted prior hardness reductions based on commutativity gadgets.

Conclusion: An entangled analogue of polymorphisms enables gap-preserving reductions between entangled CSPs, providing a new route to hardness results in the entangled setting and resolving a previously intractable case.

Abstract: Constraint satisfaction problems (CSPs) with operator assignments to the variables provide a well-structured setting to study the decision complexity of the entangled value of classes of nonlocal games. Due to the CSP dichotomy theorem, the complexity of constraint satisfaction problems with classical assignments can be fully understood by studying the symmetries of the CSP, in terms of the polymorphisms of the underlying relational structure. In this work, we show that the polymorphism-based reductions between CSPs can be generalised to gap-preserving reductions between entangled CSPs based on an entangled analogue of the polymorphisms. This reduction allows us to show undecidability of entangled graph colouring with more than three colours, a problem that has proved resistant to prior hardness reductions based on commutativity gadgets.

</details>


### [18] [High-Rate Quantum Codes with Proven Distance and Low-Weight Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02621)
*Kishor Bharti,Tobias Haug,Runzhou Tao,Kevin Ye*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper defines a family of quantum subsystem codes on even-sided rectangular coordinate grids in any dimension, derives logical qubit counts and proves dressed distances analytically (no numerical search), then maps the rate–measurement-weight tradeoff under a 10,000-data-qubit budget at distances 16, 32, and 64, with Lean formal verification snippets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subsystem codes with directly measurable coordinate-line operators are attractive for practical quantum hardware, but their parameters (logical qubits, dressed distance) and the rate–measurement-weight tradeoff are typically hard to characterize. The authors aim to provide an analytically tractable family with provable parameters and formally verified properties.

Method: Constructs subsystem codes on rectangular coordinate grids of arbitrary dimension with even side lengths ≥4; directly measured operators act along coordinate lines. For each family member, the number of protected logical qubits is derived and the dressed distance is proven analytically rather than by numerical search. The rate–weight tradeoff is evaluated under a 10,000 data-qubit budget at distances 16, 32, and 64, and Lean code snippets formally verify the construction and the bare/dressed distances.

Result: Under a 10,000-data-qubit budget: at distance 16, one code protects 4,096 logical qubits among 10,000 data qubits using weight-ten measurements; at distance 32, 7,776 data qubits protect 1,024 logical qubits with weight-six measurements; at distance 64, 9,216 data qubits protect 256 logical qubits with weight-six measurements. The complete encoding-rate vs. measurement-weight tradeoff within the family is determined for these distances.

Conclusion: The proposed grid-based subsystem-code family admits analytic determination of logical qubits and dressed distance, enabling systematic design-space exploration. Lean snippets make the construction and distance proofs formally verifiable, supporting reproducible code design.

Abstract: We study a family of quantum subsystem codes defined on rectangular coordinate grids of any dimension, with even side lengths of at least four. The directly measured operators act along coordinate lines. For every member of the family, we derive the number of protected logical qubits and prove its dressed distance without a numerical search. To illustrate the resulting design choices, we impose a budget of at most 10,000 data qubits and determine the complete tradeoff between encoding rate and measurement weight within this family at distances 16, 32, and 64. At distance 16, one code protects 4,096 logical qubits among 10,000 data qubits using weight-ten measurements. At distances 32 and 64, codes with 7,776 and 9,216 data qubits protect 1,024 and 256 logical qubits, respectively, using weight-six measurements. We include and explain Lean code snippets throughout the paper to guide readers through the formal verification of our subsystem-code construction and its dressed and bare distances.

</details>


### [19] [Gaussian Fisher Information Is Superadditive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02625)
*Jiaxin Liu,Zuoxian Wang,Danyue Ma*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Quantum Fisher information is additive over independent probes, so optimal single-parameter estimation is usually done probe-by-probe. This paper shows Gaussian measurements violate that rule: jointly measuring two independent modes with Bell homodyne outperforms the best separate readout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard rule from quantum Fisher information states that information adds over independent probes, implying separate (probe-by-probe) measurement is optimal. The authors ask whether this rule holds for Gaussian measurements and whether jointly detecting two independent modes can beat separate readout.

Method: The authors analyze Gaussian measurements, specifically Bell homodyne (a balanced beam splitter followed by two homodyne detectors), in contrast to heterodyne detection. They prove bounds on the achievable gain for parameters encoded in quadrature widths and study thermal modes at one temperature.

Result: Bell homodyne beats the best separate readout when the two modes differ in quadrature width and in the width's response to the parameter. The gain stays below (√2−1)² = 17.157% for parameters carried by widths. For thermal modes at one temperature, any frequency difference opens a temperature window with gain peaking at 12.699% at frequency ratio 3.318, where Bell homodyne is the optimal Gaussian measurement.

Conclusion: Gaussian measurements break the additivity-based optimality of separate probing. Bell homodyne converts the vacuum noise penalty of heterodyne into signal by filling the empty port with the second mode. The predicted gain is reachable with standard hardware: two coupled resonators with two homodyne detectors, or a phase-preserving amplifier whose idler band is fed by the same thermal source.

Abstract: Quantum Fisher information adds over independent probes, so a single parameter is best measured probe by probe. We show that Gaussian measurements, the linear optics and homodyne detection of optical and microwave experiments, break this rule: two independent modes are better measured together. The uncertainty principle leaves a linear detector half of phase space, and for two modes the choice of half is a resource. Bell homodyne, a balanced beam splitter followed by two homodyne detectors, beats the best separate readout when the modes differ in quadrature width and in how the width responds to the parameter. Heterodyne detection splits a mode on a beam splitter to read both quadratures and pays one unit of vacuum noise for the empty port. Bell homodyne fills that port with the second mode, so the noise turns into signal. We prove that the gain stays below $(\sqrt2-1)^2=17.157\%$ for every parameter carried by widths. For thermal modes at one temperature, the canonical case, a gain was conjectured impossible. We prove that any frequency difference opens a temperature window and that the gain peaks at $12.699\%$ at frequency ratio $3.318$, where Bell homodyne is the optimal Gaussian measurement. Standard hardware reaches the gain: two coupled resonators with two homodyne detectors, or a phase-preserving amplifier whose idler band is fed by the same thermal source.

</details>


### [20] [Where Quantum Fourier Sampling Stops Short: A Three-Gate Audit Protocol for Delay-PUF Security Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02636)
*Owen Friedewald,Ali Shiri Sichani,Chi-Ren Shyu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A Three-Gate Quantum Audit Protocol evaluates whether quantum Fourier sampling yields realizable advantage for delay-based PUFs, testing structure, algorithmics, and implementation; no end-to-end advantage is found in the evaluated regime despite an ideal sampling call-count benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Claims that quantum Fourier sampling aids auditing the spectral learnability of delay-based PUFs need scrutiny under strong classical comparators (access matching) and oracle synthesis.

Method: Three gates: (1) Structure, analyzing degree versus support at reachable challenge lengths for 4-XOR; (2) Algorithmics, comparing against Kushilevitz–Mansour membership-query baseline over 45 tasks, plus a quantum-kernel diagnostic (geometric difference vs. 1/sqrt(lambda_min(K_C)) and a balance-preserving permutation null); (3) Implementation, a simulator-validated fixed-point QFT-based phase oracle with routed-depth reduction, timed against the mapped qubits' median dephasing time T2 on a static backend snapshot.

Result: Low degree does not imply small support (at n=14, 4-XOR degree <= d_f(0.1) admits 91% of characters; median 90%-mass set spans a third of the spectrum). Kushilevitz–Mansour exhausts each finite domain across 45 tasks and no 4-XOR ideal-sampling case reaches 90% mass within 2^n calls. The quantum-kernel diagnostic shows geometric difference up to 2.151 at N=512, but correlates 0.991 with the classical Gram-matrix ill-conditioning term and shows no significant label alignment (p=0.930). The phase oracle achieves 18.9% routed-depth reduction, yet least certified precisions take 1.18–1.55x the median T2, without hardware execution.

Conclusion: No end-to-end advantage in the evaluated regime, though ideal sampling uses fewer coherent calls on the thresholded task. The paper contributes the Three-Gate Quantum Audit Protocol for separating ideal query advantage from realizable security benefit; it is neither a claim about deployed silicon nor an impossibility result.

Abstract: Quantum Fourier sampling may help audit the spectral learnability of delay-based physical unclonable functions (PUFs). We ask whether that promise survives access matching, a strong classical comparator, and oracle synthesis. Three gates structure the evaluation. Structure: low degree is not small support at reachable challenge lengths; for 4-XOR at $n=14$, degree $\le d_f(0.1)$ admits $91\%$ of all $2^n$ characters and the median $90\%$-mass set spans a third of the spectrum. Algorithmics: constructing the phase oracle logically implies classical membership access, making Kushilevitz--Mansour the correct baseline; across 45 tasks it exhausts each finite domain, and no 4-XOR ideal-sampling case reaches $90\%$ mass within $2^n$ calls. A quantum-kernel diagnostic appears more favorable, with geometric difference rising to $2.151$ at $N=512$ challenges, but it correlates $0.991$ with $1/\sqrt{λ_{\min}(K_C)}$ for the classical Gram matrix $K_C$, and the 4-XOR label-complexity ratio does not exceed a balance-preserving permutation null ($p=0.930$). Trace-normalized geometric difference can therefore grow through classical ill-conditioning alone, without task-label alignment. Implementation: a simulator-validated fixed-point phase oracle based on the quantum Fourier transform admits an $18.9\%$ routed-depth reduction, yet the least certified precisions have estimated durations of $1.18$--$1.55\times$ the median dephasing time $T_2$ of the mapped qubits on a static backend snapshot, without hardware execution. We find no end-to-end advantage in the evaluated regime, although ideal sampling does use fewer coherent calls on the thresholded task. The contribution is the Three-Gate Quantum Audit Protocol: a reproducible procedure separating an ideal query advantage from a realizable security benefit. This is not a claim about deployed silicon and not an impossibility result.

</details>


### [21] [When Normalization Selects the Sign: Auditing Robustness Ablations in Quantum Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02641)
*Owen Friedewald,Srikar Alla,Ali Shiri Sichani,Chi-Ren Shyu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Removing an input-scaling module from a quantum-attention detector confounds two effects—changes to the classifier and changes to the perturbations reaching its encoder—so apparent robustness differences may reflect the comparison rule rather than the module; the reported benefit reverses under an alternative budget and does not survive causal checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ablating an input-scaling module alters both the classifier and the perturbations that reach its encoder. Consequently, any measured robustness difference conflates the effect of the module with the effect of the comparison rule (which budget is held fixed), so the paper aims to show that such comparisons do not by themselves establish a robustness benefit caused by the module.

Method: Ablation study on a four-qubit quantum-attention detector trained on generated power-grid trajectories. The same comparison is run under two rules: a fixed physical attack budget at the input, and a matched upper bound on perturbations at the encoder. Label preservation of attacks and prediction preservation of interventions are checked; the input scales of a trained model are replaced and only the linear classification layer is retrained. Two further design checks examine why the input quantum Fisher information regularizer cannot train the model's query parameters, and why removing confidence bounds fails to establish a larger certified radius.

Result: At a fixed physical attack budget the learned scaling module appears beneficial, but matching the perturbation upper bound at the encoder reverses the ordering, so neither comparison alone establishes a module-caused robustness benefit. Tests that perturb clean examples into attacked examples while retaining original labels are inconclusive, and tests restricted to already-attacked examples do not establish a benefit. Replacing the trained model's input scales disrupts detection; retraining the linear layer restores the detection rate but changes individual predictions, making the comparison descriptive rather than causal. Evidence is limited to ten seeds, exact simulation, synthetic data, and a restricted attack set; classical baselines achieve better clean prediction.

Conclusion: Robustness claims from module ablations are only meaningful when the fixed perturbation budget is explicitly specified. Valid experiments must confirm that attacks preserve labels and interventions preserve predictions, and must separate exploratory controls from confirmatory evidence; otherwise the comparison is descriptive rather than causal and does not demonstrate a robustness benefit attributable to the module.

Abstract: Removing an input-scaling module changes both a classifier and the perturbations reaching its encoder. A robustness difference can therefore reflect the comparison rule as well as the module. We demonstrate this problem in a four-qubit quantum-attention detector on generated power-grid trajectories. A learned scaling module appears beneficial at a fixed physical attack budget, but matching an upper bound on perturbations at the encoder reverses the ordering. Neither comparison alone establishes a robustness benefit caused by the module. The initial test also perturbs clean examples into attacked examples while retaining their original labels; tests restricted to already attacked examples do not establish a benefit. Replacing a trained model's input scales disrupts detection. Retraining its linear classification layer restores the detection rate, but changes individual predictions, leaving the comparison descriptive rather than causal. Two further design checks explain why the input quantum Fisher information regularizer cannot train this model's query parameters, and why removing confidence bounds does not establish a larger certified radius. The evidence is limited to ten seeds, exact simulation, synthetic data, and a restricted set of attacks; classical baselines achieve better clean prediction. The practical lesson is to specify which perturbation budget is fixed, check that attacks preserve labels and interventions preserve predictions, and distinguish exploratory controls from confirmatory evidence.

</details>


### [22] [Optimal state detection without likelihood estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02644)
*M. D. K. Lee,Z. Chai,J. Z. F. Seng,K. J. Arnold,M. ~D. ~Barrett*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Adaptive Bayesian fluorescence state detection is reduced to comparing elapsed time against two arithmetic time sequences fixed by bright/dark count rates and confidence parameters; the resulting "sequential deadline detection" scheme requires no real-time probability computation and is provably optimal in mean detection time, with closed-form expressions for detection-time distributions, mean times, channel error rates, and robustness to miscalibration, decay, and afterpulsing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional adaptive Bayesian estimation for fluorescence state detection typically demands real-time probability computation, which is computationally burdensome. The paper aims to simplify this to an efficient, provably optimal procedure without runtime probability evaluation.

Method: The authors show that adaptive Bayesian estimation reduces to comparing elapsed time with two arithmetic time sequences determined by bright and dark count rates and confidence parameters ε_d and ε_b. This yields "sequential deadline detection," for which they derive closed-form expressions for detection-time distributions, mean detection times, and error rates of both channels, accounting for miscalibrated rates, inter-state decay during detection, and afterpulsing in single-photon counting modules. Predictions are validated by Monte Carlo simulation and demonstrated with Ba+ fluorescence detection.

Result: The derived scheme is provably optimal in mean detection time. Closed-form expressions accurately capture detection-time distributions, mean detection times, and error rates, including effects of miscalibrated rates, decay between states, and afterpulsing. Monte Carlo simulation confirms the predictions, and Ba+ fluorescence detection provides experimental demonstration.

Conclusion: Sequential deadline detection offers a computationally trivial, provably mean-time-optimal approach to adaptive Bayesian fluorescence state detection, with tractable analytic characterization of performance and robustness, validated by simulation and Ba+ experiments.

Abstract: We show that adaptive Bayesian estimation in fluorescence state detection reduces to a comparison of elapsed time with two arithmetic time sequences fixed by the bright and dark count rates and confidence parameters $ε_d$ and $ε_b$. No probabilities are computed in real time and the resulting implementation, which we term sequential deadline detection, is provably optimal in mean detection time. The analysis permits closed-form expressions for detection-time distributions, mean detection times, error rates of both channels accounting for miscalibrated rates, decay between the states during detection, and afterpulsing in single photon counting modules. The predictions are validated by Monte Carlo simulation and demonstrated by fluorescence detection with Ba$^+$

</details>


### [23] [Correlated memory effect of environment in radical-pair magnetoreception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02712)
*Shixuan Chen,Yao Wang,Rui-Xue Xu,YiJing Yan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies the radical-pair magnetoreception model by simultaneously treating the spin bath and boson bath, using exact HEOM/DEOM to capture non-Markovian memory effects, and shows these effects are essential for the magnetoreception yield.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The photoreceptor compass relies on spin-correlated radical pairs whose singlet-triplet interconversion is driven by Zeeman and anisotropic hyperfine interactions. Prior treatments typically neglect or oversimplify the surrounding spin environment, so the role of the spin bath's correlated memory effect on the radical-pair dynamics and yield remains unclear.

Method: The spin environment and the boson environment surrounding the radical pair are considered simultaneously. In the linear-response limit, the spin bath is mapped to an effective Gaussian environment. The combined system dynamics and yields are propagated uniformly and exactly via the hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM) or equivalently the dissipaton equations of motion (DEOM), which are non-Markovian and non-perturbative and exact for Gaussian environments. Markovian Lindblad master equation results are also presented for comparison.

Result: Numerical demonstrations show the non-Markovian memory effect of the spin bath, acting alongside the bosonic bath, plays a crucial role in shaping the radical-pair reaction yields and the resulting orientation-dependent magnetoreception response.

Conclusion: The non-Markovian memory effect is identified as a crucial ingredient for radical-pair magnetoreception, and exact HEOM/DEOM treatment of the combined spin and boson environments provides a reliable framework for its description.

Abstract: In the photoreceptor compass model, a light-generated, spin-correlated radical pair undergoes singlet-triplet interconversion driven by Zeeman and anisotropic hyperfine interactions, leading to orientation-dependent reaction yields. In this study, we consider simultaneously the spin environment and the boson environment surrounding the radical-pair system and concentrate on the correlated memory effect of the spin bath. In the linear-response limit, the spin bath is mapped to an effective Gaussian environment. The system dynamics and yields under its influence together with the bosonic bath are propagated uniformly using the hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM) or equivalently the dissipaton equations of motion (DEOM) method. The HEOM/DEOM is non-Markovian and non-perturbative, exact for Gaussian environments. Corresponding results of the Markovian Lindblad master equation are also shown for comparison. Numerical demonstrations highlight the non-Markovian memory effect as a crucial ingredient for radical-pair magnetoreception.

</details>


### [24] [SMP: A General Hyperedge-Based Framework for Circuit-Level Quantum Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02734)
*Siying Wang,Yue Yan,Zhixin Xia,Canwei Shi,Hannuo Yuan,Xiang-Bin Wang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Syndrome Motif Projection (SMP), a lightweight framework that injects hyperedge fault information into fast matching-based decoders via linear-complexity syndrome preprocessing, improving logical failure rates across surface codes, color codes, and gate circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperedge faults are a central obstacle in circuit-level quantum error correction. Exploiting hyperedge correlations typically demands heavy computation, hindering real-time decoding. Matching decoders are fast and scalable but rely on pairwise graph representations that discard higher-order fault information.

Method: SMP exploits the observation that a hyperedge fault generates a characteristic local syndrome motif; detecting this motif yields evidence for the corresponding fault. SMP extracts this evidence directly from the measured syndrome using linear-complexity preprocessing, while leaving the matching backend unchanged, acting as a lightweight front end for existing scalable, real-time matching decoders.

Result: On Google's Willow surface code data, SMP improves both standard and correlated matching and outperforms the tested belief-matching baseline. For color code memories, SMP with Chromobius cuts logical failure probability by up to 77.8%. For gate circuits, SMP reduces six-CNOT circuit failure probability by 35.5% versus logical observable matching, matching iterative belief matching.

Conclusion: SMP offers a lightweight, transferable approach to hyperedge decoding with a clear path toward integration into scalable real-time decoding architectures for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

Abstract: Hyperedge faults remain a major challenge in circuit-level quantum error correction. Accurately exploiting hyperedge correlations often requires substantial computational resources, which complicates real-time decoding. Matching-based decoders are fast and scalable, but their pairwise graph representation limits the use of higher-order fault information. Here, we introduce Syndrome Motif Projection (SMP), a general framework for incorporating hyperedge information into matching-based decoding. The key idea is that a hyperedge fault produces a characteristic local syndrome motif. Observing such a motif provides evidence for the corresponding fault. SMP extracts this information directly from the measured syndrome with linear-complexity preprocessing while preserving the matching backend. It can therefore serve as a lightweight front end to existing scalable and real-time matching decoders. We demonstrate SMP across surface code memories, color code memories, and logical gate circuits. On Google's Willow experimental surface code data, SMP improves both standard and correlated matching and outperforms the tested belief matching baseline. For color code memories, SMP combined with Chromobius reduces logical failure probabilities by up to 77.8\%. For logical gate circuits, SMP reduces the failure probability of a six-CNOT circuit by 35.5\% relative to logical observable matching while achieving performance comparable to iterative belief matching. These results demonstrate that SMP provides a lightweight and transferable approach to hyperedge decoding, with a clear path toward integration into scalable real-time decoding architectures for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

</details>


### [25] [Design Automation for Gray-Code Quantum Read-Only Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02757)
*Hao-Yu Lu,Yu-Ting Kao,Yeong-Jar Chang,Chao-Hung Wang,Darsen D. Lu*

Main category: quant-ph
