TL;DR: The paper proposes GQROM, a programmable quantum read-only memory based on Gray code encoding that efficiently loads classical data into quantum circuits, reducing initialization overhead and gate-induced errors, with a full EDA flow validated on Qiskit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Near-term quantum computing faces critical bottlenecks in loading classical data into quantum circuits, including high initialization overhead and gate-induced errors. Efficient state preparation is a key challenge that limits practical scalability.

Method: The authors design a programmable quantum read-only memory (GQROM) using Gray code encoding, where consecutive addresses differ by only a single bit, thereby minimizing state transition complexity. The architecture supports targeted data access through initial-state modification. A systematic EDA flow was developed, and the entire design was implemented and validated on the Qiskit platform.

Result: GQROM reduces initialization overhead and gate-induced errors. The architecture demonstrates flexibility in accessing targeted data via initial-state modification. The full design, including the EDA flow, was implemented and validated on Qiskit, confirming practical feasibility.

Conclusion: GQROM shows potential as a scalable and hardware-friendly solution for efficient state preparation in quantum circuits.

Abstract: We propose a programmable quantum read-only memory based on Gray code encoding, termed GQROM, designed to efficiently load classical data into quantum circuits. This architecture mitigates critical bottlenecks in near-term quantum computing by reducing initialization overhead and gate-induced errors. By utilizing Gray code, in which consecutive addresses differ by only a single bit, GQROM minimizes state transition complexity. We demonstrate the architecture's flexibility in accessing targeted data through initial-state modification. The entire design, including a systematic EDA flow, was implemented and validated on the Qiskit platform, confirming its practical feasibility. These results underscore GQROM's potential as a scalable and hardware-friendly solution for efficient state preparation in quantum circuits.

</details>


### [26] [Quantum Error-Corrected Memories Keep Proper Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02786)
*Sebastian P. Kish,Simon J. Devitt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Error correction does not erase relativistic time dilation; when two error-corrected quantum memories follow distinct worldlines, their local correction histories factor exactly from the proper-time difference, which reappears as a relational phase rotation—invisible to local error syndromes but manifesting as a deterministic logical gate at discrete code-admitted phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper explores the intersection of fault-tolerant quantum information and relativistic clocks, asking whether quantum error correction erases or preserves proper-time (time-dilation) information. The motivation is to establish protected quantum memories as a platform for preserving proper-time information and for long-coherence tests of quantum evolution involving relativistic proper time.

Method: The authors analyze two error-corrected quantum memories that follow distinct worldlines and are controlled by local references. They examine how the local correction histories factor relative to the proper-time difference between the memories, and study the resulting relational evolution and its relation to local error syndromes.

Result: The local correction histories factor exactly from the proper-time difference between the memories. The accumulated difference appears as a relational phase rotation when the memories are compared. This rotation is invisible to every local error syndrome during locally referenced operation, but at discrete phases admitted by a code, the final relational evolution returns exactly as a deterministic logical gate—including an S-type Clifford gate at a π/2 excursion.

Conclusion: Error correction does not erase time dilation. The results connect fault-tolerant quantum information with relativistic clocks, establishing protected quantum memories as a platform for preserving proper-time information and for long-coherence tests of quantum evolution involving relativistic proper time.

Abstract: What does quantum error correction do to relativistic proper time? We show that when two error-corrected quantum memories follow distinct worldlines and are controlled by local references, their local correction histories factor exactly from the proper-time difference between them. Error correction therefore does not erase time dilation: when the memories are compared, the accumulated difference appears as a relational phase rotation. During locally referenced operation this rotation is invisible to every local error syndrome; at discrete phases admitted by a code, the final relational evolution instead returns exactly as a deterministic logical gate, including an $S$-type Clifford gate at a $π/2$ excursion. These results connect fault-tolerant quantum information with relativistic clocks and establish protected quantum memories as a platform for preserving proper-time information and for long-coherence tests of quantum evolution involving relativistic proper time.

</details>


### [27] [Thermalization in a repeated-interaction model with memory effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02854)
*Kanji Nishii,Ryo Nagai,Yoshihiko Abe*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Memory effects in a repeated-interaction qubit model lead to non-monotonic thermalization and a Mpemba-like reversal in collision count versus target temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how a memory qubit, which persists and interacts repeatedly with the system, influences the thermalization dynamics of that system—an aspect largely absent in standard memoryless collision models.

Method: A repeated-interaction model is constructed in which a memory qubit repeatedly interacts with the system qubit, both undergoing collisions with fresh thermal ancillas. Exact closed evolution equations for populations and mutual coherence are derived, and memory effects are demonstrated via a causal-break operation.

Result: The system converges to the target thermal state, but the approach is non-monotonic. A Mpemba-like reversal is found where the number of collisions needed for thermalization depends non-monotonically on target temperature. Both effects require initial system-memory coherence or an imbalance in excited-state populations within the considered initial-state family.

Conclusion: Memory can qualitatively reshape thermalization, producing non-monotonic convergence and Mpemba-like behavior governed by initial coherence and population imbalance requirements.

Abstract: We study how memory affects the thermalization of a qubit in a repeated-interaction model. In our model, a memory qubit interacts repeatedly with the system, while both qubits undergo collisions with fresh thermal ancillas. We derive exact closed evolution equations for their populations and mutual coherence, and demonstrate memory effects using a causal-break operation. Although the system converges to the target thermal state, the thermalization behavior shows non-monotonic approach. We also find a Mpemba-like reversal, in which the number of collisions required for thermalization depends non-monotonically on the temperature of the target state. Within the initial-state family considered, both phenomena require initial coherence between the system and memory or an imbalance in their excited-state populations.

</details>


### [28] [From Heat to Homology: Spectral Gap Transfer for Exact Quantum Gibbs Sampling at All Temperatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02859)
*Caesnan M. G. Leditto,Kuo-Chin Chen,Min-Hsiu Hsieh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A spectral gap estimate at infinite temperature is converted into a lower bound on the CKG quantum Gibbs sampler's spectral gap at every positive inverse temperature, yielding temperature-independent convergence and polynomial-time preparation of harmonic states via a simplicial down-up walk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissipative preparation of Gibbs states requires convergence guarantees from the spectral gap of the dynamics generator, which is hard to establish for arbitrary Hamiltonians. Prior work sidesteps this by restricting to structured Hamiltonians, so a general, temperature-robust gap-to-gap comparison is needed.

Method: For the Chen-Kastoryano-Gilyen (CKG) construction and arbitrary finite-dimensional H, an explicit comparison converts an infinite-temperature generator spectral gap into a lower bound on the CKG Gibbs sampler's gap at every finite β. For Hodge Laplacians of simplicial complexes, coupling operators are constructed so that the infinite-temperature generator has the same gap as a classical simplicial down-up walk.

Result: A positive classical down-up walk gap plus bounded spectral width W yields a generator spectral gap independent of the number of simplices at every fixed temperature. Conditions are established under which the sampler approximately prepares zero-energy (harmonic) states in polynomial evolution time, giving a conditional convergence guarantee for the thermal approach to quantum topological data analysis.

Conclusion: A single infinite-temperature spectral gap estimate certifies CKG sampler convergence across Hamiltonians and temperatures. Applied to Hodge Laplacians, this connects classical random-walk gaps to quantum Gibbs sampling, with conditional polynomial-time harmonic-state preparation.

Abstract: Preparing Gibbs states through dissipative dynamics requires controlling convergence for the chosen Hamiltonian $H$ and coupling operators. However, efficient implementation requires convergence guarantees from the spectral gap of the generator of the dynamics, which remains a crucial challenge. Recent results address the challenge by restricting the dynamics with structural assumptions about the Hamiltonian and its interactions. For the Chen-Kastoryano-Gilyen (CKG) construction and arbitrary finite-dimensional Hamiltonians, we prove an explicit comparison that converts a spectral gap estimate for the infinite-temperature generator into a lower bound on the spectral gap of the CKG quantum Gibbs sampler at every finite positive inverse temperature $β$. It allows a single spectral gap estimate at infinite temperature to certify convergence across Hamiltonians and temperatures. Such an estimate is often known from a classical spectral gap estimate, such as the classical random walk spectral gap. For Hodge Laplacians of simplicial complexes as Hamiltonians, we construct coupling operators whose generator at infinite temperature has the same spectral gap as a classical simplicial down-up walk. A positive spectral gap of the walk and bounded spectral width $W=λ_{\max}(H)-λ_{\min}(H)$ give a generator spectral gap independent of the number of simplices at every fixed temperature. We then establish conditions under which the sampler approximately prepares zero-energy (harmonic) states in polynomial evolution time. This provides a conditional convergence guarantee for the state preparation in the thermal approach to quantum topological data analysis.

</details>


### [29] [Quasiparticle quantum simulation of materials with the Bethe-Salpeter equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02916)
*Jielun Chen,Jiace Sun,Garnet Kin-Lic Chan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops quasiparticle quantum simulation for multi-excitonic states within the Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE), using an explicit first-quantized, orbital-based block encoding of the multi-exciton BSE Hamiltonian for qubitization, with cost reduced via integral factorization and crystal symmetries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full first-principles quantum simulation of the complete electronic Hamiltonian for realistically sized material models is intractable even on future fault-tolerant quantum computers. Classical excited-state simulations instead rely on heuristic quasiparticle frameworks (GW approximation, BSE) that treat only a few quasiparticles explicitly, motivating a hybrid approach that embeds these heuristic frameworks into quantum algorithms.

Method: An explicit first-quantized, orbital-based block encoding of the multi-exciton BSE Hamiltonian tailored for qubitization. Cost is reduced through integral factorization and crystal symmetries. For a material model of size N with a fixed number of quasiparticles, two implementations are derived: a Toffoli-optimized version and, via space-time tradeoffs, a qubit-optimized version.

Result: The algorithms require Õ(N) Toffoli gates and O(log N) logical qubits, or Õ(√N) Toffoli gates and Õ(√N) logical qubits with space-time tradeoffs, achieving up to a sixth-power reduction in Toffoli count or up to exponential reduction in qubit count versus standard full Hamiltonian simulation. Resource estimates for triexcitonic eigenvalue estimation across semiconductor models of O(10^3) atoms show the Toffoli-optimized implementation reduces Toffoli count by 10^6–10^7 relative to full Hamiltonian simulation, while the qubit-optimized implementation needs only a few hundred logical qubits.

Conclusion: Classical heuristic frameworks such as GW and BSE will be essential in designing future fault-tolerant quantum algorithms for materials, as embedding them via block encoding yields dramatic resource savings over full Hamiltonian simulation.

Abstract: The first-principles quantum simulation of the full electronic Hamiltonian of a material model of realistic size appears daunting even in a future era of fully fault-tolerant quantum computers. In contrast, classical simulations of material excited states rely on heuristic quasiparticle frameworks, such as the GW approximation and Bethe-Salpeter equation, where only a few quasiparticles are explicitly considered. Here we give a detailed description of quasiparticle quantum simulation for multi-excitonic states within the BSE. We develop an explicit first-quantized, orbital-based block encoding of the multi-exciton BSE Hamiltonian for qubitization, and reduce its cost using integral factorization and crystal symmetries. For a materials model of size $N$, and a fixed number of quasiparticles, the resulting algorithms require $\widetilde{O}(N)$ Toffoli gates and $O(\log N)$ logical qubits, or, using space-time tradeoffs, $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{N})$ Toffoli gates and $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{N})$ logical qubits, achieving up to a sixth power reduction in Toffoli count, or up to exponential reduction in qubit count, versus standard full Hamiltonian simulation techniques that have been explored in materials simulation. Resource estimates for the classically challenging problem of triexcitonic eigenvalue estimation across a range of semiconductor models of a realistic physical size of $O(10^3)$ atoms, show that our Toffoli-optimized implementation reduces the Toffoli count by $10^6-10^7$ relative to full Hamiltonian simulation estimates, while a qubit-optimized implementation requires only a few hundred logical qubits. More broadly, these results show that classical heuristic frameworks will be essential in the design of future fault-tolerant quantum algorithms for materials.

</details>


### [30] [Root-sparsity scaling for Hamiltonian simulation, differential equations and linear-system solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02939)
*Dominic W. Berry,Abhishek Rawat,Sophia Simon,Guang Hao Low*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs quantum algorithms for Hamiltonian simulation, dissipative linear ODEs, and linear systems achieving square-root sparsity dependence, using exact entrywise representations and Cayley transducers, with nearly matching joint lower bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior quantum algorithms typically scale linearly (or worse) in sparsity s. The goal is to obtain optimal square-root dependence on sparsity across Hamiltonian simulation, linear differential equations, and linear systems, and to establish matching lower bounds that characterize the joint dependence on time, sparsity, and precision.

Method: The construction combines an exact entrywise representation of the input with Cayley transducers. For linear systems it uses a Cayley walk with a Chebyshev filter. Dissipative equations are handled by separating Hamiltonian and dissipative components, treating general constant generators, and certifying time discretisation for time-dependent Hamiltonians.

Result: Hamiltonian simulation of an s-sparse Hermitian matrix uses O(sqrt(s)τ + sqrt(sτ log(1/ε)) + log(1/ε)) queries where τ=η|t|, extended to time-dependent Hamiltonians; a joint lower bound nearly matches and rules out a uniformly additive bound in sqrt(s)τ and log(1/ε). Dissipative ODEs use O(ḡ[sqrt(s)(τ+sqrt τ)+1]log(Cḡ/ε)) queries. Linear systems achieve O(κ sqrt(s) log(1/ε)) queries with a matching joint lower bound.

Conclusion: Square-root sparsity dependence is achievable for Hamiltonian simulation, dissipative linear ODEs, and linear systems, and is essentially optimal via joint lower bounds. Gate-efficient implementations retaining these query bounds are given with polylogarithmic overhead under efficient entry decoding, arithmetic, and required source preparations.

Abstract: We give quantum algorithms for Hamiltonian simulation, linear differential equations, and linear systems with square-root dependence on sparsity. For an $s$-sparse Hermitian matrix with row and column Euclidean norms bounded by $η$, evolution for time $t$ to error $ε$ uses $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{s}τ+\sqrt{sτ\log(1/ε)}+\log(1/ε))$ queries, where $τ=η|t|$. The same bound holds for time-dependent Hamiltonians with coherent time-indexed sparse access and certified time discretisation. Our construction combines an exact entrywise representation with Cayley transducers. A joint lower bound nearly matches the time, sparsity, and precision dependence, ruling out a uniformly additive bound in $\sqrt{s}τ$ and $\log(1/ε)$. For dissipative linear differential equations we obtain $\mathcal{O}(\bar g[\sqrt{s}(τ+\sqrtτ)+1]\log(C\bar g/ε))$ queries, where $τ$ is the evolution time multiplied by a norm bound, $\bar g$ bounds the ratio of combined input and source norms to the final-solution norm, and $C$ is an absolute constant. We sharpen this bound by separating Hamiltonian and dissipative components, and treat general constant generators. Lower bounds capture joint parameter dependence for time-dependent equations and precision hardness for constant positive semidefinite dissipation. For linear systems, a Cayley walk and Chebyshev filter give $\mathcal{O}(κ\sqrt{s}\log(1/ε))$ queries when $\|A\|\le1$ and $\|A^{-1}\|\leκ$, and we give an alternative proof of a matching joint lower bound. We also give gate-efficient implementations of time-independent Hamiltonian simulation and the dissipative ODE solver, including Lipschitz time dependence, retaining the query bounds with polylogarithmic overhead in inverse precision given efficient entry decoding, arithmetic, and required source preparations.

</details>


### [31] [Resource Theory of Aquaternionicity: Every Pure State Is a Replicable Resource for Exact Simulation of Arbitrary Quantum Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02942)
*Hayato Arai,Yasuaki Nakayama*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces aquaternionicity, a new nontrivial alchemical resource theory for even-dimensional qudits (d≥4) based on a quaternionic structure (antiunitary Θ with Θ²=−I), generalizing the qubit-only theories of imaginarity and parity asymmetry, and enabling recalibration-free exact simulation robust to standard noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alchemical resource theories are powerful because a single nonfree state can be replicated by free operations, and finitely many copies simulate arbitrary quantum instruments. However, only two nontrivial examples were known—imaginarity and parity asymmetry—both restricted to qubits (exhausting the qubit case) and both exhibiting an all-or-nothing property: any nonmaximal resource only simulates free unitaries. This severely limits applicability to higher dimensions and practical noise scenarios, motivating a genuinely new, higher-dimensional, and robustly useful alchemical resource.

Method: The authors define aquaternionicity for even-dimensional qudits d≥4, grounded in a quaternionic structure represented by an antiunitary operator Θ satisfying Θ²=−I. They study its extremal properties: every pure state is alchemical, and alchemicality is maximally abundant under mixing. They analyze the admissible pure-mixing set M_g for any nontrivial d-dimensional resource theory with a pure alchemical state g, deriving the dimension bound dim M_g ≤ 2d−4, and prove aquaternionicity saturates this bound for every pure state while showing these two extremal properties characterize the quaternionic structure.

Result: Aquaternionicity saturates the bound dim M_g = 2d−4 for every pure state, making it extremal in both senses (all pure states alchemical; maximal abundance under mixing). The two extremal properties uniquely characterize the quaternionic structure. Since every pure state is alchemical, the initial resource can be chosen to match known noise structure; when noisy resource states lie in a common isotropic subspace, the same free processor works exactly for all of them while returning the resource unchanged.

Conclusion: Aquaternionicity provides recalibration-free exact simulation, including universal quantum computation, and is realized for standard dephasing and dissipative noise, where it substantially outperforms imaginarity and parity asymmetry. It establishes a new general framework for higher-dimensional alchemical resource theories with practical noise resilience.

Abstract: An alchemical resource theory admits a nonfree state that can be exactly replicated by free operations and whose finitely many copies enable exact simulation of arbitrary quantum instruments. The only known nontrivial examples, imaginarity and parity asymmetry, exhaust the qubit case (arXiv:2609.12988) and exhibit an all-or-nothing property: any nonmaximal resource exactly simulates only free unitaries. We introduce aquaternionicity for even-dimensional qudits $d\ge4$, based on a quaternionic structure represented by an antiunitary $Θ$ with $Θ^2=-I$. It is extremal in two senses: every pure state is alchemical, and alchemicality is maximally abundant under mixing. Within this framework, for any nontrivial $d$-dimensional resource theory and pure alchemical state $g$, the admissible pure-mixing set satisfies $\dim\mathcal M_g\le 2d-4,$ which aquaternionicity saturates for every pure state. Conversely, these two extremal properties characterize the quaternionic structure. Because every pure state is alchemical, the initial resource can be chosen to match the known noise structure. Moreover, when the noisy resource states remain within a common isotropic subspace, the same free processor works exactly for all of them while returning the resource unchanged. This enables recalibration-free exact simulation, including universal quantum computation, and is realized for standard dephasing and dissipative noise, where aquaternionicity substantially outperforms imaginarity and parity asymmetry.

</details>


### [32] [Einstein--Langevin source correlations in closed non-relativistic systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02980)
*Peng Xu,Li-E Qiang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the Newtonian projection of stochastic semiclassical gravity for closed, particle-number-conserving non-relativistic sources. It shows the connected one-particle source covariance is state dependent and distinct from the universal potential-noise law assumed in the Diósi–Penrose model, and derives bounded/vanishing probe dephasing for isolated non-degenerate eigenstates, with special cases (stationary doublets, freely spreading Gaussian sources) giving static/low-frequency lines or finite limiting exponents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic semiclassical gravity models stress–energy fluctuations via the Einstein–Langevin noise kernel, and the Diósi–Penrose model postulates a universal potential-noise law linking source fluctuations to matter decoherence. Whether the actual source covariance matches such a universal law—and under what spatial, temporal, and dynamical assumptions source fluctuations can be related to a matter-decoherence model—had not been systematically examined for closed, particle-number-conserving non-relativistic sources.

Method: The authors examine the Newtonian projection of stochastic semiclassical gravity for specified closed, particle-number-conserving non-relativistic sources, distinguishing the source covariance from a reduced matter channel. They compute the connected one-particle covariance, define a separate static probe subject to a prescribed Gaussian noise law, and relate the source spectrum to probe dephasing. They analyze isolated non-degenerate eigenstates with finite-variance source operators, stationary doublets, and a freely spreading Gaussian source, and compare coherent versus incoherent many-body preparations, establishing no-signalling for fixed classical-noise channels.

Result: The connected one-particle covariance is state dependent and differs from the universal potential-noise law postulated in the Diósi–Penrose model. For a finite-variance source operator in an isolated non-degenerate eigenstate, the full connected spectral measure has a gap around zero, so the probe dephasing exponent is bounded and its long-time slope vanishes. Stationary doublets can carry static or low-frequency spectral lines, while a freely spreading Gaussian source gives a finite limiting exponent. Continuum results depend on preparation, normalisation, infrared regularisation, and the order of limits. No-signalling holds for fixed classical-noise channels.

Conclusion: The results identify the spatial, temporal, and dynamical assumptions needed to relate source fluctuations to a matter-decoherence model. They do not exclude Markovian limits in other number-conserving non-relativistic preparations.

Abstract: Stochastic semiclassical gravity represents stress--energy fluctuations through the Einstein--Langevin noise kernel. We examine its Newtonian projection for specified closed, particle-number-conserving non-relativistic sources and distinguish the source covariance from a reduced matter channel. The connected one-particle covariance is state dependent and differs from the universal potential-noise law postulated in the Diósi--Penrose model. To relate the source spectrum to dephasing, we define a separate static probe subject to a prescribed Gaussian noise law. For a finite-variance source operator in an isolated non-degenerate eigenstate, the full connected spectral measure has a gap around zero. The probe dephasing exponent is then bounded and its long-time slope vanishes. Stationary doublets can instead carry static or low-frequency spectral lines, while a specified freely spreading Gaussian source gives a finite limiting exponent. Continuum results depend on preparation, normalisation, infrared regularisation and the order of limits. We also compare coherent and incoherent many-body preparations and establish no-signalling for fixed classical-noise channels. These results identify the spatial, temporal and dynamical assumptions needed to relate source fluctuations to a matter-decoherence model. They do not exclude Markovian limits in other number-conserving non-relativistic preparations.

</details>


### [33] [Two-qutrit Werner state is always local](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02997)
*Paweł Caban,Muhammad Mohsin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that a two-qutrit Werner state admits a local hidden-variable model for all parameter values in a scenario with two projective measurements per site and three outcomes per measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish the boundary between quantum nonlocality and local hidden-variable descriptions in higher-dimensional (qutrit) bipartite systems.

Method: Derive a simple sufficient condition under which a two-qutrit state satisfies the Collins-Gisin-Linden-Massar-Popescu (CGLMP) inequality for arbitrary observable choices, and show the Werner state always satisfies this condition. Then invoke the fact that any two-qutrit state satisfying the CGLMP inequality for arbitrary observable choices admits a local hidden-variable model.

Result: The two-qutrit Werner state satisfies the derived sufficient condition, hence satisfies the CGLMP inequality for arbitrary Alice/Bob observables, and therefore admits a local hidden-variable model for all parameter values.

Conclusion: The two-qutrit Werner state is local (admits an LHV model) across the entire parameter range in the two-measurements-per-site, three-outcomes-per-measurement scenario.

Abstract: In our paper, we show that a two-qutrit Werner state admits a local hidden-variable model for all values of the parameter in a scenario involving two projective measurements per site, with three outcomes per measurement. To this end, we derive a simple sufficient condition under which a two-qutrit state satisfies the Collins-Gisin-Linden-Massar-Popescu inequality for arbitrary choices of Alice's and Bob's observables, and show that the Werner state always satisfies this condition. Next, we use the fact that any two-qutrit state satisfying the Collins-Gisin-Linden-Massar-Popescu inequality for arbitrary choices of Alice's and Bob's observables admits a local hidden variable model.

</details>


### [34] [Spatiotemporal quantum advantages for rare-event sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03026)
*Akshat Gupta,Jianjun Chen,Chengran Yang,Mile Gu,Caterina Doglioni,Jayne Thompson,Thomas J. Elliott*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a rare-event sampling method built on quantum stochastic models, combining quantum memory advantages with quantum amplitude amplification to achieve joint reductions in spatial and temporal complexity relative to classical Monte Carlo methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare, high-consequence, low-probability events are critical to anticipate for mitigation, but their rarity hampers study: few past occurrences exist to draw on, and sampling them from models is typically time-costly.

Method: The approach integrates quantum stochastic modelling — exploiting its memory advantages and efficient preparation of quantum sample states — with quantum amplitude amplification to speed up rare-event sampling.

Result: The method yields simultaneous reductions in both spatial and temporal computational complexity compared with classical Monte Carlo-based approaches.

Conclusion: Quantum stochastic modelling plus amplitude amplification offers a more efficient route to rare-event sampling, addressing the scarcity and computational cost limitations of classical sampling, thereby supporting better mitigation of extreme risk events.

Abstract: In a world fraught with uncertainty, it behooves us to consider the most disastrous possible outcomes, in order that we can best mitigate against them. Often these are extreme risk events -- of high consequence, but low probability. Yet, the rarity of such events limits our ability to study them; there is a paucity of past occurrences to draw upon, and sampling such rare events from models is typically costly in time. Here, we introduce an approach for rare-event sampling based on quantum stochastic models. This integrates the memory advantages of quantum stochastic modelling -- and its associated efficient means of preparing quantum sample states -- with the `speed-ups' of quantum amplitude amplification, to yield simultaneous reductions in spatial and temporal computational complexity compared to classical Monte Carlo-based approaches.

</details>

  <summary>Details</summary>

Conclusion: Direct estimation of g^(2)(0) via this technique is established as a simple and versatile method for quantitatively characterizing quantum light using multiplexed click detectors.

Abstract: We present and theoretically analyze a method for direct estimation of the second-order correlation function $g^{(2)}(0)$ from the click statistics of a multiplexed detector composed of M binary on-off detection channels. The method uses linear estimators of the first and second moments of the photon-number operator and can provide accurate results even for optical fields with substantial mean photon numbers. For a fixed number of channels, the detection range can be extended by introducing additional losses before the detector. We demonstrate the performance of the method for coherent, thermal, amplitude-squeezed, and photon-added coherent states. We also derive a nonclassicality criterion based on the estimated correlation function and show how the method can be adapted to unbalanced detectors. Our work establishes direct estimation of $g^{(2)}(0)$ as a simple and versatile method for quantitatively characterizing quantum light using multiplexed click detectors.

### [36] [Analytical controls for dispersive multi-qubit interactions in mediator-coupled quantum registers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03064)
*Bora Baran,Tommaso Calarco,James O'Sullivan,Matthias M. Müller,Felix Motzoi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: DIAL is an analytical control method that engineers excitation-suppressed multi-qubit interactions in mediator-coupled quantum registers by off-resonantly driving an effective two-level mediator, mapping drive intensities linearly to Pauli-Z interaction rates and achieving >0.997 gate fidelity with ~1% transient mediator excitation.

  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast control of long-lived quantum registers typically relies on an auxiliary mediator degree of freedom, but exciting the mediator can introduce decoherence, leakage, or loss when it is not the coherence-optimized component. There is a need for interaction-engineering schemes that retain speed and controllability while suppressing mediator excitation.

Method: The paper introduces dispersive interaction via analytical linear inversion (DIAL). In the dispersive regime, off-resonant driving of an effective two-level mediator transition produces a diagonal Hamiltonian whose Pauli-Z interaction rates depend linearly on drive intensities via a transfer matrix set by the register-resolved transition spectrum. This yields a direct linear map from experimentally accessible controls to induced multi-qubit interactions, enabling control design without iterative propagation of the full driven dynamics. The target-aware DIAL algorithm selects favorable off-resonant tones and determines their drive intensities for a prescribed target interaction.

Conclusion: DIAL provides an analytical, non-iterative framework for excitation-suppressed interaction engineering in mediator-coupled quantum registers, delivering high-fidelity multi-qubit gates (including GHZ-state generation up to local equivalence) with minimal mediator excitation. An open-source Python package implements the target-aware DIAL algorithm and the associated numerical validation workflow.

Abstract: Fast control of long-lived quantum registers often relies on an auxiliary degree of freedom, a mediator, but real mediator excitation can introduce decoherence, leakage, or loss when the mediator is not the coherence-optimized component. We introduce dispersive interaction via analytical linear inversion (DIAL), an analytical control construction for excitation-suppressed interaction engineering in mediator-coupled quantum registers. In the dispersive regime, we show that off-resonant driving of an effective two-level mediator transition yields a diagonal Hamiltonian whose Pauli-\(Z\) interaction rates depend linearly on the drive intensities through a transfer matrix determined by the register-resolved transition spectrum. This establishes a direct linear map from experimentally accessible controls to induced multi-qubit interactions, enabling control design without iterative propagation of the full driven dynamics. We turn this structure into the target-aware DIAL algorithm, which selects favorable off-resonant tones and determines their drive intensities for a prescribed target interaction. We benchmark the resulting controls across 100 register realizations for each register size \(n=2,3,4,5\), targeting the highest-order interaction \(Z_1\cdots Z_n\) with an interaction phase of magnitude \(π/4\), which generates an \(n\)-qubit GHZ state up to local equivalence. Propagation under the full driven mediator-register dynamics yields typical gate fidelities above \(0.997\), while the maximum transient mediator excitation remains near \(1\%\) on average, far below what is typically possible with resonant Rabi drives. The manuscript is accompanied by an open-source Python package implementing the target-aware DIAL algorithm and the associated numerical validation workflow.

</details>


### [37] [Orbital-Rotation Shadow Tomography Reduces the Classical Cost of Quantum-Classical Auxiliary-Field Quantum Monte Carlo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03103)
*Luning Zhao,Joshua Goings,Evgeny Epifanovsky,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces particle-number-preserving fermionic (orbital-rotation) shadows to drastically cut the classical post-processing bottleneck in quantum-classical AFQMC, with proven efficient estimators and GPU-accelerated demonstration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QC-AFQMC is a promising route to using quantum computers for strongly correlated electronic structure, but its scalability is limited by a classical post-processing bottleneck: the measured quantum trial state must be used to evaluate overlaps, force biases, and local energies for many walkers throughout AFQMC propagation. Existing matchgate-shadow approaches avoid the exponential cost of generic classical shadows but their post-processing remains expensive for large systems.

Method: The authors employ particle-number-preserving fermionic shadows implemented as orbital-rotation shadows to reduce QC-AFQMC post-processing costs. They derive efficient estimators for overlaps and their derivatives, giving O(N^3) scaling for overlaps and force biases and O(N^4) scaling for local energies per shadow and walker, with O(N) Cholesky vectors. They prove the overlap estimator is unbiased, with variance upper bounded by O(eta) for fixed normalized target determinants, yielding a sufficient budget of O(eta/eps^2) ideal independent measurements for additive overlap error eps. They combine these estimators with a novel virtual-correlation-energy treatment and a GPU implementation.

Result: Performance projections for pi-conjugated molecules up to pentacene with a (22e, 22o) active space and 356 total orbitals show the full classical post-processing workflow can be completed in slightly more than two days on 8xA100 GPUs.

Conclusion: Orbital-rotation shadows significantly reduce classical post-processing costs for QC-AFQMC, motivating further improvements in preparation and measurement of the trial state on quantum hardware.

Abstract: Quantum-classical auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo (QC-AFQMC) is a promising route to using quantum computers for strongly correlated electronic structure problems, but its scalability is limited by a classical post-processing bottleneck: the measured quantum trial state must be used to evaluate overlaps, force biases, and local energies for many walkers throughout the AFQMC propagation. Existing matchgate-shadow approaches avoid the exponential cost of generic classical shadows, but their post-processing remains expensive for large systems. In this work, we show that particle-number-preserving fermionic shadows, implemented as orbital-rotation shadows, are able to drastically reduce QC-AFQMC post-processing costs. We derive efficient estimators for overlaps and their derivatives, giving $O(N^3)$ scaling for overlaps and force biases and $O(N^4)$ scaling for local energies per shadow and walker and with $O(N)$ Cholesky vector. For fixed normalized target determinants, we also prove that the overlap estimator is unbiased with variance upper bounded by $O(η)$, where $η$ is the particle number, yielding a sufficient budget of $O(η/ε^2)$ ideal independent measurements for additive overlap error $ε$. We combine these estimators with a novel virtual-correlation-energy treatment and a GPU implementation and demonstrate QC-AFQMC using orbital-rotation shadows. Performance projections for $π$-conjugated molecules up to pentacene with a (22e, 22o) active space and 356 total orbitals show that the full classical post-processing workflow can be completed in slightly more than two days on 8$\times$A100 graphics processing units (GPUs). These results significantly reduce classical post-processing costs for QC-AFQMC and motivate further improvements in preparation and measurement of the trial state on quantum hardware.

</details>


### [38] [Certification of high-dimensional entanglement in continuous-variable systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03113)
*Ida Mishra,Simon Morelli,Klára Baksová,Phila Rembold,Nicolai Friis*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces fidelity bounds for Schmidt-number detection in mode-entangled continuous-variable systems using only first- and second-moment measurements, providing analytical formulas and comparing them to existing high-dimensional entanglement certification methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Schmidt number quantifies entanglement dimensionality and large values offer advantages for quantum communication and computation. Existing Schmidt-number detection for finite-dimensional systems relies on fidelity estimation to highly entangled pure states, but the required measurements are typically difficult to realize for mode-entangled continuous-variable systems.

Method: The authors introduce and assess fidelity bounds for Schmidt-number detection based on practically accessible measurements of the first and second statistical moments of arbitrary continuous-variable quantum states. They derive analytical formulas to evaluate these bounds and test them on Gaussian and non-Gaussian state families, including two-mode squeezed thermal states and photon-added displaced two-mode squeezed vacuum states.

Result: Analytical formulas are provided for evaluating the proposed bounds. The bounds are tested on two-mode squeezed thermal states and photon-added displaced two-mode squeezed vacuum states, and are compared against other high-dimensional entanglement certification methods based on the same measurements.

Conclusion: Fidelity bounds based on first- and second-moment measurements offer a practically accessible route to Schmidt-number detection in continuous-variable systems, providing an alternative to measurement-intensive fidelity estimation and complementing existing certification methods.

Abstract: The dimensionality of entanglement in high-dimensional quantum systems is quantified by the Schmidt number, whose large value signals potential advantages for quantum communication and quantum computation. A commonly used approach to practical Schmidt-number detection in finite-dimensional systems is based on the estimation of fidelities to pure states with large entanglement dimensionality or lower bounds to these fidelities. However, for mode-entangled continuous-variable systems, the corresponding measurements are typically difficult to realize. Here, we introduce and assess fidelity bounds for Schmidt-number detection based on practically more accessible measurements of the first and second statistical moments of arbitrary continuous-variable quantum states. We provide analytical formulas that allow evaluating these bounds and test them for families of Gaussian and non-Gaussian states, including two-mode squeezed thermal states and photon-added displaced two-mode squeezed vacuum states. We compare our bounds to other methods for the certification of high-dimensional entanglement based on the same measurements.

</details>


### [39] [Circuit growth and subspace read-out in quantum-selected configuration interaction: Separating the Hartree-Fock determinant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03114)
*Ayaka Fujimoto,Norifumi Matsumoto,Shota Kanasugi,Kazunori Maruyama,Hirotaka Oshima*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper modifies ADAPT-QSCI by classically seeding the Hartree-Fock configuration into the diagonalization subspace, eliminating redundant measurements and introducing a new closed-form operator-selection criterion, which yields substantial gate and shot reductions at practical shot budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In single-reference molecules, the sampling distribution of ADAPT-QSCI is dominated by the Hartree-Fock configuration, so most quantum measurements reproduce a configuration already known. This measurement redundancy wastes quantum resources, motivating a scheme that avoids re-sampling the known HF configuration.

Method: The HF configuration is placed into the diagonalization subspace classically before any measurement. Because this invalidates the original ADAPT-QSCI operator-selection criterion, a consistent replacement is derived with energy and gradient in closed form, requiring no additional queries to the quantum device.

Result: At the per-iteration shot budgets previously used for ADAPT-QSCI, the proposed scheme reaches chemical accuracy with approximately 63% and 43% of the CNOT gates for H4 and H6, and 44% and 40% of the total shots. However, the advantage is budget-dependent: in the infinite-shot limit, the H4 CNOT count rises to 125% of that of ADAPT-QSCI.

Conclusion: The per-iteration shot budget is identified as a key design variable governing resource savings in QSCI-based algorithms, and seeding the HF configuration classically can deliver significant practical resource reductions under finite-shot conditions.

Abstract: Predicting many molecular properties requires accurate ground-state energies of molecular Hamiltonians. Quantum-selected configuration interaction (QSCI) obtains such energies by measuring an input state to select important electronic configurations and classically diagonalizing the Hamiltonian in the subspace they span. ADAPT-QSCI, an adaptive extension of QSCI, improves the input state through iterative circuit growth. For single-reference molecules, the sampling distribution is dominated by the Hartree-Fock (HF) configuration, so most measurements reproduce a configuration already known. We reduce this redundancy by placing the HF configuration in the diagonalization subspace classically, before any measurement. This invalidates the operator-selection criterion of ADAPT-QSCI, and we derive a consistent replacement with energy and gradient in closed form, adding no query to the quantum device. At the per-iteration shot budgets previously used for ADAPT-QSCI, the proposed scheme reaches chemical accuracy with approximately $63\%$ and $43\%$ of the CNOT gates for $\mathrm{H}_4$ and $\mathrm{H}_6$, and $44\%$ and $40\%$ of the total shots. The advantage depends on the budget, however: in the infinite-shot limit the $\mathrm{H}_4$ CNOT count increases to $125\%$ of that of ADAPT-QSCI. These results identify the per-iteration shot budget as a key design variable governing resource savings in QSCI-based algorithms.

</details>


### [40] [Infrared Memory of a Majorana Shutter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03133)
*Ali Vahedi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A finite-width Majorana shutter is shown to have infrared memory controlled only by its low-frequency scattering data, with three many-body observables (vacuum overlap, quasiparticle number, entanglement entropy) governed by the same scattering angle but with non-interchangeable coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to determine which features of the infrared memory of a local, time-dependent scattering defect (a finite-width Majorana shutter) are insensitive to its microscopic spatial profile, and to clarify how low-frequency scattering data connect distinct global and spatial observables.

Method: The authors model the finite-width Majorana shutter as a local time-dependent scattering defect. They analyze the zero-frequency scattering matrix for a smooth shutter, derive the infrared Bogoliubov kernel from a time-dependent switch, and compute three observables: the overlap of pre- and post-shutter Gaussian vacua, a lattice calculation of the post-quench quasiparticle number, and the entanglement entropy for a cut through or inside the shutter (conformal defect/effective-central-charge description), plus a local bilinear correlator.

Result: For a smooth shutter, the zero-frequency scattering matrix is fixed by the integrated coupling, with finite-width corrections vanishing in the long-wavelength limit. The vacuum overlap shows Anderson orthogonality with an exponent set by the scattering angle. The lattice quasiparticle number shows logarithmic infrared growth, matching the predicted coefficient in the slow-switch test and the expected dependence on the scattering-angle change. Entanglement entropy follows the effective-central-charge description of a conformal defect with parameter-free percent-level agreement, improving for wider shutters; when the shutter lies inside the interval its contribution is a subleading finite-size correction. The local bilinear correlator approaches the expected Majorana power law with no stable logarithmic amplitude.

Conclusion: The same low-frequency scattering data control distinct global and spatial observables, but their infrared coefficients are not interchangeable. The remaining limits are made explicit: the finite-memory correction to the continuum kernel and the controlled continuum limit of large-interval spatial entropy.

Abstract: We study a finite-width Majorana shutter as a local, time-dependent scattering defect and determine which features of its infrared memory are insensitive to the microscopic spatial profile. For a smooth shutter, the zero-frequency scattering matrix is fixed by the integrated coupling, while finite-width corrections vanish in the long-wavelength limit. A time-dependent switch then produces an infrared Bogoliubov kernel whose singular part is controlled by the change in this scattering matrix.
  We examine the many-body response through three observables. The overlap of the pre- and post-shutter Gaussian vacua exhibits Anderson orthogonality, with an exponent set by the scattering angle. A lattice calculation of the post-quench quasiparticle number resolves its logarithmic infrared growth, reaching the predicted coefficient in the slow-switch test and reproducing the expected dependence on the scattering-angle change. When the entanglement cut passes through the shutter, the entropy follows the effective-central-charge description of a conformal defect, with parameter-free agreement at the percent level, improving for the wider shutter. When the shutter lies inside the interval, its contribution remains a subleading finite-size correction. A local bilinear correlator approaches the expected Majorana power law and shows no stable logarithmic amplitude.
  These results show that the same low-frequency scattering data control distinct global and spatial observables, while their infrared coefficients are not interchangeable. The remaining limits are also made explicit: the finite-memory correction to the continuum kernel and the controlled continuum limit of large-interval spatial entropy.

</details>

*Vadim Lebedev,Alejandro Martínez-Méndez,Jesús Moreno-Meseguer,Jan Engels,Jürgen Weippert,Jan Kustermann,Felix Hoffmann,Javier Prior,Jan Jeske*

  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Negatively charged nitrogen-vacancy (NV⁻) centers in diamond are promising for quantum technologies, but creating spatially controlled, high-density 2D/3D arrays of these color centers at wafer scale is challenging. Abstract: Here, we report the fabrication and characterization of single crystal diamond films on a two-inch wafer-scale containing three-dimensional layered structures of negatively charged nitrogen-vacancy color centers (NV$^{-}$). The (100)-oriented layered diamond films were grown by chemical vapor deposition (CVD) to create spatially separated regions of enhanced emission associated with NV$^{-}$ centers. Such localized emission enhancement as well as 2D and 3D arrays of spins could enable close-packed architectures for emerging quantum devices, such as data processors, memory cells, and magnetic sensors. A cyclic sequence of CVD growth and oxygen-plasma etching is employed, resulting in the in-situ formation of layered inverted pyramidal structures with flat $\{$111$\}$ sidewalls. These pits are subsequently overgrown by nitrogen-doped diamond to achieve NV$^{-}$-rich facets with a uniform N-V bond arrangement and improved nitrogen incorporation efficiency.

</details>


### [42] [Landscape-Dependent Performance of Photonic Quantum Solvers in QUBO Feature Selection for Financial Risk Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03161)
*Nirvik Sahoo,Paul Robert Griffin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks three computing paradigms—classical branch-and-bound (Gurobi), photonic entropy computing (QCI Dirac-3), and simulated photonic boson sampling (Piquasso)—across thirteen feature-selection methods on two imbalanced datasets (ULB credit card fraud, 30 features; AmEx consumer default, 159 features), finding that solver choice rarely matters beyond run-to-run variation, while method-to-method variance dominates on the smaller dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feature selection for imbalanced classification tasks such as credit card fraud and consumer default detection must simultaneously satisfy predictive relevance, low inter-feature redundancy, and computational feasibility. The paper asks whether emerging non-classical computing paradigms (photonic entropy computing and simulated photonic boson sampling) can serve as competitive solvers relative to established classical optimization, and how solver choice interacts with feature-selection method and dataset structure.

Method: The authors benchmark three paradigms—classical branch-and-bound optimization (Gurobi), photonic entropy computing (QCI Dirac-3), and simulated photonic boson sampling (Piquasso)—across thirteen feature-selection methods on two datasets: ULB Credit Card Fraud (30 features) and AmEx consumer default (159 features). Each method is routed to the solver matched to its mathematical structure, and performance is measured by F1 over five runs.

Result: On ULB, Dirac-3 MI-Spearman matches the all-features model using 13 of 30 features (mean F1 0.873 +/- 0.023, best run 0.896), and Piquasso is the best method at k=5. On AmEx, performance rises steadily with the feature budget and every paradigm approaches F1 = 0.80 only near the full feature set. Most differences between Gurobi and Dirac-3 on identical methods fall within run-to-run variation; large gaps appear where the certified optimum generalizes poorly, most sharply for distance correlation on AmEx at k=25 (Gurobi F1 = 0.422 vs. Dirac-3 mean 0.746). At matched budgets, F1 varies roughly ten times more across methods on ULB than on AmEx, traced to how concentrated the predictive signal is in each feature space.

Conclusion: Solver choice among the three paradigms is largely secondary: most Gurobi–Dirac-3 gaps are within run-to-run noise, and discrepancies arise mainly when the certified optimum generalizes poorly. Method-to-method variability, rather than paradigm choice, is the dominant factor on the smaller ULB dataset (about ten times that on AmEx), driven by how concentrated the predictive signal is. Feature budget requirements differ sharply by dataset, with AmEx needing near-full feature sets to reach F1 = 0.80.

Abstract: Feature selection for imbalanced classification tasks such as credit card fraud and consumer default detection requires balancing predictive relevance, inter-feature redundancy, and computational feasibility. We benchmark three computing paradigms, classical branch-and-bound optimization (Gurobi), photonic entropy computing (QCI Dirac-3), and simulated photonic boson sampling (Piquasso), across thirteen feature-selection methods on two datasets: ULB Credit Card Fraud (30 features) and AmEx consumer default (159 features). Each method is routed to the solver matched to its mathematical structure. On ULB, Dirac-3 MI-Spearman matches the all-features model using 13 of 30 features (mean F1 0.873 +/- 0.023 over five runs, best run 0.896), and Piquasso is the best method at k=5. On AmEx, performance rises steadily with the feature budget and every paradigm approaches F1 = 0.80 only near the full feature set. Most differences between Gurobi and Dirac-3 on identical methods fall within run-to-run variation; the large gaps occur where the certified optimum generalizes poorly, most sharply for distance correlation on AmEx at k=25 (Gurobi F1 = 0.422 vs. a Dirac-3 mean of 0.746). At matched budgets, F1 varies about ten times more across methods on ULB than on AmEx, which we trace to how concentrated the predictive signal is in each feature space.

</details>


### [43] [Self-testing ideal quantum measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03168)
*Debashis Saha,Zhen-Peng Xu,Adán Cabello*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Quantum mechanics permits device-independent self-testing of any ideal (projective) measurement, certified via the output statistics of bipartite Bell tests in which a non-demolition measurement is sometimes added.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ideal measurements—repeating consistently and not disturbing jointly measurable observables—are a fundamental prediction of quantum mechanics, described in the quantum instrument formalism by projective effects with the Lueders state-update rule. Certifying such measurements in a device-independent way is the goal.

Method: The authors use only the output statistics of bipartite Bell tests, in which a non-demolition measurement is sometimes added, to certify whether the added measurement is ideal.

Result: Quantum mechanics allows for self-testing any ideal measurement.

Abstract: A fundamental prediction of quantum mechanics is that observables can be measured through ideal measurements, that is, measurements that give the same outcome when repeated and do not disturb any observable that is jointly measurable. In terms of quantum instruments, such an ideal measurement is described by projective effects together with the associated Luders state-update rule. Here, we show that quantum mechanics allows for self-testing any ideal measurement. Using solely the output statistics of bipartite Bell tests in which a non-demolition measurement is sometimes added, it is possible to certify in a device-independent manner whether the added measurement is ideal.

</details>


### [44] [Topological phase transition in a symmetric blockade structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03186)
*Johannes Mögerle,Simon Fell,Tobias F. Maier,Ali Otaifi,Lode Pollet,Nicolai Lang,Hans Peter Büchler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A large-scale iDMRG study maps the full phase diagram of a blockade Hamiltonian with local Z2 symmetry, confirming a single continuous transition between a topologically ordered and a trivial paramagnetic phase, with charge confinement in the trivial phase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A topologically ordered ground state has been rigorously proven only for weak Rabi driving in this blockade Hamiltonian; the phase diagram beyond weak driving remains an open question.

Method: Large-scale infinite-size density matrix renormalization group (iDMRG) simulations on a cylinder, complemented by computation of excitation gaps (charge gap and symmetric-sector gap).

Result: A single continuous phase transition separates the topologically ordered phase from a trivial paramagnetic phase. The topological order persists up to Rabi drives comparable to the characteristic energy scale. The charge gap stays open across the transition while charges become confined in the trivial phase; the symmetric-sector gap closes at the transition, consistent with flux-condensation.

Conclusion: The numerically mapped phase diagram extends the rigorous weak-driving result, showing robust topological order and a flux-condensation-driven continuous transition, with charge confinement characterizing the trivial phase.

Abstract: We present a numerical study of a blockade Hamiltonian with a local $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry. For such models, the existence of a topologically ordered ground state has been proven rigorously for weak Rabi driving [T. F. Maier et al., PRX Quantum 6, 030340 (2025)]. However, the phase diagram beyond weak driving is still an open question. We perform large-scale infinite-size density matrix renormalization group (iDMRG) simulations on a cylinder and map out the full phase diagram of the model. We find a single continuous phase transition between the topologically ordered phase and a trivial paramagnetic phase, and confirm numerically the stability of the topologically ordered phase up to Rabi drives comparable to the characteristic energy scale of the system. Furthermore, we compute the excitation gaps and show that the charge gap remains open across the phase transition and charges become confined in the trivial phase. On the other hand, the gap in the symmetric sector closes at the transition, consistent with the condensation of flux excitations.

</details>


### [45] [Emergent Dissipation from Fluctuating Quantum-Network Topology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03194)
*Alireza Nourmandipour,Stefano Mancini*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Dissipation in a quantum network can arise from temporal fluctuations of the interaction topology: averaging over rapidly redrawn graphs yields a Markovian master equation where the mean network sets the coherent part and the adjacency covariance sets the dissipative structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how dissipation can emerge intrinsically in a quantum network from temporal fluctuations of its interaction topology, rather than being imposed externally, and to establish fluctuating connectivity as an engineering resource.

Method: The graph of the network is redrawn at short time intervals; averaging the resulting dynamics over the fluctuations yields a Markovian master equation. The mean network fixes the coherent (Hamiltonian) part, while the covariance of the adjacency fixes the dissipative structure. The covariance eigenvalues set noise strengths and its eigenvectors determine the collective jump operators. Two ensembles with identical mean adjacency and covariance spectra but different eigenvectors are compared, and a physically realizable model is analyzed for its dissipative gap.

Result: Two ensembles sharing identical mean adjacency and covariance spectra but differing eigenvectors produce distinct dynamics despite identical purity. A physically realizable model yields an analytically predicted dissipative gap, giving quasi-protected dynamics.

Conclusion: Fluctuating connectivity is established as a resource for engineering quantum dissipation and protection, with the covariance structure providing a design handle over noise strengths and collective operators.

Abstract: We investigate how dissipation can emerge in a quantum network from temporal fluctuations of the interaction
  topology. For a network whose graph is redrawn at short time intervals, the averaged
  dynamics yields a Markovian master equation: the mean network fixes the
  coherent part and the covariance fixes the dissipative structure. The
  covariance eigenvalues set the noise strengths, while its eigenvectors
  determine the collective operators on which those strengths act. Two ensembles
  with identical mean adjacency and covariance spectra but different
  eigenvectors produce distinct dynamics despite identical purity. A physically
  realizable model yields an analytically predicted dissipative gap, giving
  quasi-protected dynamics. These results establish fluctuating connectivity as
  a resource for engineering quantum dissipation and protection.

</details>


### [46] [Hamiltonian locality testing and certification do not achieve the Heisenberg limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03205)
*Francisco Escudero Gutiérrez,Junseo Lee,Sebastian Zur*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves the first lower bounds ruling out Heisenberg-limited 1/ε scaling for natural Hamiltonian testing problems, showing Ω(1/ε²) total evolution time is necessary in a forward-only continuous-time query model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hamiltonian learning and testing often hope for Heisenberg-limited scaling (total evolution time ~1/ε). Prior work (Kallaugher–Liang, Sinha–Tong) gave upper bounds of O(1/ε²), but no matching lower bounds existed for natural problems when only the forward time-evolution operator (not its inverse) is accessible. The authors aim to close this gap and determine whether Heisenberg-limited scaling is achievable in this setting.

Method: The model allows multiple queries to the time-evolution operator (no inverse), with distances measured in normalized Frobenius norm. The core technique reduces all three problems to the hardness of distinguishing the zero Hamiltonian from a suitably chosen ensemble of random Hamiltonians. This hardness is established by adapting the continuous-time adversary method to forward-only Hamiltonian evolution.

Result: (1) Testing whether a Hamiltonian is k-local or ε-far from every k-local Hamiltonian requires Ω(1/ε²) total evolution time, matching Kallaugher–Liang (TQC'25). (2) Testing whether an unknown Hamiltonian equals a target or is ε-far from it requires Ω(1/ε²), matching Sinha–Tong (2025). (3) Amplitude estimation to precision ε requires Ω(1/ε²) total evolution time, recovering Tang–Wright (QIP'26) in the continuous-time query model. These are the first lower bounds for natural Hamiltonian learning/testing problems that rule out Heisenberg-limited 1/ε scaling.

Conclusion: In the forward-only continuous-time query model, Heisenberg-limited scaling is impossible for these natural Hamiltonian testing and amplitude estimation tasks; Ω(1/ε²) total evolution time is necessary and matches known upper bounds, establishing tight complexity.

Abstract: We establish lower bounds for Hamiltonian property testing with access to the time-evolution operator but not its inverse. Each experiment may query the time-evolution operator multiple times, and distances between Hamiltonians are measured in the normalized Frobenius norm.
  In this model, we show that testing whether a Hamiltonian is $k$-local or $\varepsilon$-far from every $k$-local Hamiltonian requires $Ω(1/\varepsilon^2)$ total evolution time, matching the upper bound of Kallaugher and Liang (TQC'25). We also prove that testing whether an unknown Hamiltonian equals a target Hamiltonian or is $\varepsilon$-far from it requires $Ω(1/\varepsilon^2)$ total evolution time, matching the upper bound of Sinha and Tong (2025). These are the first lower bounds for natural problems in Hamiltonian learning and testing that rule out Heisenberg-limited scaling of $1/\varepsilon$.
  As a third result, we show that amplitude estimation to precision $\varepsilon$ requires $Ω(1/\varepsilon^2)$ total time evolution, recovering the result of Tang and Wright (QIP'26) in the continuous-time query model. All three results follow from the hardness of distinguishing the zero Hamiltonian from a suitably chosen ensemble of random Hamiltonians. We establish this hardness by adapting the continuous-time adversary method to forward Hamiltonian evolution.

</details>


### [47] [Quantum code parameters, checkable by a certificate of provable size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03214)
*Shuoming An,Fusheng Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper makes quantum code parameters (notably the distance) formally checkable by shifting the unit of work from the code itself to a short certificate, whose correctness is verified by computation in the Lean proof assistant kernel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A code's distance is an optimum over an exponentially large set, reliably read off a solver whose answer carries no certificate. Whether code parameters can be checked uniformly across codes, with a certificate of proved size, remains open. The work aims to make a distance checkable rather than believed.

Method: Move the unit of work from the code to a certificate—a short object that is a list, a pairing, or a symbolic instance—and let the Lean kernel decide its correctness by computation. Certificate size is itself a theorem: the enumeration form lists all vectors of weight below d, i.e. sum_{j<d} C(n,j) of them, polynomial in n at fixed d; for product families a smaller, symbolically proved bound is used. Outside-the-row-space decisions reduce to one matrix-vector product and one inner product. Only Lean's three standard logical axioms are trusted.

Result: The full-file check for the row-space decision on an 18-qubit toric code drops from 42 s to 9 s. Eleven code families and thirty-nine parameter sets are covered, the widest at 1872 qubits. Caveats: the certificate generator stops at twenty qubits, and the lower bound for the 144-qubit code is imported rather than proved here.

Conclusion: Distance becomes checkable rather than believed. No uniform bound in both n and d is smaller than the enumeration certificate size, and the same certificate-based move applies wherever a computation ends in a solver.

Abstract: A code is described by three numbers: its physical qubits, its logical qubits, and the smallest error it cannot detect, counted in qubits. The first two are linear algebra. The third, the distance, is an optimum over an exponentially large set, read off a solver whose answer carries no certificate. Whether a code's parameters can be checked uniformly across codes, with a certificate of proved size, is open. Here we show that they can, by moving the unit of work from the code to a \emph{certificate}, a short object, either a list, a pairing or a symbolic instance, whose correctness the kernel of the \Lean\ proof assistant decides by computation. The kernel is the small core that checks each step. The certificate's size is itself a theorem: its enumeration form lists the vectors of weight below the distance $d$, $\sum_{j<d}\binom{n}{j}$ of them, a polynomial in the code's length $n$ at fixed distance $d$. No bound uniform over codes in both $n$ and $d$ is smaller. For product families the certificate is smaller still, the bound being proved once, symbolically. Deciding that a vector lies outside the row space of the check matrix, the set of sums of its rows, becomes one matrix-vector product and one inner product. On an eighteen-qubit toric code, a surface code closed into a torus, the whole-file check of that decision falls from 42\,s to 9\,s. Eleven code families and thirty-nine parameter sets follow, the widest at 1872 qubits. Nothing beyond the three standard axioms of \Lean's logic is trusted, though the generator that writes a code's checks stops at twenty qubits, and the lower bound for the 144-qubit code is imported rather than proved here. A distance becomes checkable rather than believed, and the same move applies wherever else a computation ends in a solver.

</details>


### [48] [Two-center Dirac equation in a Gaussian basis set](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03217)
*Dávid Ferenc*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A Gaussian-product basis set exploiting axial symmetry solves the two-center Dirac equation, yielding H₂⁺ ground-state energies at 10⁻¹⁸ relative precision and enabling the first evaluation of the zero-potential one-loop self-energy in Feynman and Coulomb gauges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-precision relativistic and QED calculations for diatomic systems require an efficient, symmetry-adapted representation of the two-center Dirac equation. A suitable basis should achieve extreme numerical accuracy and remain analytically tractable to support future QED corrections.

Method: A Gaussian-product basis set tailored to the axial symmetry of diatomic systems is used to solve the two-center Dirac equation. The basis properties permit an analytic Fourier transform of the wave function. This framework is then applied to compute the zero-potential contribution to the one-loop self-energy for H₂⁺ in both Feynman and Coulomb gauges.

Result: For the H₂⁺ ground state at internuclear distance R = 2.0 a₀, a relativistic energy is obtained with relative precision at the 10⁻¹⁸ level. The zero-potential one-loop self-energy is evaluated for the first time for H₂⁺ in Feynman and Coulomb gauges.

Conclusion: Although the zero-potential self-energy contribution is not separately gauge invariant, its calculation demonstrates proof of principle for applying the present framework to prospective QED calculations in diatomic systems.

Abstract: A method for solving the two-center Dirac equation using a Gaussian-product basis set tailored to the axial symmetry of diatomic systems is presented. For the ground state of H$_2^+$ at an internuclear distance $R=2.0~a_0$, we obtain a relativistic energy with relative precision at the $10^{-18}$ level. The properties of the basis set allow for an analytic Fourier transform of the wave function. As a first application we evaluate, for the first time, the zero-potential contribution to the one-loop self-energy for H$_2^+$ in Feynman and Coulomb gauges. Although this contribution is not separately gauge invariant, its calculation provides a proof of principle for the use of the present framework in prospective QED calculations.

</details>


### [49] [Covertness as a resource constraint in quantum target sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03233)
*Yu Yang,Fengyu Guan,Shiping Guo,Ruifeng Liu,Pei Zhang,Fuli Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper builds a unified framework linking covert target discrimination to quantum-enhanced parameter estimation, deriving covertness-constrained bounds on probe energy and the ultimate precision of target-reflectivity estimation for GCS and TMSV probes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum covert target sensing must detect low-reflectivity targets in a thermal environment without being discovered, so the covertness requirement limits admissible probe energy and creates a trade-off with sensing performance. However, how this resource constraint governs the ultimate estimation precision of the target parameter remains largely unexplored.

Method: The authors establish a unified framework connecting covert target discrimination with quantum-enhanced parameter estimation. They derive perturbative ε-covertness-induced bounds on probe energy, define the covertness-constrained quantum Fisher information (CCQFI) for target-reflectivity estimation, and evaluate it for Gaussian-distributed coherent-state (GCS) and two-mode squeezed vacuum (TMSV) probes, assessing attainability with photon counting and homodyne detection.

Result: The admissible energy window narrows as O(√(ε/M)). The CCQFIs of GCS and TMSV probes scale as O(ε) and O(M)+O(√(εM))+O(ε), respectively. In the strong covertness regime ε→0, the GCS CCQFI vanishes while the TMSV retains a finite O(M) contribution, showing a pronounced metrological advantage. Photon counting saturates the GCS CCQFI, whereas homodyne detection achieves higher attainability for the TMSV probe.

Conclusion: Covertness acts as a quantitative resource constraint governing the metrological limits of quantum target sensing, with TMSV probes offering a clear advantage over GCS probes under strong covertness.

Abstract: Quantum covert target sensing aims to detect low-reflectivity targets embedded in the thermal environment while avoiding discovery by an adversary. This covertness requirement constrains the admissible probe energy, thereby imposing a trade-off between covertness and sensing performance. However, how this resource constraint determines the ultimate estimation precision of the target parameter remains largely unexplored. To fill this gap, we establish a unified framework connecting covert target discrimination with quantum-enhanced parameter estimation. We first present perturbative $ε$-covertness-induced bounds on the probe energy, which yield an admissible energy window narrowing as $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{ε/M})$. Moreover, we introduce the covertness-constrained quantum Fisher information (CCQFI) to quantify the ultimate precision of target-reflectivity estimation and assess its attainability with specific measurements. For Gaussian-distributed coherent-state (GCS) and two-mode squeezed vacuum (TMSV) probes, their CCQFIs scale as $\mathcal{O}(ε)$ and $\mathcal{O}(M)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{εM})+\mathcal{O}(ε)$, respectively. In particular, in the strong covertness regime $ε\to0$, the GCS CCQFI vanishes, whereas the TMSV retains a finite $\mathcal{O}(M)$ contribution exhibiting a pronounced metrological advantage. Besides, photon counting saturates the GCS CCQFI, whereas homodyne detection achieves a higher attainability for the TMSV probe. Our work identifies covertness as a quantitative resource constraint governing the metrological limits of quantum target sensing.

</details>


### [50] [Two-body control of noiseless subsystems with mixed $SU(3)$ representations lifts teleportation above the classical fidelity limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03235)
*Otavio A. D. Molitor,Sergii Strelchuk,Michał Studziński*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves semi-universality for a system of r fundamental and s dual qutrits subject to collective SU(3) noise, showing that adjacent exchanges and a singlet-projector interaction generate arbitrary special unitary gates on noiseless subsystems via finite pulse sequences, and applies the encoding to qutrit teleportation under correlated Weyl noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collective noise leaves certain degrees of freedom (noiseless subsystems) unaffected, which can store quantum information, but processing this information requires physically realizable logical gates. The paper addresses whether a universal gate set can be generated for qutrits under collective SU(3) noise.

Method: The authors consider r fundamental qutrits transforming under U in SU(3) and s dual qutrits transforming under the complex conjugate U-bar, with collective action R_{r,s}(U) = U^{⊗r} ⊗ U-bar^{⊗s}. They use the image of the walled Brauer algebra and Lie-algebra arguments to show that adjacent exchanges within each qutrit type plus one singlet-projector interaction between the two types generate any independently chosen family of special unitary gates on the noiseless subsystems via a finite pulse sequence.
