They illustrate with a numerically optimized 24-pulse sequence for three fundamental qutrits and one dual qutrit, then apply the encoding and logical corrections to qutrit teleportation under correlated Weyl noise.

Result: For every r,s ≥ 1, adjacent exchanges within each qutrit type and one singlet-projector interaction between the two types generate arbitrary special unitary gates on the noiseless subsystems via a finite pulse sequence, establishing semi-universality. A numerically optimized 24-pulse sequence is demonstrated for three fundamental qutrits and one dual qutrit. For qutrit teleportation under correlated Weyl noise, under explicit assumptions on channel composition and retained trials (allowing baseline implementation errors), noise ranges are identified where encoded teleportation exceeds the classical fidelity bound of 1/2 while the unencoded channel is entanglement breaking.

Conclusion: The system of fundamental and dual qutrits under collective SU(3) noise is semi-universal for encoding and processing quantum information, with an explicit 24-pulse sequence example. The encoding enables qutrit teleportation that outperforms the classical fidelity bound of 1/2 within specific noise ranges where the unencoded channel is entanglement breaking; fidelity on accepted trials is analyzed separately from the transmission costs of additional physical qutrits.

Abstract: Collective noise can leave degrees of freedom unaffected, allowing them to store quantum information. Processing this information also requires physically available logical gates. We consider $r$ fundamental qutrits transforming under $U\in SU(3)$ and $s$ dual qutrits transforming under its complex conjugate $\overline U$, with collective action $R_{r,s}(U)=U^{\otimes r}\otimes\overline U^{\otimes s}$. Using the image of the walled Brauer algebra and Lie-algebra arguments, we show that adjacent exchanges within each qutrit type and one singlet-projector interaction between the two types generate any independently chosen family of special unitary gates on the noiseless subsystems by a finite pulse sequence, for every $r,s\geq1$. This establishes semi-universality of this system. We illustrate the result with a numerically optimised sequence of 24 pulses for three fundamental qutrits and one dual qutrit. We then use the encoding and available logical corrections for qutrit teleportation under correlated Weyl noise. Under explicit assumptions on channel composition and which trials are retained, and allowing for baseline implementation errors, we identify noise ranges in which encoded teleportation exceeds the classical fidelity bound of $1/2$, while the unencoded teleportation channel is entanglement breaking. We analyse fidelity on accepted trials separately from the transmission costs of the additional physical qutrits.

</details>


### [51] [Stimulated Parametric Down-Conversion: From Foundational Coherence to Structured Light Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03241)
*M. F. Z. Arruda,M. G. Damaceno,G. H. dos Santos,R. Medeiros de Araújo,N. Rubiano da Silva,S. P. Walborn,P. H. Souto Ribeiro*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This review consolidates the transverse spatial physics of stimulated parametric down-conversion (StimPDC), showing that a single relation—idler amplitude as the pointwise product of the pump field with the complex conjugate of the seed—unifies image/coherence transfer, phase conjugation, orbital angular momentum, and vector vortex beam effects, and presents StimPDC as a metrological instrument extending to the quantum regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Seeding one input port of parametric down-conversion with a coherent field leaves the Hamiltonian, phase-matching function, and two-photon amplitude unchanged while amplifying outputs to levels detectable by classical detectors. Results on its transverse spatial physics have been scattered across separate literatures, motivating a unified treatment.

Method: The authors derive and apply a single governing relation in which the idler amplitude equals the pointwise product of the pump field and the complex conjugate of the seed. They use this relation to reinterpret and organize existing experimental and theoretical results, and to treat StimPDC as an instrument (e.g., stimulated emission tomography, advanced-wave picture) rather than merely a process to be studied.

Result: The single relation reproduces and connects image and coherence transfer experiments of the 1990s, phase conjugation and wavefront correction, conservation and conjugation of orbital angular momentum, radial mode and geometric intensity pattern generation, and processing of vector vortex beams with spatially varying polarization. As an instrument, StimPDC enables stimulated emission tomography, experimental realization of Klyshko's advanced-wave picture, and transfer of partial coherence including conservation of the twist phase of twisted Gaussian Schell-model beams.

Conclusion: StimPDC's transverse spatial behavior is unified by one idler–pump–seed relation, bridging classical-detector-accessible optics and quantum optics. The review closes by addressing the quantum regime—reached as the seed approaches the single-photon level or the idler serves as a herald—and by assessing open problems and prospective applications.

Abstract: Stimulated parametric down-conversion (StimPDC) is the seeded version of the process that underlies much of photonic quantum information. Replacing the vacuum at one input port with a coherent seed leaves the interaction Hamiltonian, the phase-matching function and the two-photon amplitude unchanged, while raising the output to levels accessible with ordinary classical detectors. This review treats the transverse spatial physics of that process. We show that a single relation, in which the idler amplitude is the pointwise product of the pump field with the complex conjugate of the seed, organizes results usually presented separately: the image and coherence transfer experiments of the 1990s; phase conjugation and its use for wavefront correction; the conservation and conjugation of orbital angular momentum, together with the generation of radial modes and geometric intensity patterns; and the processing of vector vortex beams carrying spatially varying polarization. We then consider StimPDC as an instrument rather than as an object of study, covering stimulated emission tomography, the experimental realization of Klyshko's advanced-wave picture, and the transfer of partial coherence, including the conservation of the twist phase of twisted Gaussian Schell-model beams. We close with the quantum regime, reached when the seed approaches the single-photon level or the idler is used as a herald, and with an assessment of open problems and prospective applications.

</details>


### [52] [Pulse-resolved post-generation phase control of squeezed light at 93 MHz with a bulk lithium-niobate modulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03257)
*Edoardo Suerra,Igor Vasiljevic,Sebastiano Corli,Enrico Prati,Simone Cialdi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work demonstrates pulse-resolved, post-generation phase control of squeezed-vacuum states at 93 MHz using a low-loss bulk lithium-niobate electro-optic modulator driven by a commercial broadband RF amplifier, achieving a maximum differential phase excursion of 1.93 rad and enabling independent phase control of consecutive temporal modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-domain multiplexing of continuous-variable quantum states enables scalable photonic quantum information processing, where individual temporal modes must be individually manipulated within a common optical path. Achieving pulse-level phase control after state generation, without custom high-voltage electronics, is a key building block for programmable manipulation of time-multiplexed quantum states.

Method: A low-loss bulk lithium-niobate electro-optic modulator is driven synchronously with the optical pulse train at 93 MHz using a broadband RF power amplifier. Instead of dedicated custom high-voltage electronics, the driving architecture exploits low-cost commercial RF components and the impedance mismatch between the RF chain and the capacitive crystal load, operating above the piezoelectric-resonance region of lithium niobate to obtain large voltage excursions. An interferometric calibration measures the differential phase excursion. The modulator is applied to a 1035 nm squeezed-vacuum field from a synchronously pumped optical parametric oscillator, and the output is characterized via time-resolved balanced homodyne detection with a fixed local-oscillator phase while the squeezed-state phase is varied pulse by pulse.

Result: The interferometric calibration yields a maximum differential phase excursion of 1.93 rad. The reconstructed phase-dependent quadrature variance scan agrees with an equivalent measurement obtained by varying the local-oscillator phase with a piezoelectric actuator. The phase of consecutive temporal modes is individually controlled.

Conclusion: Post-generation quantum-state phase control at the individual-pulse level is demonstrated, providing an electro-optic building block for programmable manipulation of time-multiplexed quantum states in recirculating optical architectures.

Abstract: Time-domain multiplexing of continuous-variable quantum states provides a scalable approach to photonic quantum information processing, in which individual temporal modes can be sequentially manipulated within a common optical path. Here we demonstrate pulse-resolved post-generation phase control of squeezed-vacuum states at a repetition rate of 93 MHz using a low-loss bulk lithium-niobate electro-optic modulator driven by a broadband RF power amplifier. The driving architecture exploits low-cost, commercially available RF components and the impedance mismatch between the RF chain and the capacitive crystal load, rather than relying on dedicated custom high-voltage electronics, to obtain large voltage excursions on the modulator while operating above the piezoelectric-resonance region of lithium niobate. The modulator is driven synchronously with the optical pulse train, enabling the phase of consecutive temporal modes to be individually controlled. An interferometric calibration yields a maximum differential phase excursion of 1.93 rad. We apply the modulator to a 1035 nm squeezed-vacuum field generated by a synchronously pumped optical parametric oscillator and characterize the output using time-resolved balanced homodyne detection. With the local-oscillator phase kept fixed, the phase of the squeezed state is varied from pulse to pulse, and the corresponding phase-dependent quadrature variance is reconstructed. The resulting quadrature scan agrees with an equivalent measurement obtained by varying the local-oscillator phase with a piezoelectric actuator. These results demonstrate post-generation quantum-state phase control at the individual-pulse level and provide an electro-optic building block for programmable manipulation of time-multiplexed quantum states in recirculating optical architectures.

</details>


### [53] [Compiling Together: High-Throughput Distributed Quantum Computing via Multi-Compilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03260)
*Yipei Liu,Sen Zhang,Zebo Yang,Lei Yang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes multi-compilation execution for distributed quantum computing, where logically equivalent but resource-differently-compiled circuit variants are run concurrently and their shots pooled under per-link Bell-pair budgets, formulated as CMA (NP-hard) and solved via DP, AppDP, MILP, and Effi, raising throughput 2-4.5x in simulation and up to 93% fidelity on hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DQC scales qubits by linking QPUs, but inter-QPU links generate Bell pairs at finite rates orders of magnitude slower than local gates, and every remote gate consumes a Bell pair. Since programs run repeatedly, the slowest/busiest link caps shot throughput. Existing DQC compilers emit a single implementation per circuit, leaving other links idle, so throughput is limited by the busiest link.

Method: The authors observe that alternative compilations of the same circuit are logically equivalent yet stress different links. They execute such compilations concurrently and pool their samples, converting idle Bell pairs into extra shots. They formalize joint compilation selection and shot allocation under per-link Bell-pair capacities as Candidate-Constrained Max-Shot Allocation (CMA), prove it NP-hard, and solve it with a dynamic program (DP), its approximate variant AppDP, a compact MILP, and a greedy heuristic Effi.

Result: In simulation on six-QPU networks, multi-compilation raises throughput by 2-4.5x over single compilation and improves output fidelity under equal Bell-pair budgets. On hardware, it increases measured fidelity by up to 93% and reaches single-compilation fidelity in up to 10x less time. Scaling to 36 QPUs and 144-qubit circuits, MILP improves throughput over single compilation by 76.8% on average across 48 configurations, while Effi allocates in milliseconds.

Conclusion: Concurrent execution of logically equivalent, link-diverse compilations with pooled sampling effectively mitigates link-rate bottlenecks in DQC. The CMA formulation and its DP/AppDP/MILP/Effi solvers yield substantial throughput and fidelity gains at scale, with Effi providing millisecond allocation for large networks.

Abstract: Quantum computing is a promising paradigm for problems that are challenging for classical machines, but realizing that promise requires far more qubits than a single processor can offer. Distributed quantum computing (DQC) scales out by connecting multiple quantum processing units (QPUs), at the cost of making entanglement the scarce resource: every remote gate consumes a Bell pair, and inter-QPU links generate Bell pairs at finite rates orders of magnitude slower than local gates. Since quantum programs are executed repeatedly, this rate bounds how fast shots complete and thus how fast results are obtained. Existing DQC compilers emit a single implementation per circuit, so throughput is capped by its busiest link while other links stay idle. We observe that alternative compilations of the same circuit are logically equivalent yet stress different links; executing them concurrently and pooling their samples converts idle Bell pairs into additional shots. We formulate the joint selection of compilations and allocation of shots under per-link Bell-pair capacities as Candidate-Constrained Max-Shot Allocation (CMA), prove it NP-hard, and solve it with a dynamic program and its approximate variant AppDP, a compact MILP, and a greedy heuristic Effi. In simulation on six-QPU networks, multi-compilation raises throughput by 2-4.5* over the single compilation and correspondingly improves output fidelity under equal Bell-pair budgets. On hardware, it increases measured fidelity by up to 93% and reaches the single-compilation fidelity in up to 10* less time. Scaling to 36 QPUs and 144-qubit circuits, MILP improves throughput over the single compilation by 76.8% on average across 48 configurations while Effi allocates in milliseconds.

</details>


### [54] [Pathway-resolved analysis of internal conversion enabled by Gaussian boson sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03271)
*Mikhail Roiz,Rashid Valiev,Jan-Lucas Eickmann,Theo Kurten,Benjamin Brecht,Michael Stefszky,Christine Silberhorn*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives a Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS)-compatible mapping for the internal conversion rate constant k_IC, incorporating Duschinsky rotations and Herzberg–Teller effects, and shows that GBS photon-number patterns encode non-radiative pathways, enabling pathway-resolved analysis of molecular decay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GBS has demonstrated strong evidence of quantum advantage, motivating the search for real-world applications. Such applications require mapping physical problems onto the native GBS output: photon-number patterns drawn from a highly non-trivial probability distribution.

Method: The authors derive a GBS-compatible mapping for the internal conversion rate constant k_IC that incorporates two physical effects, Duschinsky rotations and the Herzberg–Teller contribution. They validate the framework via sampling simulations on dibenzoterrylene, reproducing reported k_IC values, and analyze the resulting photon-number patterns by grouping them into physically motivated observables.

Result: Simulations on dibenzoterrylene reproduce reported k_IC values. The photon-number patterns encode the vibrational mode combinations responsible for internal conversion (non-radiative pathways), enabling k_IC analysis from individual modes and mode families to recurring vibrational configurations shared across pathways. Duschinsky rotations raise the fraction of k_IC associated with correlated vibrational pathways from 46% to 86%, and the mode combinations with the largest rate contributions are identified.

Conclusion: The pathway-resolved insights from GBS output can guide targeted molecular modifications, such as selective deuteration and bond substitution, to control non-radiative decay.

Abstract: Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS) has provided strong evidence of quantum advantage, motivating the search for real-world applications. Such applications rely on mapping physical problems to the native GBS output: photon-number patterns sampled from a highly non-trivial probability distribution. Here we derive a GBS-compatible mapping for the rate constant $k_\mathrm{IC}$ of internal conversion (IC), incorporating two important physical effects -- Duschinsky rotations and Herzberg--Teller contribution. We validate this framework using sampling \textit{simulations} of dibenzoterrylene, reproducing reported values of $k_\mathrm{IC}$. Beyond $k_\mathrm{IC}$ reconstruction, we show that the photon-number patterns of GBS encode the vibrational mode combinations responsible for the IC process, which we refer to as non-radiative pathways. This pathway-resolved data can then be grouped in different ways to extract multiple physically motivated observables. In particular, it enables $k_\mathrm{IC}$ analysis at several levels, from individual modes and mode family contributions to recurring vibrational configurations shared by multiple pathways. We find that Duschinsky rotations increase the fraction of $k_\mathrm{IC}$ associated with correlated vibrational pathways from 46% to 86%, and identify the mode combinations with the largest rate contributions. These pathway-resolved insights can guide targeted molecular modifications, including selective deuteration and bond substitution, to control non-radiative decay.

</details>


### [55] [Pricing of graph-based quantum portfolios from first principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03279)
*Daniel J. Spencer,Bin Cheng,Dinh-Long Vu,Kishor Bharti,Alexey V. Gorshkov,Patrick Rebentrost*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work merges quantum finance with graph theory and contextuality, showing that holding exclusive non-commuting quantum assets can outperform classical assets and that a "quantum market" built on nonlocal games (e.g., CHSH) has well-defined financial structures, with fair prices bounded by graph invariants like the independence number and the Lovász theta number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical valuation of strategic games, bets, and uncertain-cashflow investments relies on probabilistic models and expectation values. The paper extends this framework to settings where assets are non-commuting operators and uncertainty is governed by quantum states, aiming to understand how a financial system would operate in a quantum-technology world.

Method: The authors combine quantum finance with a graph-theoretic framework and contextuality ideas. They define exclusive quantum assets, construct a "quantum market" based on nonlocal games such as the CHSH game, and derive fair-price upper bounds using graph-theoretic quantities, including the independence number and the Lovász theta number.

Result: Holding exclusive quantum assets can provide advantages over holding classical assets. A market based on nonlocal games like CHSH can function as a market with well-defined financial structures, and fair prices of quantum assets admit upper bounds expressible via graph-theoretic invariants (independence number, Lovász theta number).

Conclusion: The findings clarify what a financial system built on non-commuting assets might look like in a world permeated by quantum technology, extending classical valuation theory into the quantum domain.

Abstract: A central topic in economics and finance is the valuation of strategic games and bets, of investments providing uncertain future cashflows, and of other asset-like structures and contracts. Often, such valuation involves a probabilistic model, random variables, and computing expectation values of complex payoffs. Extending beyond this classical framework, a scenario emerges where assets are non-commuting operators and the inherent uncertainty is described by quantum states. In this work, we combine such notions of quantum finance with a graph-theoretic framework and ideas from contextuality. We discuss exclusive quantum assets and introduce scenarios when holding such assets can provide advantages over holding classical assets. In particular, we consider a "quantum market" based on nonlocal games such as the CHSH game and demonstrate that this setting can function as a market with well-defined financial structures. We provide upper bounds on the fair prices of the quantum assets based on graph-theoretic quantities like the independence number and the Lovász theta number. Our insights into markets of non-commuting assets help to elucidate what a financial system in a world filled with quantum technology could look like.

</details>


### [56] [Efficiently and Reliably Measuring Information Processing Capacity in Dynamical Systems via Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03281)
*Alessio Benavoli,Felix Binder,Cangxiong Chen*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Kernel-IPC (KIPC), a kernel-based framework that computes the total Information Processing Capacity (IPC) of a dynamical system without explicitly enumerating the infinite orthogonal polynomial basis, overcoming the combinatorial complexity and degree truncation of standard IPC, and provides a null-hypothesis test for statistical comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard IPC, a widely used system-agnostic metric for computational capability in quantum reservoir computing, has severe computational limitations: it requires linear regression over an in principle infinite family of orthogonal polynomial functions of delayed inputs, forcing a finite truncation at a chosen maximum degree, and its computational complexity grows combinatorially with maximum degree and delay.

Method: The authors propose Kernel-IPC (KIPC), a scalable framework that leverages kernel methods to compute the total IPC without explicitly enumerating basis functions, thereby accounting for the full infinite basis without degree truncation. They also design a null-hypothesis test for statistically comparing the KIPC of two dynamical systems.

Result: KIPC is validated on an echo state network and demonstrated to be useful for quantum reservoir computing on a disordered spin chain.

Conclusion: KIPC provides a scalable, truncation-free approach to quantify total information processing capacity, with statistical comparison capability, and is applicable to both classical (echo state network) and quantum reservoir computing systems.

Abstract: Information Processing Capacity (IPC) is a powerful system-agnostic metric for quantifying a dynamical system's computational capability and is widely used in quantum reservoir computing. However, standard IPC suffers from severe computational limitations. Computing IPC involves estimating how well the system can reconstruct, via linear regression, an (in principle) infinite family of orthogonal polynomial functions of delayed inputs. In practice, this evaluation must be made finite, so we need only to consider polynomials up to a chosen maximum degree. Moreover, the computational complexity for computing IPC grows combinatorially with the considered maximum degree and delay. Here, we introduce Kernel-IPC (KIPC), a scalable framework that uses kernel methods to compute the total IPC without explicitly enumerating basis functions. This allows us to account for the full (infinite) basis (without degree truncation). We also provide a null-hypothesis test for statistically comparing the KIPC of two dynamical systems. We validate KIPC on an echo state network and demonstrate its utility for quantum reservoir computing on a disordered spin chain.

</details>


### [57] [Hamiltonian Eigenvalue Transformation by Tridiagonal Gadgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03295)
*Arthur Braida,Joseph Cunningham,Jérémie Roland*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A device implementing a local Hamiltonian H cannot directly realize a polynomial P(H), since P(H) is generally non-local. The paper encodes P(H) exactly (to arbitrary accuracy) as the action of a single time-independent local Hamiltonian on an invariant subspace, using short ancilla chains whose isolated eigenvalues vanish to order 2m, enabling term-by-term polynomial synthesis. This yields locality two greater than H, O(l² + log²(1/ε)) ancillas, and polynomial energy scales, with applications to eigenstate filtering and adiabatic optimisation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analog devices naturally implement local Hamiltonians H, but a polynomial P(H) is in general non-local and thus cannot be implemented directly by such a device. The goal is to realise P(H) as a single time-independent local Hamiltonian acting on an invariant subspace, to bridge analog Hamiltonians and algorithmic operations (e.g. filters and driver replacement) without the sequential oracle-call cost of the circuit model.

Method: Short chains of ancilla qubits are attached to H. A chain of 2m sites possesses a unique isolated eigenvalue that is an analytic function of the input and vanishes to order exactly 2m, because the input must traverse the chain and return before shifting the far-end energy. Chains of different lengths form a triangular family, and a weighted sum reproduces a prescribed polynomial term by term; even chains give the even part, odd chains the odd part. Uniqueness, analyticity and uniform coefficient bounds are proven for inputs of norm below any r < 1. For filtering, the exact eigenvalue branch of the two-site chain is iterated k times, and for adiabatic optimisation with a rank-one driver, the non-local driver is replaced using O(log n) ancillas and O(log n)-body terms.

Result: P(H) is realised, to any prescribed accuracy, as one time-independent local Hamiltonian of locality two more than that of H, at a cost of O(l² + log²(1/ε)) ancillas and comparable energy scale—versus degree 2l in 2l sequential oracle calls in the circuit model. The iterated two-site-chain filter on k+1 ancilla qubits has error decaying geometrically in k at an energy scale polynomial in the inverse passband width and independent of k, with locality growing by one per stage. The rank-one-driver replacement uses O(log n) ancillas, O(log n)-body terms, and energy scale polynomial in n. Simulations including every ancilla reproduce the ideal algorithm's spectrum.

Conclusion: Non-local polynomial transforms of a local Hamiltonian can be implemented exactly as a single time-independent local Hamiltonian on an explicitly described invariant subspace via ancilla chains forming a triangular family. This framework supports energy-efficient eigenstate filtering (geometric error decay, k-independent polynomial energy scale) and replaces non-local rank-one drivers in adiabatic optimisation with local, few-body Hamiltonians. Full simulations including all ancillas reproduce the ideal algorithm's spectrum.

Abstract: An analog device implements a local Hamiltonian H, but a polynomial P (H) is in general not local, so the device cannot implement it. We carry P (H), to any prescribed accuracy, as the action of a single time-independent local Hamiltonian on an explicitly described invariant subspace. Short chains of ancilla qubits are attached to H. A chain of 2m sites has a unique isolated eigenvalue that is an analytic function of the input vanishing to order exactly 2m, because the input must cross the chain and come back before it can shift the energy at the far end. Chains of different lengths therefore form a triangular family, and a weighted sum of them reproduces a prescribed polynomial term by term. Even chains give the even part and odd chains the odd part, so any polynomial is reached. We prove uniqueness, analyticity and coefficient bounds uniform in the chain length for inputs H of norm below any r < 1. Where the circuit model pays degree 2l in 2l sequential oracle calls, the result here is one Hamiltonian of locality two more than that of H, which pays the degree in O(l^2 + log2^(1/eps)) ancillas and in energy scale. As an application we filter a marked eigenstate of a local Hamiltonian. Composing a synthesised square along the Chebyshev doubling identity works, but its energy scale grows quasi-polynomially with the degree. Iterating instead the exact eigenvalue branch of the two-site chain k times gives a filter on k + 1 ancilla qubits whose error decays geometrically in k, at an energy scale polynomial in the inverse passband width and independent of k, the locality growing by one per stage. In adiabatic optimisation with a rank-one driver, the construction replaces that non-local driver, by a Hamiltonian with O(log n) ancillas and O(log n)- body terms, at an energy scale polynomial in n. Simulations including every ancilla reproduce the spectrum of the ideal algorithm.

</details>


### [58] [Free fermionic black holes as polarised mirrors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03304)
*Maureen Krumtünger,Maxwell West*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies information scrambling in a random free fermionic model of black hole dynamics, finding a "polarised mirror" behaviour governed by representation-theoretic structure, where low-degree information is retained and high-degree information reflected rapidly, preventing faithful recovery until near-complete evaporation unless a fixed Clifford unitary is added, which restores reflectivity and can outperform Haar-random black holes by ~37% fewer emitted qubits in the high-fidelity limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how fast quantum systems scramble information is central to quantum information theory and quantum algorithm efficiency. Hayden and Preskill famously showed that Haar-random black hole dynamics make old black holes act as near-instantaneous mirrors. However, it is unclear which aspects of this mirror behaviour survive beyond the idealized Haar-random setting, motivating the study of more physically realistic scrambling models.

Method: The authors replace Haar-random unitary dynamics with a random free fermionic model of black hole dynamics and analyze information release using representation-theoretic structure, specifically decomposition into fermionic-degree irreps. They also augment the dynamics with a fixed Clifford unitary and compare recovery efficiency against the Haar-random benchmark using the ensemble frame potential.

Result: The black hole behaves as a "polarised mirror": information in low fermionic-degree irreps is retained while information in high fermionic-degree irreps is reflected much faster. Faithful recovery of infalling information is impossible until all but a vanishing fraction of the black hole has evaporated. Adding a fixed Clifford unitary restores reflective behaviour even though the frame potential is unchanged and far above its Haar value. In the high-fidelity limit, recovering a single-qubit message requires asymptotically ~37% fewer emitted qubits than Haar-random counterparts.

Conclusion: Beyond the Haar-random setting, black hole mirror behaviour is more nuanced: free fermionic dynamics yield a polarised mirror that retains low-degree information and delays faithful recovery, but a fixed Clifford augmentation can restore and even surpass Haar-random performance, achieving a ~37% reduction in emitted qubits needed for single-qubit recovery.

Abstract: Understanding the speed with which various classes of quantum systems can scramble information is a key goal of quantum information theory, with relevance to both fundamental physics and the study of the efficiency of quantum algorithms. Famously, such understanding enabled Hayden and Preskill to show that, under the assumption that black hole dynamics may be modelled by a Haar-random unitary, old black holes act as ''mirrors'', reflecting infalling quantum information back out again effectively instantaneously. In this work, we investigate which aspects of this mirror behaviour survive beyond the Haar-random setting by considering a random free fermionic model of black hole dynamics. We find that the rate of information release is governed by the underlying representation-theoretic structure; such a black hole acts as a ''polarised mirror'', retaining information encoded in low fermionic-degree irreps while reflecting information encoded in high fermionic-degree irreps much more rapidly. As a result of the retention of low-degree information, we show that faithful recovery of the infalling quantum information is impossible until all but a vanishing fraction of the black hole has evaporated. However, augmenting the dynamics with a fixed Clifford unitary can restore their reflective behaviour, even though the ensemble's frame potential remains unchanged and far above its Haar value. Remarkably, we find that such black holes can outperform their fully Haar-random counterparts: in the high-fidelity limit, recovering a single-qubit message requires asymptotically approximately $37\%$ fewer emitted qubits.

</details>


### [59] [Learning Noise-Robust Stabilizer Structure via Bell Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03307)
*Letian Tang,Thomas Steckmann*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper shows that the ideal stabilizer group of a low-magic state remains encoded in its Pauli-noisy image as the set of Pauli operators under which the state is invariant, and provides Bell-sampling algorithms that learn this group in polynomial or controlled-exponential sample/runtime regimes via a finder-verifier scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bell sampling efficiently learns the stabilizer group and stabilizer nullity of pure low-magic states, but real devices prepare noisy states modeled as Pauli channels, which generically lack exact Pauli stabilizers. Existing mixed-state structure learners require exact stabilizers, while agnostic tomography scales exponentially in the nullity t. This work aims to bridge that gap.

Method: The authors model noisy states as Pauli channels acting on ideal states and observe that the ideal stabilizer group survives as the invariance group of the noisy state. They design Bell-sampling algorithms within a finder-verifier scheme that extracts stabilizer structure from partially corrupted sample batches and certifies proposed group updates, overcoming the loss of group structure in noisy samples.

Result: For weak global depolarizing noise, both sample and runtime complexity are poly(n,t,1/epsilon) when the channel's total error probability is of order epsilon n/t or less. Up to a constant total error probability (~0.16), samples stay polynomial while classical processing costs 2^t; for any error probability below one, a 4^t overhead suffices. Arbitrary Pauli channels follow the same three regimes with more restrictive thresholds.

Conclusion: For weak Pauli noise, the proposed algorithms close much of the gap between agnostic learners such as stabilizer bootstrapping and polynomial pure-state algorithms, enabling efficient learning of stabilizer structure from noisy low-magic states.

Abstract: Bell sampling efficiently learns the stabilizer group, and hence the stabilizer nullity, of pure states with little magic, a primitive underlying tomography and certification of low-magic states. Real devices, however, only prepare noisy images of the target, which we model as a Pauli channel acting on the ideal state; the resulting noisy state generically has no exact Pauli stabilizers. Existing structure learners for mixed states rely on such exact stabilizers. Agnostic tomography does not, but its guarantees scale exponentially in the nullity $t$. We show that the ideal stabilizer group remains encoded in the noisy state as the set of Pauli operators under which it is invariant. We give Bell-sampling algorithms that learn the group to accuracy $\varepsilon$ for states of nullity at most $t$. The difficulty is that noisy samples break the group structure pure-state learners rely on, so more samples can erase structure rather than reveal it. We resolve this with a finder-verifier scheme that extracts stabilizer structure from partially corrupted sample batches while certifying proposed updates. For sufficiently weak global depolarizing noise, both samples and runtime are $\mathrm{poly}(n,t,1/\varepsilon)$ when the channel's total error probability is at most of order $\varepsilon n/t$. Up to a constant total error probability ($\approx 0.16$), samples remain polynomial while classical processing costs $2^t$, and for any error probability below one a $4^t$ overhead suffices. Arbitrary Pauli channels exhibit the same three regimes, with more restrictive thresholds. For weak Pauli noise, this closes much of the gap between agnostic learners such as stabilizer bootstrapping and polynomial pure-state algorithms.

</details>


### [60] [Quantum Key Distribution with Entanglement-Swapped Photons from a Quantum Emitter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03351)
*Michele B. Rota,Francesco Basso Basset,Alessandro Laneve,Francesco Salusti,Nicolas Claro-Rodriguez,Giuseppe Ronco,Mattia Beccaceci,Tobias M. Krieger,Quirin Buchinger,Saimon F. Covre da Silva,Sandra Stroj,Mariia Gumberidze,Vladyslav Usenko,Sven Höfling,Tobias Huber-Loyola,Mario A. Usuga Castaneda,Armando Rastelli,Klaus D. Jöns,Rinaldo Trotta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Demonstration of source-independent QKD over entanglement-swapped photons using a deterministic GaAs quantum dot source, enabling secret key exchange via temporal post-selection as a step toward quantum repeater-based long-distance QKD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QKD is a fundamental quantum network functionality, but extending its range requires quantum repeaters—a technology still under development and not yet demonstrated together with quantum cryptographic protocols. This work tests whether elementary repeater operation (entanglement swapping) can support secret key distribution.

Method: The authors use a state-of-the-art deterministic photon source: a strain-tuned GaAs quantum dot embedded in a photonic cavity. They implement a source-independent QKD protocol between two nodes using entanglement-swapped photons, and benchmark the system, applying high-resolution temporal post-selection to enable secure key exchange.

Result: Secure key exchange is enabled through high-resolution temporal post-selection, with further rate improvements expected from realistic device and protocol optimizations.

Conclusion: Because the approach is compatible with a wide range of quantum memory platforms, it represents a significant step toward long-distance quantum-safe communication based on viable quantum repeater schemes.

Abstract: Quantum key distribution (QKD) is a fundamental functionality of quantum networks. Extending its range requires quantum repeaters, a technology still under development and not yet demonstrated in combination with quantum cryptographic protocols. Here, we address the elementary operation of a viable quantum repeater scheme by evaluating whether correlated photons distributed via entanglement swapping can support secret key distribution. We employ a state-of-the-art deterministic photon source based on a strain-tuned GaAs quantum dot embedded in a photonic cavity and implement a source-independent QKD protocol between two nodes using entanglement-swapped photons. We benchmark the system and present how high-resolution temporal post-selection can enable secure key exchange, with further rate improvements expected through realistic device and protocol optimizations. Due to its compatibility with a wide range of quantum memory platforms, our approach represents a significant step towards long-distance quantum-safe communication.

</details>


### [61] [Rate-Optimal Quantum Discrete Simulation Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03360)
*Mingjie Hu,Jian-Qiang Hu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies whether quantum computing can accelerate discrete simulation optimization in the fixed-budget setting, establishing a minimax lower bound and proposing QUEST, the first fixed-budget quantum algorithm that achieves the optimal rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum computing has shown potential advantages over classical computing across domains, motivating the question of whether it can accelerate the convergence rate of discrete simulation optimization under a fixed simulation budget.

Method: The authors formulate the quantum discrete simulation optimization problem by defining a quantum simulation oracle, performance measure, and optimality guarantee. They derive a minimax lower bound by reducing the problem to sequential quantum phase testing, and propose QUEST, which combines a quantum Monte Carlo subroutine, a sequential elimination framework, and a slow-measurement mechanism to preserve superposition.

Result: The quantum optimal convergence rate shows a quadratic improvement in its dependence on the simulation budget compared to the classical counterpart, at the cost of worse dependence on the number of decisions. QUEST achieves the optimal rate up to logarithmic factors, and numerical experiments show it outperforms the classical counterpart.

Conclusion: Quantum computing can accelerate the convergence rate of discrete simulation optimization, with QUEST providing both theoretical optimality and empirical evidence of quantum advantage.

Abstract: Quantum computing has developed rapidly in recent years and has shown potential advantages over classical computing in various domains. In this paper, we study whether quantum computing can accelerate the convergence rate of discrete simulation optimization in the fixed-budget setting. We first formulate the quantum discrete simulation optimization problem by defining the quantum simulation oracle, the algorithmic performance measure, and the corresponding optimality guarantee. Based on this formulation, we establish a minimax lower bound on the expected optimality gap for any quantum algorithm. The lower bound analysis reduces the discrete simulation optimization problem to a sequential quantum phase-testing problem, and the resulting lower-bound argument may be of independent interest. By comparing the quantum lower bound with its classical counterpart, we show that the optimal convergence rate in the quantum setting exhibits a quadratic improvement in its dependence on the simulation budget, at the cost of a worse dependence on the number of decisions. We then propose the first fixed-budget quantum algorithm, called \textsc{QUEST}, for discrete simulation optimization. The algorithm combines a quantum Monte Carlo subroutine with a sequential elimination framework and uses a slow-measurement mechanism to help preserve quantum superposition during the optimization process. We prove that \textsc{QUEST} achieves the optimal convergence rate up to logarithmic factors. Numerical experiments further show that \textsc{QUEST} outperforms its classical counterpart, which provides empirical evidence for the quantum advantage in improving the convergence rate of discrete simulation optimization algorithms.

</details>


### [62] [Strict non-tightness of the level-3 quantum bootstrap for a natural three-dimensional Hamiltonian, at an excited level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03362)
*Daniel Keren,Ye Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs a linear functional on degree-≤6 polynomials that satisfies all level-3 eigenstate bootstrap constraints, has a strictly positive definite moment matrix, yet assigns a negative value to a nonnegative polynomial—revealing an interior point of the level-3 relaxation not realized by any quantum state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The eigenstate bootstrap (Lasserre-type moment relaxations) is used to bound quantum energies, but it is unclear whether solutions to the relaxation always correspond to actual quantum states. The paper aims to expose a gap between the relaxation and physical realizability.

Method: Study the coupled 3D double well H = ½|p|² + 2Σ(qᵢ²−1)² − Σ_{i<j}qᵢ²qⱼ². Construct an explicit linear functional on polynomials of degree ≤6 passing all standard level-3 eigenstate bootstrap constraints with a strictly positive definite moment matrix, while violating nonnegativity on a nonnegative polynomial. All algebraic ingredients are exact rational certificates; the only numerical parts (spectral enclosures of an eigenfunction) use rigorous ball arithmetic.

Result: A functional is exhibited that is an interior point of the level-3 relaxation (strictly feasible) but realized by no quantum state, since it assigns a negative value to a nonnegative polynomial. The construction reflects the general fact that eigenstate rows never reach top-degree momentum moments; at level 3 this means the corresponding moment-matrix block carries no information about which energies admit a state.

Conclusion: The level-3 eigenstate bootstrap relaxation admits strictly feasible points that are not physically realizable, showing it does not faithfully encode state existence. This is tied to a structural fact holding at every level: eigenstate rows never touch top-degree momentum moments, so the relevant moment-matrix block is uninformative at level 3.

Abstract: For the coupled three-dimensional double well $H=\tfrac12|p|^2+2\sum_i(q_i^2-1)^2-\sum_{i<j}q_i^2q_j^2$ we exhibit, at the first excited energy level (and also at the ground state), a linear functional on polynomials of degree $\le 6$ that satisfies every constraint of the standard level-3 eigenstate bootstrap, has a \emph{strictly} positive definite moment matrix, and nevertheless assigns a negative value to a nonnegative polynomial. It is therefore an interior point of the level-3 relaxation that is realized by no quantum state. The construction reflects a fact that holds at every level: the eigenstate rows never reach the top-degree momentum moments. At level 3, where strictly feasible points exist, this means that the corresponding block of the moment matrix carries no information about which energies admit one. Every algebraic ingredient is an exact rational certificate, and the only numerical ingredients (spectral enclosures of an eigenfunction) are carried out in rigorous ball arithmetic.

</details>


### [63] [Distance-Independent Universality of Clifford+T](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03364)
*Jens Palsberg,Keli Huang,Abdullah Almanei*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper defines projective distance measures via four axioms and proves that Clifford+T universality holds for any distance measure satisfying them, making the theorem independent of any particular metric, and it formalizes the proof in Lean.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard Clifford+T universality theorem relies on approximation under a distance measure on unitary matrices plus equality up to global phase. Prior proofs handle these two notions only for specific distance measures and never isolate the abstract properties that make them interact correctly.

Method: The authors introduce projective distance measures through four axioms that capture the necessary interplay between a metric on unitaries and global-phase equivalence. They prove the universality theorem abstractly for all measures satisfying these axioms, show Hilbert-Schmidt distance is already projective, and give a general construction converting a broad class of distance measures (operator-norm, Frobenius, trace) into projective ones. The proof is then formalized in Lean.

Result: Universality holds for every distance measure satisfying the four axioms. Hilbert-Schmidt distance satisfies them directly, and projective versions of operator-norm, Frobenius, and trace distances are obtained via the general transformation. A machine-checked Lean formalization of the theorem is provided.

Conclusion: The Clifford+T universality theorem can be stated and proved in a distance-measure-agnostic way, with the four axioms characterizing exactly the properties required and the Lean formalization ensuring rigor.

Abstract: A well-known theorem states that the Clifford+T gate set is universal for quantum computing. The theorem combines approximation using a distance measure on unitary matrices with equality up to global phase. Previous proofs combine these two notions for specific distance measures, but do not identify the properties that govern how they work together. Which properties are sufficient to state and prove the theorem?
  We answer this question by defining projective distance measures using four axioms. We prove that the universality theorem holds for every distance measure satisfying these axioms. Thus, our formulation of the theorem is independent of any particular choice of distance measure. We show that the Hilbert-Schmidt distance is already a projective distance measure. We also develop a general construction that transforms a large class of distance measures into projective distance measures and apply it to obtain projective versions of the operator-norm distance, the Frobenius distance, and the trace distance. Finally, we formalize the proof of the universality theorem in Lean.

</details>


### [64] [No Size-Preserving Amplification with Quantum Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03377)
*Shih-Han Hung,Han-Hsuan Lin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that state-size-preserving error reduction, known to hold for QMA, fails for BQP with quantum advice. It proves a separation BQP_ε/qm ⊊ BQP_{ε+δ}/qm for various advice lengths m and errors ε, via a stronger separation from a randomized-advice class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marriott and Watrous proved that QMA games admit generic error reduction without increasing witness size. The authors ask whether this state-size-preserving amplification property extends to polynomial-time quantum computation with quantum advice (BQP/qm).

Method: They prove a stronger separation: P_{ε+δ}/rm ⊄ BQP_ε/qm, where P_{ε}/rm denotes deterministic polynomial-time algorithms with an m-bit advice string sampled from a distribution depending only on the input length. This stronger separation is then used to derive the BQP_ε/qm vs. BQP_{ε+δ}/qm separation.

Result: For every polynomially bounded advice length m(n) ≥ n^4 and every error bound ε(n) staying below 1/2 by at least an inverse polynomial, there exists δ(n) = O(min{(log m/m)^{1/4}, sqrt(log m/m)/(1/2−ε(n))}) such that BQP_ε/qm ⊊ BQP_{ε+δ}/qm. For constant ε, the gap is O(sqrt(log m/m)).

Conclusion: State-size-preserving amplification (error reduction without increasing advice size) does not hold for BQP with quantum advice, unlike the QMA case. Even a vanishing additive error reduction can require longer quantum advice.

Abstract: Marriott and Watrous showed that quantum Merlin--Arthur games admit generic error reduction without increasing witness size [Computational Complexity, 2005]. In this work, we show that this state-size-preserving amplification property does not hold for polynomial-time quantum computation with quantum advice. In particular, we present decision problems for which even a vanishing additive error reduction requires longer advice. More precisely, for every polynomially bounded advice length $m(n)\geq n^4$ and every error bound $\varepsilon(n)$ that stays below $1/2$ by at least an inverse polynomial, there is a positive function $δ$ with $δ(n)=O\bigl(\min\{(\log m/m)^{1/4},\ \sqrt{\log m/m}\,/(1/2-\varepsilon(n))\}\bigr)$ such that $\mathsf{BQP}_{\varepsilon}/\mathsf{q}m \subsetneq \mathsf{BQP}_{\varepsilon + δ}/\mathsf{q}m$; for constant $\varepsilon$ the gap is $O(\sqrt{\log m/m})$. Here, $\mathsf{BQP}_\varepsilon/\mathsf{q}m$ is the class of languages recognizable with error at most $\varepsilon(n)$ by a polynomial-time quantum algorithm with an $m(n)$-qubit advice state that only depends on the input length $n$. We show this by proving a stronger separation $\mathsf{P}_{\varepsilon+δ}/\mathsf{r} m \not\subset \mathsf{BQP}_{\varepsilon}/\mathsf{q} m$, where $\mathsf{P}_{\varepsilon}/\mathsf{r}m$ is the class of languages recognizable with error at most $\varepsilon(n)$ by a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm with an $m(n)$-bit advice string sampled from a distribution that depends only on $n$.

</details>


### [65] [Degree-conditioned anneal offsets reshape certified-optimum sampling for minimum vertex cover](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03379)
*Jiwan Kang,Namshik Han*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: DC-OSQA, a static degree-conditioned anneal-offset schedule, diversifies the certified optima sampled by a D-Wave Advantage2 QPU for Minimum Vertex Cover at a fixed read budget, increasing distinct certified candidates and Hamming-distance families versus a zero-offset baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum annealers sample degenerate optima unevenly, so the composition of finite-read output matters when multiple optimal solutions are sought. The paper asks whether problem structure itself can steer this output.

Method: MVC vertex degree equals the number of incident edge-cover constraints, measuring local constraint participation. This relation defines DC-OSQA, a static rule assigning more negative offsets to vertices with larger max-normalized degree. Experiments on a D-Wave Advantage2 QPU compared DC-OSQA against a zero-offset baseline under identical MVC encoding, fixed physical embedding, majority-vote chain decoding, and a 1000-read budget. A sampled bitstring counted as a certified candidate only if feasible and of minimum cardinality as independently certified by integer linear programming.

Result: Across 120 paired comparisons on sparse Barabasi-Albert graphs (two attachments per added vertex, N = 70-150), DC-OSQA raised the mean number of distinct certified candidates from 171.7 to 455.8 and mean complete-linkage Hamming-distance families from 22.5 to 80.0; paired gains were positive at every size. Lower empirical concentration indicates a change in the composition of the certified support. At N = 150, degree-aligned assignment beat the within-instance mean over 20 random reassignments in 19 of 20 graphs for certified-support size and all 20 graphs for Hamming families.

Conclusion: Within the tested ensemble, an MVC-specific degree rule changes the certified optima observed within a fixed QPU read budget, showing that problem structure can steer the composition of degenerate-optimum output.

Abstract: Quantum annealers can sample degenerate optima unevenly, so the composition of finite-read output matters when multiple solutions are sought. We investigate whether problem structure can steer this output through node-dependent anneal offsets for Minimum Vertex Cover (MVC). In MVC, vertex degree equals the number of incident edge-cover constraints and therefore measures local constraint participation. We use this relation to define degree-conditioned offset-scheduled quantum annealing (DC-OSQA), a static rule assigning more negative offsets to vertices with larger max-normalized degree. Experiments used a D-Wave Advantage2 system1 quantum processing unit (QPU). For each graph, DC-OSQA was compared with a zero-offset baseline using the same MVC encoding, fixed physical embedding, majority-vote chain decoding, and 1000-read budget. A sampled bitstring was counted as a certified candidate only if it represented a feasible vertex cover and its cardinality matched the minimum independently certified by integer linear programming. Across 120 paired comparisons on sparse Barabasi-Albert graphs with two attachments per added vertex and graph sizes N = 70-150, DC-OSQA increased the mean number of distinct certified candidates from 171.7 to 455.8 and the mean number of complete-linkage Hamming-distance families from 22.5 to 80.0; paired gains were positive at every tested size. These shifts, together with lower empirical concentration, indicate a change in the composition of the observed certified support. At N = 150, the degree-aligned assignment exceeded the within-instance mean over 20 random reassignments of the same offset values in 19 of 20 graphs for certified-support size and all 20 graphs for Hamming families. Within the tested ensemble, these results show that an MVC-specific degree rule changes the certified optima observed within a fixed QPU read budget.

</details>


### [66] [Quantum utility routing in continuous-variable QKD networks with trusted and untrusted relays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03382)
*Farsad Ahmad,Aeysha Khalique*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: QUR is a weighted routing framework for CV-QKD networks that embeds finite-size composable secret key rates into route search, balancing bottleneck SKR against resource use via graph-optimized and ML-based fixed-weight variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CV-QKD networks need routing strategies that simultaneously account for secret key generation, relay trust levels, and finite network resources, which existing routing approaches do not jointly address.

Method: Quantum Utility Routing (QUR) is introduced as a weighted routing framework for networks combining trusted point-to-point links with untrusted CV Bell measurement relays. Transition-level composable finite-size SKRs are incorporated directly into route search. Two variants are studied: QUR_go (graph-optimized) and QUR_fw (fixed weights learned via machine learning to avoid repeated graph-specific optimization), benchmarked against A* and Max-Min routing.

Result: At the default network configuration, QUR_go and QUR_fw improve mean path SKR relative to A* while requiring substantially fewer network resources than Max-Min. The framework also maintains secure connectivity across a broad range of trusted node availability by exploiting untrusted infrastructure as measurement relays.

Conclusion: Routing in CV-QKD networks can be treated as a configurable trade-off between cryptographic performance and network resource consumption.

Abstract: Continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CV-QKD) networks require routing strategies that account simultaneously for secret key generation, relay trust and finite network resources. We introduce Quantum Utility Routing (QUR), a weighted routing framework for networks combining trusted point-to-point links with untrusted CV Bell measurement relays. Transition level composable finite size secret key rates (SKRs) are incorporated directly into the route search, allowing QUR to balance bottleneck SKR against route geometry and resource use. We consider graph optimized QUR (QUR$_{\mathrm{go}}$) and a fixed weight implementation (QUR$_{\mathrm{fw}}$) that uses machine learning to avoid repeated graph specific weight optimization, and benchmark both against A* and Max--Min routing. At the default network configuration, QUR$_{\mathrm{go}}$ and QUR$_{\mathrm{fw}}$ improve the mean path SKR relative to A* while requiring substantially fewer network resources than Max--Min. The framework also maintains secure connectivity over a broad range of trusted node availability by exploiting untrusted infrastructure as measurement relays. These results demonstrate that routing in CV-QKD networks can be treated as a configurable trade off between cryptographic performance and network resource consumption.

</details>


### [67] [Multivariate quantum signal processing with optimal query complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03393)
*Mingrui Jing,Xin Wang,Zhan Yu,Lei Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quantum circuit implements every multivariate trigonometric polynomial with query complexity equal to each variable's degree—proven optimal—and is extended to commuting unitaries and to trainable rotations with gradient-variance guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Polynomial transformations of quantum-encoded data underpin many quantum algorithms, but for multiple variables existing methods are limited: they either realize only restricted polynomial families or implement monomials separately at a query cost exponential in the number of variables. A general, query-efficient construction was missing.

Method: The authors design a quantum circuit that implements any multivariate trigonometric polynomial up to an explicit normalization. It processes one variable coherently over the frequency components of the remaining variables, so all components share the same signal queries, with each variable queried exactly its polynomial degree. They prove this query complexity optimal, extend the construction to commuting unitaries (applying the polynomial simultaneously across the shared eigenbasis using minimal forward and inverse queries), and analyze trainable rotations with fixed preparation and local readout of the last input.

Result: The circuit achieves optimal query complexity, matching each variable's degree. For the extended commuting-unitary case, it uses the minimum number of forward and inverse queries per unitary. For trainable rotations at query depth at least two, independent uniform initialization yields a gradient-variance lower bound for every active readout gradient, set by its squared branch weight and the inverse square root of depth, uniformly over data states and dimensions. For supervised squared loss, retaining sample correlations gives an initialization gradient-variance bound in terms of targets and input spectra, which also bounds the expected loss decrease after the first exact gradient step at a specified step size.

Conclusion: The work provides a scalable and practical foundation for multivariate quantum algorithms and quantum learning models, combining optimal polynomial-implementation query complexity with trainability guarantees for initialized trainable rotations.

Abstract: Polynomial transformations of data encoded in quantum operations are a building block of quantum algorithms, and their query complexity measures how often those operations are used. For multiple variables, existing methods either realize restricted polynomial families or implement monomials separately at a query cost exponential in the number of variables. We present a quantum circuit that implements every multivariate trigonometric polynomial up to an explicit normalization. The circuit processes one variable coherently over the frequency components of the remaining variables, allowing all components to share the same signal queries. Each variable is queried exactly as many times as its degree in the polynomial, and we prove that this query complexity is optimal. We further extend the construction to commuting unitaries. The resulting circuit applies the multivariate polynomial simultaneously across their shared eigenbasis, using the minimum numbers of forward and inverse queries to each unitary. We also study trainable rotations with fixed preparation and a local readout of the last input. At query depth at least two, independent uniform initialization gives every active readout gradient a variance lower bound set by its squared branch weight and the inverse square root of the depth, uniformly over data states and dimensions. For supervised squared loss, retaining sample correlations gives an initialization gradient-variance bound in terms of targets and input spectra. This also bounds the expected loss decrease after the first exact gradient step with a specified step size. These results provide a scalable and practical solution for developing multivariate quantum algorithms and quantum learning models.

</details>


### [68] [Optimizing quantum error correction through error attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03399)
*Yuguo Shao,Zi-Wen Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces an error attribution scheme that quantifies how individual circuit components influence the logical error rate of quantum error correction (QEC), enabling targeted noise reduction. Component sensitivities are computed jointly from the same Monte Carlo samples used for logical error rate estimation, revealing hotspots where intervention is most effective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Logical error rate is only an aggregate benchmark and masks the fact that errors at different circuit locations can have markedly different impacts on logical failure, even with equal probabilities. This limits the ability to prioritize where to invest limited noise-reduction resources in practical QEC architectures.

Method: An error attribution scheme computes sensitivities of all circuit components jointly from the same Monte Carlo samples used to estimate the logical error rate, producing sensitivity maps that identify hotspots for targeted noise reduction. The scheme is tested via circuit simulations of rotated surface code memories and high-rate lifted product codes.

Result: Halving noise strength on roughly 5%–7% of physical components selected by sensitivity reduces logical error rates by about 15%–25%, roughly twice the mean reduction obtained from the same intervention on an equal number of randomly selected components.

Conclusion: By linking performance benchmarking to targeted optimization, the scheme offers a practical strategy for improving QEC performance under limited resources.

Abstract: Logical error rate is a standard benchmark for quantum error correction, providing an aggregate measure of the overall effect of physical noise. In actual QEC architectures, errors at different circuit locations can have markedly different effects on logical failure even when their probabilities are equal. Here we introduce an error attribution scheme to quantitatively characterize the influence of individual circuit components on the logical error rate and use this refined information to guide targeted intervention and design for improved QEC performance. Our attribution estimator computes all component sensitivities jointly from the same Monte Carlo samples used to estimate the logical error rate. The resulting sensitivity maps identify hotspots where targeted noise reduction can most effectively improve logical performance. We test the scheme in circuit simulations of rotated surface code memories and high-rate lifted product codes: halving the noise strength on about 5\%-7\% of physical components selected by sensitivity reduces logical error rates by approximately 15\%-25\%, roughly twice the mean reduction from the same intervention on an equal number of randomly selected components. By connecting performance benchmarking to targeted optimization, our scheme provides a practical strategy for improving quantum error correction with limited resources.

</details>


### [69] [Subdimensional linear-optical quantum computation: from qudit resource states to qubit quantum computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03408)
*Tomohiro Yamazaki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A linear-optical quantum computation scheme uses high-dimensional entangled qudits to boost fusion success probability from 50% to 1−1/d, enabling efficient generation of a 2D qubit cluster state from 5-qudit 1D cluster states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear-optical fusion gates typically succeed with only 50% probability, which limits scalability and loss tolerance. Existing solutions rely on ancilla photons and quantum codes, adding overhead. The paper seeks to boost fusion success probability without such extra resources.

Method: The scheme starts from high-dimensionally entangled qudits but performs quantum computation on qubits defined in their subspaces. Fusion gates are applied pairwise to 5-qudit 1D cluster states to generate a 2D cluster state of qubits while preserving the boosted success probability. Numerical simulations assess loss tolerance of a percolation-based scheme.

Result: The fusion success probability is increased from 50% to 1−1/d for qudits of dimension d. A 2D cluster state of qubits can be generated from 5-qudit 1D cluster states via pairwise fusion gates. Numerical results show improved loss tolerance of the percolation-based scheme while keeping photon number per resource state constant.

Conclusion: High-dimensional qudits provide a resource-efficient route to boosted fusion success probability in linear-optical quantum computation, improving loss tolerance without ancilla photons or quantum codes and without increasing photon number per resource state.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a linear-optical quantum computation scheme that starts from high-dimensionally entangled qudits but performs quantum computation on qubits defined in their subspaces. This approach enables us to increase the success probability of linear-optical fusions from $50\%$ to $1-1/d$ for qudits of dimension $d$ without relying on existing approaches using ancilla photons and quantum codes. In particular, we show that a 2D cluster state of qubits can be generated from 5-qudit 1D cluster states using pairwise fusion gates while preserving their boosted success probability. We numerically demonstrate that this approach can improve the loss tolerance of a percolation-based scheme while keeping the photon number per resource state constant.

</details>


### [70] [Fault tolerance of quantum circuits with tensor networks and symplectic geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03429)
*Soham Ghosh,Holger Boche,Andrew Tanggara*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper builds an operator-algebraic framework for quantum fault tolerance, giving necessary and sufficient conditions (via SDP infeasibility) for the existence of recovery maps, exact algebraic distance characterizations for non-adaptive stabilizer circuits, MacWilliams identities and LP/Singleton-like bounds on circuit distance, and applies these to Floquet-code and flag syndrome-extraction circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault tolerance in quantum circuits lacks a unified, noise-model-agnostic characterization. The authors aim to provide rigorous necessary and sufficient conditions for fault tolerance under arbitrary noise models and to make circuit distance—a key fault-tolerance metric—computable algebraically for practically relevant circuit families.

Method: An operator-algebraic framework is developed, with a semidefinite program (SDP) test whose infeasibility certifies non-existence of any recovery map for given circuit families and noise models. For non-adaptive stabilizer circuits, stabilizer symmetries of components are analyzed using symplectic geometry to obtain closed-form detection and logical-effect matrices characterizing circuit distance algebraically. MacWilliams identities for circuit weight enumerators are derived to yield LP upper bounds on circuit distance, plus Singleton-like output-code bounds. The t-flag criterion is incorporated as extra linear constraints into the distance-bounding LP for flag syndrome-extraction circuits specified by CNOT orderings.

Result: For a finite Hastings-Haah honeycomb Floquet-code circuit, the closed-form matrices certify dynamical encoding of two logical qubits with distance four under Pauli noise including measurement errors. Under i.i.d. depolarizing noise, weight enumerators determine decoder failure probabilities and give upper bounds on finite-circuit pseudothresholds. For flag syndrome-extraction circuits, an analytical CSS Hamming-code benchmark yields a tight distance-three bound attained by the Chao-Reichardt one-flag construction.

Conclusion: The operator-algebraic and symplectic-geometric approach yields exact, closed-form certificates of fault tolerance and circuit distance, SDP-based necessary and sufficient fault-tolerance conditions, and LP/Singleton-like distance bounds that connect circuit weight enumerators to decoder failure and pseudothresholds, with the t-flag criterion naturally expressible as linear constraints.

Abstract: We develop an operator-algebraic framework for analyzing fault tolerance in quantum circuits and establish necessary and sufficient conditions for fault tolerance under any given noise model. For prescribed circuit families and noise models, we derive a semidefinite program (SDP) based test whose infeasibility certifies that there does not exist any recovery map restoring its intended operation. For non-adaptive stabilizer circuits, analyzing the stabilizer symmetries of its components with symplectic geometry, we obtain closed-form detection and logical-effect matrices that characterize circuit distance algebraically. Applied to a finite Hastings--Haah honeycomb Floquet-code circuit, these matrices certify the dynamical encoding of two logical qubits and distance four under Pauli noise including measurement errors. Using these matrices, we then derive MacWilliams identities for circuit weight enumerators and obtain linear-programming (LP) upper bounds on circuit distance. Output-code constraints give additional Singleton-like bounds. Under independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) depolarizing noise, the weight enumerators determine decoder failure probabilities and yield upper bounds on finite-circuit pseudothresholds. Finally, we derive LP distance bounds for families of flag syndrome-extraction circuits specified by their CNOT orderings, by incorporating the $t$-flag criterion as additional linear constraints to the distance-bounding LP. An analytical CSS Hamming-code benchmark yields a tight distance-three bound attained by the Chao--Reichardt one-flag construction.

</details>


### [71] [An exact semidefinite characterization of the stoquasticity cone of permutationally invariant Bell operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03438)
*Jan Li,Owidiusz Makuta,Evert van Nieuwenburg,Jordi Tura*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives an exact semidefinite characterization of the stoquasticity cone for permutationally invariant Bell operators in the thermodynamic limit, valid at any operator order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bell correlations have been detected in atomic ensembles using permutationally invariant (PI) Bell operators, and all realized operators' maximally violating states can be brought to a nonnegative-amplitude form in some basis, making the Bell operator stoquastic. The coefficients producing stoquasticity form a convex cone (the stoquasticity cone), but characterizing it beyond two-body operators was open because the number of inequalities grows with system size n.

Method: The authors exploit the observation that, along each off-diagonal, the matrix elements are values of a single univariate polynomial. Since the condition is univariate, it is equivalent to a linear matrix inequality with no relaxation hierarchy, reducing stoquasticity to a single semidefinite feasibility problem.

Result: In the thermodynamic limit, the stoquasticity cone admits an exact semidefinite characterization valid at any operator order. Its lowest-degree instances recover the known three-hyperplane characterization for two-body operators and yield a curved boundary for three-body operators.

Conclusion: Stoquasticity of PI Bell operators can be decided exactly via a single semidefinite feasibility problem, providing a complete characterization of the stoquasticity cone at any order in the thermodynamic limit.

Abstract: Bell correlations have been detected in atomic ensembles ranging from hundreds to hundreds of thousands of particles, witnessed by the permutationally invariant (PI) Bell operators. For all the operators realized so far, the maximally violating state can be brought, in a suitable basis, into a nonnegative-amplitude form. In that basis, the Bell operator is stoquastic. The coefficients parametrising these operators that at the same time lead to stoquasticity form a convex cone, the so-called stoquasticity cone. Its characterization had remained open beyond two-body operators, as for many-body operators the number of inequalities grows with the system size $n$. In this work we show that, in the thermodynamic limit, the stoquasticity cone admits an exact semidefinite characterization, valid at any operator order. The key observation is that, along each off-diagonal, the matrix elements are the values of a single univariate polynomial. Being univariate, this condition is equivalent to a linear matrix inequality, with no relaxation hierarchy. Therefore, deciding stoquasticity becomes a single semidefinite feasibility problem. As its lowest-degree instances, this description recovers the known three-hyperplane characterization for two-body operators and yields, for three-body operators, a curved boundary.

</details>


### [72] [Geometric Aspects of Entanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03446)
*Lucio De Simone,Lorenzo Capra,Arthur Vesperini,Leonardo Rossi,Loris Di Cairano,Roberto Franzosi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces the entanglement distance (ED), a geometric entanglement measure on multi-qubit states based on the Fubini-Study metric and local-unitary invariance, and shows it reduces to monotone functions of concurrence and entanglement entropy for two-qubit pure states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterizing and quantifying entanglement, especially in multipartite systems, remains challenging. The work seeks a geometric framework that gives standard bipartite measures a clear geometric interpretation.

Method: Exploits the Riemannian structure induced by the Fubini-Study metric on the projective Hilbert space of multi-qubit states and its local-unitary invariance to define ED as the obstruction to locally minimizing the sum of squared Fubini-Study distances generated by local operations. Properties are analyzed for pure states, including behavior under LOCC.

Result: For two-qubit pure states, ED is an exact monotone function of the concurrence and, independently, an explicit monotone function of the entropy of entanglement. Correspondences with established measures hold only in restricted settings and break down beyond the two-qubit case.

Conclusion: ED provides a geometric interpretation of standard bipartite entanglement measures within a unified Riemannian framework, while highlighting the limitations of extending such correspondences to multipartite systems.
