Abstract: Quantum entanglement is a fundamental resource in quantum information theory, yet its general characterization and quantification remain challenging, especially in multipartite systems. In this work we investigate entanglement from a geometric perspective, focusing on the Riemannian structure induced by the Fubini--Study metric on the projective Hilbert space of multi-qubit quantum states. By exploiting the local-unitary invariance of this metric, we derive the entanglement distance (ED), a geometric measure that quantifies entanglement as an obstruction to locally minimizing the sum of squared Fubini--Study distances generated by local operations.
  We analyze the properties of ED for pure multi-qubit states and discuss its behavior under local operations and classical communication. In particular, we show that ED reproduces established entanglement measures in well-defined and restricted settings. For pure states of two qubits, ED reduces to an exact monotone function of the concurrence and, independently, to an explicit monotone function of the entropy of entanglement. These results provide a clear geometric interpretation of standard bipartite entanglement measures within the present framework, while highlighting the limitations of such correspondences beyond the two-qubit case.

</details>


### [73] [Generalization of Transformer-Based Neural Quantum States via In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03463)
*Zhen Qin,Qing Qu,Alfred O. Hero*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a theoretical framework analyzing generalization of Transformer-based neural quantum states under in-context learning, proving MSE error bounds that decay with in-context examples and depth, with depth scaling linearly in system size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer-based neural quantum states empirically capture long-range correlations and generalize well, but their generalization behavior lacks theoretical understanding. This work aims to fill that gap with a rigorous theoretical analysis.

Method: The authors establish an inference-time generalization error bound in MSE for Transformer-based neural quantum states under in-context learning, then extend the analysis to full quantum states as rank-one density operators under physical constraints over continuous and discrete domains. Numerical simulations validate the theory.

Result: The pointwise prediction error decreases inversely with both the number of in-context examples and Transformer depth. The required depth scales only linearly with system size (number of particles in continuous systems or qudits in discrete systems). MSE-based generalization bounds are derived for continuous and discrete domains.

Conclusion: Transformer-based neural quantum states enjoy favorable generalization guarantees under in-context learning, with depth requirements scaling linearly with system size, supported by both theory and numerical simulations.

Abstract: Neural quantum states based on modern deep learning architectures have emerged as powerful representations for quantum many-body systems. In particular, Transformer-based neural quantum states provide expressive models capable of capturing long-range correlations, and their empirical generalization performance has recently been demonstrated. However, a theoretical understanding of their generalization behavior remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we develop a theoretical framework to analyze the generalization properties of Transformer-based neural quantum states under in-context learning. We establish a rigorous inference-time generalization error bound in terms of mean squared error (MSE), showing that the pointwise prediction error decreases inversely with both the number of in-context examples and the depth of the Transformer. We further show that the Transformer depth required to achieve this guarantee scales only linearly with the system size--namely, the number of particles in continuous systems or the number of qudits in discrete systems. Building on this result, we extend our analysis to full quantum states formulated as rank-one density operators, and derive MSE-based generalization bounds over both continuous and discrete domains under physical constraints. Finally, numerical simulations corroborate our theoretical analysis.

</details>


### [74] [Efficient Sampling for Many-Body Fermionic Non-Gaussianity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03492)
*Ryota Matsuda,Masahiro Hoshino,Yuto Ashida*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The authors introduce a perfect-sampling method to compute the second-order magic Renyi entropy (MRE) from matrix product states, generating each sample in O(nD^3) time, and benchmark it on XXZ chains up to 128 sites to reveal higher-order correlations beyond covariance-based measures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fermionic non-Gaussianity is a key resource for universal quantum computation, but existing convolution-based measures like the magic Renyi entropy (MRE) can only be evaluated on small systems because computational cost grows exponentially with system size. A scalable evaluation tool for many-body fermionic non-Gaussianity is therefore needed.

Method: The paper develops a perfect-sampling method to calculate the second-order MRE from matrix product states (MPSs). It employs a bounded estimator to control sampling fluctuations and a recursive sweep to draw samples directly from the input MPS, with each sample generated in O(nD^3) time, where n is the number of modes and D is the MPS bond dimension.

Result: Benchmarking on the XXZ chain with up to 128 sites shows that the MRE captures intricate higher-order correlations at large scales that are inaccessible to covariance-based measures.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides a computational tool that quantitatively evaluates many-body fermionic non-Gaussianity by accounting for higher-order correlations, enabling scalable resource quantification.

Abstract: Fermionic non-Gaussianity is a key resource for universal quantum computation, and its behavior in quantum many-body systems has attracted growing interest. Recently, a convolution-based measure, which we call the magic Rényi entropy (MRE), has been proposed as a resource measure of non-Gaussianity, but its evaluation is limited to small systems due to the computational cost growing exponentially with system size. In this work, we develop a perfect-sampling method to calculate the second-order MRE from matrix product states (MPSs). Our method uses a bounded estimator to control sampling fluctuations and a recursive sweep to draw the samples directly from the input MPS, which allows us to generate each sample with $\mathcal O(nD^3)$ time, where $n$ and $D$ denote the number of modes and MPS bond dimension, respectively. We benchmark our method on the XXZ chain with up to $128$ sites, demonstrating that the MRE captures intricate higher-order correlations at large scales, which are inaccessible to covariance-based measures. These results provide a computational tool that quantitatively evaluates many-body fermionic non-Gaussianity by accounting for higher-order correlations.

</details>


### [75] [Mode-tunable inter-core coupling of photon-number-resolved quantum light in a telecom multicore fiber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03493)
*Chiran Wijesundara,Yasmin Sarhan,Henry Strong,Jeffrey Cai,Dan T. Nguyen,Tim Thomay*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A telecom-grade 39-core fiber is operated far from its design wavelength so each core supports two LP mode groups (LP01, LP11). Heralded |1> and |2> photon states are measured with simultaneous resolution of photon number and intra-core spatial mode (or core index), revealing a launch-controlled mode-selectable inter-core coupling knob and photon-number-independent propagation, with a spurious crosstalk artifact identified when accidentals are not subtracted.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multicore fibers, originally developed for classical telecommunications scaling, are emerging as quantum platforms for high-dimensional key distribution, entanglement, and photonic quantum walks. However, quantum light has so far been confined to single-mode path channels within each core, leaving the intra-core spatial-mode structure unexploited. The authors aim to harness this untapped spatial-mode degree of freedom and to establish the linear-optics baseline against which genuine multiphoton interference in fiber quantum walks must be certified.

Method: A telecom-grade 39-core fiber is operated far from its design wavelength so that each core supports exactly two LP mode groups (LP01, LP11). Heralded photon-number states |1> and |2> are measured, resolving photon number simultaneously with intra-core spatial mode or core index. A displaced launch prepares a coherent LP01-LP11 superposition; intermodal phase preservation is probed via the single-sided intensity pattern using a drift-resolved joint fit. Mode-dependent inter-core coupling rates are quantified, and propagation is tested for photon-number independence via per-photon crosstalk of |2> versus |1>, an imaged mode-distribution comparison, and an exact Fock-space simulation.

Result: The single-sided intensity pattern yields a pixel asymmetry of 0.4% versus 47%, incompatible with an incoherent mode mixture, confirming intermodal phase is preserved along the fiber. The two modes couple to neighboring cores at different rates (kappa_LP11/kappa_LP01 = 2.4), so launched mode content acts as a state-selectable coupling knob, setting effective inter-core coupling of the passive fiber between 3.9% and 13.1% purely by launch, without electro-optic, thermo-optic, or mechanical reconfiguration. Per-photon crosstalk of heralded |2> equals that of |1>, bounding any Fock-state dependence to |dx/x| < 6.1% (95% CL); imaged mode distributions of |1> and |2> agree to better than 1%. Coincidence-based event selection is shown to produce spurious photon-number-dependent crosstalk if accidentals are not subtracted.

Conclusion: Multicore fiber cores can serve as dual-mode quantum channels whose intra-core spatial structure provides a launch-controlled, state-selectable coupling knob without active reconfiguration, and whose propagation is photon-number independent. This measured linear-optics null, validated against exact Fock-space simulation, establishes the baseline required to certify genuine multiphoton interference in fiber quantum walks, and highlights the need to subtract accidentals in coincidence-based event selection.

Abstract: Multicore optical fibers (MCFs), developed to scale classical telecommunications, are gaining traction as a quantum platform for high-dimensional key distribution, entanglement, and photonic quantum walks. So far, quantum light has occupied these fibers only as single-mode path channels; the spatial-mode structure within each core has remained untouched. Here we operate a telecom-grade 39-core fiber far from its design wavelength, where each core supports exactly two LP mode groups (LP01, LP11), and measure heralded photon-number states |1> and |2>, resolving the photon number simultaneously with the intra-core spatial mode or with the core index. Displacing the launch prepares a coherent LP01-LP11 superposition whose single-sided intensity pattern shows that the intermodal phase is preserved over the fiber: a drift-resolved joint fit gives a pixel asymmetry (0.4% vs 47%) incompatible with an incoherent mode mixture. Because the two modes couple to neighboring cores at different rates (kappa_LP11/kappa_LP01 = 2.4), the launched mode content acts as a state-selectable coupling knob: the effective inter-core coupling of the passive fiber is set by the launch alone (3.9-13.1%), a tunability that otherwise requires electro-optic, thermo-optic, or mechanical reconfiguration. The propagation is photon-number independent: the per-photon crosstalk of heralded |2> states equals that of |1>, bounding any Fock-state dependence to |dx/x| < 6.1% (95% CL), and the imaged mode distributions of |1> and |2> agree to better than 1%. This measured linear-optics null, validated against exact Fock-space simulation, is the baseline against which genuine multiphoton interference in fiber quantum walks must be certified; we also show how coincidence-based event selection produces spurious photon-number-dependent crosstalk if accidentals are not subtracted.

</details>


### [76] [Opportunistic full reconstruction of 100-dimensional frequency-bin quantum states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03496)
*Hanson H. Nguyen,Zachary Goisman,Chen-You Su,Diego Maragnano,Marco Liscidini,Jason D. McKinney,Hsuan-Hao Lu,Joseph M. Lukens*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates threshold quantum state tomography of entangled frequency-bin qudits using adaptive, sparse measurements plus Bayesian inference, reconstructing biphoton frequency combs up to Hilbert space dimension d²=100 with only 1.9% of conventionally required measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frequency-bin encoding enables scalable high-dimensional photonic quantum information processing, but characterizing these systems is hard: conventional quantum state tomography requires measurement resources that grow rapidly with dimensionality, and implementing controllable measurements across many frequency modes simultaneously is difficult.

Method: The authors use adaptive measurements that exploit the sparsity of highly entangled biphoton frequency combs, combined with Bayesian inference, to perform threshold quantum state tomography. The approach is measurement-aware and free from a priori assumptions about state structure.

Result: They experimentally achieve full state estimation of biphoton frequency combs with Hilbert space dimensions up to d²=100—a 56% increase over the previous record—while performing only 1.9% of the measurements required by conventional tomographically complete approaches.

Conclusion: Measurement-aware approaches are promising for characterizing high-dimensional photonic quantum systems, enabling hardware-efficient, state-aware full tomography without a priori assumptions about state structure.

Abstract: Frequency-bin encoding provides a scalable platform for photonic quantum information processing by enabling high-dimensional qudit states compatible with the telecommunications infrastructure. However, characterizing these systems remains challenging, as conventional quantum state tomography requires measurement resources that increase rapidly with dimensionality, compounded by the difficulty of implementing controllable measurements across many frequency modes at once. Here, we address these challenges and demonstrate threshold quantum state tomography of entangled frequency-bin qudits using adaptive measurements that exploit the sparsity of highly entangled biphoton frequency combs. Combined with Bayesian inference, we experimentally demonstrate full state estimation of biphoton frequency combs with Hilbert space dimensions up to $d^2=100$---a 56% increase over the previous record---all while performing a mere 1.9% of the measurements required by conventional tomographically complete approaches. This work confirms the promise of measurement-aware approaches for characterizing high-dimensional photonic quantum systems, unlocking new tools for full tomography that are hardware-efficient, state-aware, and free from a priori assumptions about state structure.

</details>


### [77] [Breaking the chain: geometry-native state preparation with ASPIRE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03528)
*Fredrik Hasselgren,Matthew L. Sims-Goh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: ASPIRE is an algorithm that approximately compiles quantum state-preparation circuits for matrix product states by iteratively selecting long-ranged, parallelised gates based on entanglement geometry and hardware connectivity, achieving higher fidelities and shallower circuits than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Loading data into quantum computers is a key bottleneck threatening end-to-end quantum advantage. Structured states like MPS are critical enablers for quantum chemistry, finance, and many-body simulation, but approximate preparation typically relies on layered sequential staircases of nearest-neighbour gates, which are inefficient for long-ranged entanglement.

Method: ASPIRE compiles state-preparation circuits by selecting gates based on the state's underlying entanglement geometry and the target processor's connectivity. It scores candidate qubit pairs on the removability of their entanglement and identifies layers of parallelised long-ranged gates that remove the most entanglement.

Result: Empirically demonstrated across a range of settings: preparing Hamiltonian ground states and multivariate amplitude-encoded functions, accounting for qubit connectivity, hardware noise, and both NISQ and early fault-tolerant settings. ASPIRE typically achieves higher fidelities and shallower circuits than existing methods for states with long-ranged entanglement, even when classical optimisation is accounted for.

Conclusion: ASPIRE is positioned as a competitive, resource-frugal state-preparation protocol, with particular promise for applications tolerant to imperfect fidelity, such as preparation of guide states for quantum phase estimation.

Abstract: Loading data into quantum computers is a key bottleneck that threatens end-to-end quantum advantage. Quantum-circuit compilers for structured states like matrix product states (MPS) are a critical enabler to manage this in quantum chemistry, finance, and many-body quantum simulation. Approximate preparation of MPS typically relies on layered sequential staircases of nearest-neighbour gates. We present Adaptive State Preparation by Iterative Removal of Entanglement (ASPIRE), an algorithm that approximately compiles state-preparation circuits by selecting gates based on the state's underlying entanglement geometry and the available connectivity of the target quantum processor. Our protocol scores candidate qubit pairs on the removability of their entanglement and identifies layers of parallelised long-ranged gates that remove the most entanglement. We demonstrate our protocol empirically across a range of settings, illustrating its success for preparing Hamiltonian ground states and multivariate amplitude-encoded functions, and accounting for qubit connectivity, hardware noise, and both NISQ and early fault-tolerant settings. Throughout, ASPIRE typically achieves higher fidelities and shallower circuits than existing methods for states with long-ranged entanglement, even when classical optimisation is accounted for. Our investigation positions ASPIRE as a competitive, resource-frugal state-preparation protocol, with particular promise for applications tolerant to imperfect fidelity such as preparation of guide states for quantum phase estimation.

</details>


### [78] [What Must a Quantum-Memory Decoder Know About Temporally Correlated Noise?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03545)
*Danesh Morales-Hashemi,Graeme Smith,Ningping Cao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies what a quantum error-correction decoder must know about temporally correlated noise, formulating calibration via fidelity differences and proving a sharp separation: two models with identical syndrome statistics and the same twirled Pauli process need different logical corrections, with worst-case fidelity loss approaching 1/2 without model information, and calibration costs scaling as inverse rotation angle to the X distance (or inverse-square with two uninterrupted intervals).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decoders are typically designed assuming they know the noise model, but real noise is temporally correlated and its parameters must be learned. The paper asks what a decoder must know about temporally correlated noise and what it costs to learn that information, for fixed stabilizer memory experiments with known system-environment interactions.

Method: Calibration is formulated in terms of the fidelity differences that select a Pauli recovery, relating missing information to fidelity loss. The main example uses weak coherent X rotations in a class of CSS codes (Shor, Steane, odd-distance rotated surface codes), where all physical qubits share a sign either fixed throughout a run or independently redrawn each interval. Both models are compared via syndrome statistics and the spatiotemporal Pauli process (SPP) obtained by Pauli-twirling each noise interval.

Result: The two models have identical syndrome statistics and the same SPP, yet at suitable storage times require different logical corrections. In the weak-noise limit each model is almost perfectly correctable when known, but without model information even the best final recovery suffers worst-case fidelity loss approaching 1/2 relative to the noise-aware optimum despite full syndrome access. Matching calibration bounds are proven for this model pair at fixed code and fixed positive target loss below 1/2. Complete QEC cycles with logical preparation and readout require a number of noise intervals scaling as the inverse rotation angle raised to the code's X distance; allowing two uninterrupted intervals before one extraction reduces the optimal cost to inverse-square scaling.

Conclusion: Physical Pauli twirling of every noise interval changes both recovery performance and its information requirements: discarding the record of applied random Pauli operations makes the SPP sufficient for optimal recovery, while keeping this record can improve attainable fidelity but reaching that optimum may require additional noise information.

Abstract: What must a quantum error-correction decoder know about temporally correlated noise, and what does it cost to learn? We study this question for fixed stabilizer memory experiments with known system-environment interactions. We formulate calibration in terms of the fidelity differences that select a Pauli recovery and relate missing information to the resulting fidelity loss. Our main example uses weak coherent $X$ rotations in a class of CSS codes including Shor, Steane, and odd-distance rotated surface codes. All physical qubits share a sign either fixed throughout a run or independently redrawn each interval. The two models have identical syndrome statistics and the same spatiotemporal Pauli process (SPP), obtained by Pauli-twirling every noise interval. Yet at suitable storage times, they require different logical corrections. In the weak-noise limit, each model is almost perfectly correctable when known. Without model information, even the best final recovery has a worst-case fidelity loss approaching $1/2$ relative to the noise-aware optimum, despite access to the full syndrome record. For this pair of models, at fixed code and fixed positive target loss below $1/2$, we prove matching calibration bounds. With logical preparation and final readout, complete QEC cycles require a number of noise intervals scaling as the inverse rotation angle raised to the code's $X$ distance. Allowing two uninterrupted intervals before one extraction reduces the optimal cost to inverse-square scaling. Physical Pauli twirling of every noise interval changes both recovery performance and its information requirements. Discarding the record of applied random Pauli operations makes the SPP sufficient for optimal recovery. Keeping this record can improve the attainable fidelity, but reaching that optimum may require additional noise information.

</details>


### [79] [A graph-theoretic analysis of non-generic free-fermion solvability by Krylov decompositions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03557)
*Jannis Ruh,Samuel J. Elman,Adrian Chapman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper characterizes a family of quantum spin Hamiltonians that are exactly solvable only at finely tuned couplings, showing that these non-generic free-fermion solutions correspond to Krylov currents whose structure is captured by induced paths of the frustration graph.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exactly solvable spin Hamiltonians are usually identified via structural features of their interactions, captured by the frustration graph of anticommuting Pauli terms, which characterize models mapping to free fermions for generic couplings. However, some models admit free-fermion solutions only at non-generic, finely tuned couplings, and a graph-theoretic characterization of such non-generically solvable models was lacking. This work aims to fill that gap.

Method: Given an arbitrary instance of Fendley's free-fermions-in-disguise (FFD) chain, the authors construct a set of operators called Krylov currents that are exactly quadratic in the effective fermionic modes, extending the model to a linear family of free-fermion solvable Hamiltonians. They analyze the structure of these currents in terms of the frustration graph, proving that each Krylov current admits an expansion in terms of induced paths of the graph and that the currents generate the full special-orthogonal algebra of Gaussian transformations.

Result: The Krylov currents are nonlinear in the FFD Hamiltonian terms, so the generated family members are non-generically solvable and fail known conditions for generic solvability. Every Krylov current expands in terms of induced paths of the frustration graph, and collectively they generate the full special-orthogonal algebra of Gaussian transformations.

Conclusion: Path support in the frustration graph is identified as a signature of non-generic solvability, providing a systematic route to finding exactly solvable many-body Hamiltonians.

Abstract: Exactly solvable models are often identified from structural features of their interactions. For quantum spin Hamiltonians, these are captured by the frustration graph of anticommuting Pauli terms. The frustration graph characterizes broad families of spin models that map to free fermions for arbitrary, or generic, values of their coupling strengths. Nevertheless, there exist non-generically solvable models that admit free-fermion solutions only at finely tuned couplings. In this work, we characterize a family of such models in terms of graph structures. Given an arbitrary instance of Fendley's free-fermions-in-disguise (FFD) chain, we construct a set of operators, called Krylov currents, that are exactly quadratic in its effective fermionic modes and thus extend it to a linear family of free-fermion solvable Hamiltonians. As the Krylov currents are nonlinear in the FFD Hamiltonian terms, the elements of this generated family are non-generically solvable, and they accordingly fail the known conditions for generic solvability. We prove that every Krylov current admits an expansion in terms of induced paths of the frustration graph and that they generate the full special-orthogonal algebra of Gaussian transformations. Our results identify path support as a signature of non-generic solvability, and they provide a systematic route to finding exactly solvable many-body Hamiltonians.

</details>


### [80] [Gibbs state preparation through quantum decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03566)
*Christophe Piveteau,Yihui Quek,M. Isabel Franco Garrido*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces QD-HDQI, a Gibbs state preparation algorithm that replaces the classical decoder in Hamiltonian Decoded Quantum Interferometry with a quantum decoder, improving achievable inverse temperature thresholds and, via BPQM, reaching temperatures beyond classical decoding limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gibbs state preparation is a central task in quantum computing and simulation. HDQI recently reframed this problem for Pauli Hamiltonians as decoding error-correcting codes, but its reliance on a coherent classical decoder limits the achievable inverse temperature to linear in the correctable error fraction and cannot exploit quantum interference between errors.

Method: The authors define quantum decoding HDQI (QD-HDQI), substituting HDQI's coherent classical decoder with a quantum decoder benefiting from interference among errors. For commuting Pauli Hamiltonians they formulate the reduction as transmission of a symplectic code over i.i.d. pure-state classical-quantum channels, where the decoder's vanishing-error threshold sets the reachable temperature. They analyze two quantum decoders: one based on unambiguous state discrimination (efficient for arbitrary binary linear codes, and dequantized to a classical rejection sampler for commuting Hamiltonians), and belief propagation with quantum messages (BPQM).

Result: For commuting Pauli Hamiltonians the reduction is efficient, and with uniformly random Hamiltonian signs the decoder threshold directly determines the minimum achievable temperature. Even with only classical decoders, QD-HDQI improves the achievable inverse temperature scaling from linear to square-root in the correctable error fraction; quantum decoders tolerate stronger noise. The noncommuting regime reduces to correlated noise within small independent blocks. The unambiguous-state-discrimination decoder works efficiently for arbitrary binary linear codes but is dequantized to a classical rejection sampler matching its temperature threshold. For certain dilute spin glasses, BPQM's decoding threshold exceeds the classical Shannon limit, reaching temperatures inaccessible to any classical decoder in this framework.

Conclusion: Exploiting algebraic Hamiltonian structure via quantum decoding yields a strictly more powerful Gibbs state preparation framework: it improves temperature thresholds over classical decoding, admits efficient dequantized classical counterparts, and, through BPQM, surpasses the classical Shannon limit for specific dilute spin glasses.

Abstract: We propose a Gibbs state preparation algorithm that exploits algebraic structure in the Hamiltonian. Our starting point is Hamiltonian Decoded Quantum Interferometry (HDQI), a recent algorithm that reduces Gibbs state preparation for Pauli Hamiltonians to decoding error-correcting codes. We introduce quantum decoding HDQI (QD-HDQI), replacing HDQI's coherent classical decoder by a quantum decoder that can exploit interference between different errors.
  For commuting Pauli Hamiltonians, our reduction is efficient and the associated quantum decoding problem corresponds to transmitting the associated symplectic code over i.i.d. pure-state classical-quantum channels. For uniformly random Hamiltonian signs, the vanishing-error threshold of an efficient decoder directly determines the lowest temperature that our algorithm can reach. Even using only classical decoders, this improves upon HDQI's achievable inverse temperature from linear to square-root in the correctable error fraction; quantum decoders can improve further by tolerating still stronger noise. A similar picture extends to the noncommuting regime previously handled by HDQI, where now the noise is correlated within small, independent blocks.
  We study two such quantum decoders in our QD-HDQI Gibbs sampling framework. The first, based on unambiguous state discrimination, works efficiently for arbitrary binary linear codes. However, we "dequantize" this strategy for commuting Hamiltonians, contributing a classical rejection sampler that matches its temperature threshold. The second is belief propagation with quantum messages (BPQM), a quantum generalization of classical belief propagation. We show that for certain dilute spin glasses, BPQM's decoding threshold exceeds the Shannon limit for classical decoding, reaching temperatures inaccessible to any classical decoder within our framework.

</details>


### [81] [Rapid mixing of Gibbs samplers via quantum Dobrushin--Shlosman conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03578)
*Cambyse Rouzé,Daniel Stilck França*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends classical Dobrushin–Shlosman block-update theory to noncommuting quantum lattice systems via a "smoothed heat-bath dynamics" condition, proving log trace-norm mixing for finite-range quantum spin chains at fixed finite temperature and stability under small local perturbations, yielding efficient Gibbs-state preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Dobrushin–Shlosman theory relies on heat-bath block updates to derive sharp temperature thresholds for rapid mixing of Gibbs samplers, often succeeding where stricter single-site conditions fail. The authors aim to bring this block-update machinery to noncommuting quantum lattice systems, where analogous mixing guarantees are lacking.

Method: They formulate a quantum Dobrushin–Shlosman condition for finite-block dynamics ("smoothed heat-bath dynamics") that combines a local Gibbs reset with quantum belief propagation to account for interactions across block boundaries, producing exactly Gibbs-preserving, KMS-reversible channels. Two independently tunable parameters govern contraction: an internal evolution time suppressing intra-block site influence, and the block size letting interior contraction dominate boundary influence.

Result: For finite-range quantum spin chains at every fixed finite temperature, they prove logarithmic trace-norm mixing in system size, with normalized bounds uniform in on-site fields and single-exponential in inverse temperature. They also prove stability under small local perturbations of interacting (possibly noncommuting) reference Hamiltonians on graphs of polynomial volume growth. For bounded-interaction classical references, uniform strong spatial mixing implies their quantum condition. With bounded local terms and fixed parameters, the samplers prepare Gibbs states to trace-norm error ε using N polylog(N/ε) gates and classical operations.

Conclusion: The quantum Dobrushin–Shlosman framework generalizes classical block-update mixing theory to noncommuting quantum systems, enabling efficient Gibbs-state preparation and providing a finite-time relaxation mechanism (via internal evolution time) that has no classical heat-bath counterpart.

Abstract: Classical Dobrushin--Shlosman theory uses heat-bath block updates to identify sharp temperature thresholds for establishing the rapid mixing of Gibbs samplers. In particular, these mixing guarantees often hold even when stricter single-site conditions fail. We extend this approach to noncommuting quantum lattice systems through a quantum Dobrushin--Shlosman condition for finite-block dynamics, which we call smoothed heat-bath dynamics. We establish this condition in two regimes. For finite-range quantum spin chains at every fixed finite temperature, we prove logarithmic trace-norm mixing in system size, with normalized bounds uniform in on-site fields and single exponential in inverse temperature. We also prove stability under small local perturbations of interacting, possibly noncommuting reference Hamiltonians on graphs of polynomial volume growth. For classical references with bounded local interactions, uniform strong spatial mixing implies our quantum condition. With bounded local terms and fixed physical, geometric and block parameters, the resulting samplers prepare Gibbs states to trace-norm error $\varepsilon$ using $N\operatorname{polylog}(N/\varepsilon)$ gates and classical operations.
  Our block updates combine a local Gibbs reset with quantum belief propagation to incorporate interactions across the block boundary, yielding exactly Gibbs-preserving, KMS-reversible channels. Two independently tunable parameters control contraction: an internal evolution time suppresses the influence of sites inside a block, while the block size allows interior contraction to dominate boundary influence. This finite-time relaxation has no counterpart in a classical heat-bath block update.

</details>


### [82] [From Symmetry to Secrecy: Covariant Classical--Quantum Wiretap Channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03580)
*Masahito Hayashi,Farzin Salek*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs finite-block preprocessing schemes for classical-quantum wiretap channels with transitive covariant input action, exploiting unitary group orbit structure to express private information as an entropy difference between group-convolution-related distributions, and applies these to product groups for multi-use correlations. It derives a uniform-input bound, achieves rates via constant-composition codes and universal$_2$ hashing with leakage controlled by sandwiched Rényi information, and demonstrates improved rates and nonadditivity for the hybrid binary channel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical-quantum wiretap channels with transitive covariant input action possess a rich symmetry structure that has not been fully exploited for finite-block preprocessing and multi-use coding. The goal is to leverage this group-theoretic structure to construct practical preprocessing schemes, understand when multi-use private information matches single-use, and improve achievable rates—particularly for the hybrid binary channel studied by Tikku, Berta, and Renes.

Method: The authors describe output ensembles as unitary group orbits of fixed seed states and construct finite-block preprocessing schemes whose private information equals an entropy difference evaluated at two distributions related by group convolution. They apply the construction to product groups to capture correlations across a fixed number of channel uses, derive a sufficient condition for the uniform-input bound to equal single-use private information, and use constant-composition channel codes with universal$_2$ hashing, controlling trace-distance leakage via sandwiched Rényi information. For uniform outer randomization, representation structure reduces the comparison-state optimization to Eve's invariant states. Numerical optimization is performed for the hybrid binary channel at three and four uses, and nonadditivity is proven via a two-use encoder with an analytic single-use converse and certified entropy bounds.

Result: For the hybrid binary channel, numerical optimization at three and four uses yields rates above the numerical estimate of the optimized noisy-repetition benchmark wherever that estimate is positive on the tested grid, with improvement arising from noise distributions beyond independent physical flips plus a symmetric logical flip. The paper also proves nonadditivity at an explicit channel parameter using a two-use encoder, an analytic single-use converse, and certified entropy bounds. Additionally, the hybrid model is related to binary phase-shift keying with a fixed receiver measurement.

Conclusion: The transitive covariant structure enables systematic construction of multi-use preprocessing and coding schemes with entropy-difference private information, achieving the uniform-input bound under a sufficient condition. Constant-composition codes and universal$_2$ hashing attain the resulting rates. For the hybrid binary channel, the approach surpasses the noisy-repetition benchmark and establishes nonadditivity, and the hybrid model connects to binary phase-shift keying with a fixed receiver measurement.

Abstract: For classical--quantum wiretap channels with a transitive covariant input action, both output ensembles admit a description as unitary group orbits of fixed seed states. We use this structure to construct finite-block preprocessing schemes whose private information is an entropy difference evaluated at two distributions related by group convolution. Applying the construction to product groups allows correlations across any fixed number of channel uses. We give a sufficient condition for the associated uniform-input bound to equal the single-use private information. Constant-composition channel codes and universal$_2$ hashing achieve the resulting rates, with trace-distance leakage controlled by sandwiched Rényi information. For uniform outer randomization, the representation structure reduces the comparison-state optimization to Eve's invariant states. For the hybrid binary channel considered by Tikku, Berta and Renes, numerical optimization at three and four uses gives rates above our numerical estimate of the optimized noisy-repetition benchmark wherever that estimate is positive on the tested grid. The improvement comes from noise distributions beyond the family of independent physical flips and a symmetric logical flip. We also prove nonadditivity at an explicit channel parameter using a two-use encoder, an analytic single-use converse, and certified entropy bounds. Finally, we relate the hybrid model to binary phase-shift keying with a fixed receiver measurement.

</details>


### [83] [Quantum algorithms for orthogonal polynomial transforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03581)
*Anupam Prakash,Shree Hari Sureshbabu,Dylan Herman,Shouvanik Chakrabarti*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper constructs efficient quantum orthogonal polynomial transforms (QOPTs) for multiple families in the Askey scheme, achieving polylogarithmic runtime for discrete Charlier, Meixner, Krawtchouk, and integer-order Laguerre transforms, and a quadratic speedup for the Hahn transform via improved Clebsch-Gordan compilation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum orthogonal polynomial transforms are algorithmic primitives mapping superpositions of standard basis states to polynomial bases orthogonal under a measure μ(x). Prior work only covered the quantum Hermite transform; extending efficient QOPTs to other Askey scheme families enables broader quantum algorithmic applications.

Method: The authors reveal links between Askey scheme polynomials and Gaussian quantum optical gates, and build a compilation framework for SU(2) and SU(1,1) optical gates on Cartesian grids. They reduce the continuous Jacobi transform to the discrete Hahn transform via a more efficient compilation of the Clebsch-Gordan transform for coupling SU(2) representations with spins (j1, j2). For general-order Laguerre transforms, they fast-forward the radial oscillator using a 3-term chirp decomposition and an efficient Quantum Hankel Transform on a logarithmic grid.

Result: Efficient QOPTs run in O(polylog(N, 1/ε)) time for discrete Charlier, Meixner, Krawtchouk, and integer-order Laguerre transforms. The Hahn transform runs in O(N polylog((N+α+β+1)/ε)) time, a quadratic speedup over naive implementation. A framework is also developed for Laguerre transforms of all orders ν>0.

Conclusion: The work extends efficient QOPTs across the Askey scheme using quantum optical gate compilations and related techniques, delivering polylogarithmic algorithms for several families and a quadratically faster Hahn transform, thereby broadening the set of primitives available for quantum algorithms.

Abstract: A quantum orthogonal polynomial transform (QOPT) is an algorithmic primitive that maps a superposition of standard basis states $\sum_k α_{k} \ket{k}$ coherently to a basis of normalized univariate polynomials orthogonal with respect to a probability measure $μ(x)$. We provide new discrete and continuous QOPTs for polynomial families in the Askey scheme extending the results for the quantum Hermite transform (Jain et al., STOC'26). Our efficient QOPT algorithms require time $O(\text{polylog}(N, 1/ε))$, where $N$ is the grid size and $ε$ is the error, for the discrete Charlier, Meixner and Krawtchouk transforms and for the integer-order Laguerre transform in the continuous setting. The efficient QOPTs are obtained by uncovering the links between Askey scheme polynomials and Gaussian quantum optical gates and developing a compilation framework for $SU(2)$ and $SU(1,1)$ optical gates on Cartesian grids extending the framework developed by Iyer et al. (arXiv:2602.15180). Further, we reduce the continuous Jacobi transform to the discrete Hahn transform and provide an $O\!\left(N\operatorname{polylog}((N+α+β+1)/ε)\right)$-time Hahn transform, a quadratic speedup over the naive implementation. This is based on a more efficient compilation of the Clebsch--Gordan transform for coupling $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ representations with spins $(j_{1}, j_{2})$. Finally, we develop a new framework for Laguerre transforms for all orders $ν>0$ by fast-forwarding the corresponding radial oscillator using a 3-term chirp decomposition and an efficient algorithm for the Quantum Hankel Transform on a logarithmic grid.

</details>


### [84] [Robustness of quantum spectrum estimation: weak Schur sampling under noisy inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03582)
*Vladyslav Visnevskyi,Laura Mančinska*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper initiates the study of weak Schur sampling (WSS) under noisy input, proving that per-copy errors do not accumulate and establishing noise-robustness for quantum spectrum estimation and quantum state tomography.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many optimal quantum learning algorithms (e.g., quantum spectrum estimation and quantum state tomography) rely on weak Schur sampling, and their standard analysis assumes that the n input copies of the target state are identical. This assumption is unrealistic, and noise breaks the permutation symmetry on which WSS is built. The naive data-processing argument suggests errors accumulate linearly as nε, which is problematic for large inputs. The authors aim to understand WSS under a more realistic noisy scenario.

Method: The authors analyze WSS when the n input copies form a product state, with each copy ε-close in trace distance to the d-dimensional target state ρ. They prove bounds on the expected output error in total variation. Beyond product states, they show that the outcome observables of WSS are 1/n-Lipschitz with respect to the quantum Wasserstein distance W1 of De Palma et al., yielding a general error bound for arbitrary inputs ω.

Result: The expected output error in total variation is at most ε + η(n,d), where η(n,d) = O(d/√n) is the noiseless error and the additive noise term ε is optimal. For arbitrary inputs ω, the error is at most η(n,d) + (1/n)||ω - ρ^⊗n||_{W1}. These results imply, for the first time, noise-robustness of Keyl and Werner's seminal QSE algorithm, more than two decades after its introduction. The authors also show that within their model, the promise cannot be weakened to closeness of single-copy marginals or to a global trace-distance budget alone, since in both cases some noisy inputs defeat every measurement and estimator.

Conclusion: WSS is robust to noise: per-copy errors do not accumulate, and the error remains close to the noiseless error plus an optimal additive ε term. This provides noise-robustness for QSE and represents a necessary step towards optimal quantum learning in practice, with potential to help establish robustness for many other problems in quantum information theory.

Abstract: We initiate the study of weak Schur sampling (WSS) under noisy input. Many optimal quantum learning algorithms rely on this measurement, for instance in such fundamental tasks as quantum spectrum estimation (QSE) and quantum state tomography (QST). The standard analysis of WSS, QSE and QST assumes that the $n$ input copies of the target state $ρ$ are identical. We study what happens when they are not, a more realistic noisy scenario that breaks the very permutation symmetry on which WSS is built. In the simplest noise model, the copies are independent but not necessarily identical, i.e. they form a product state, and each is $ε$-close in trace distance to the $d$-dimensional target state $ρ$. A naive data-processing argument lets the per-copy errors accumulate to $nε$ on measurement output, growing with the input size. We prove that they do not accumulate: the expected output error in total variation is at most $ε+η(n,d)$, where $η(n,d)=O(d/\sqrt n)$ is the noiseless error, and the additive noise term $ε$ is optimal. Beyond noisy product states, for arbitrary inputs $ω$ we show that the outcome observables of WSS are $1/n$-Lipschitz with respect to the quantum Wasserstein distance $W_1$ of De Palma et al., so the error is at most $η(n,d)+\frac{1}{n}\|ω-ρ^{\otimes n}\|_{W_1}$. In particular, our results imply, for the first time, noise-robustness of Keyl and Werner's seminal algorithm for QSE, more than two decades after its introduction. Within our model of robustness, the promise cannot be weakened to closeness of single-copy marginals or to a global trace-distance budget alone, since in both cases some noisy inputs defeat every measurement and estimator. Our results are a necessary step towards optimal quantum learning in practice, and help make progress towards robustness of many other problems in quantum information theory.

</details>


### [85] [A Complete Proof of $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-Hardness for Weighted Gapped Clique Homology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03584)
*Tal Barak*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper provides a complete proof of the hard direction for the $\mathsf{QMA}_1$ hardness of weighted, spectrally gapped clique homology, repairing a gap in the King-Kohler reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kaibel and Pfetsch raised the question of the computational complexity of simplicial homology; Crichigno and Kohler later showed clique homology to be $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-hard. King and Kohler introduced a weighted, spectrally gapped version and sketched a reduction to establish the same hardness, but their argument contained a gap in the analysis of nontrivially folded local gadgets, leaving the hardness result unproven.

Method: The authors re-establish the required topological and spectral properties directly on the clique complexes produced by the reduction. They use exact finite certificates for the local combinatorial data and relative homology to determine each gadget's effect on the encoded qubit space, prove the finite-dimensional filtered-Hodge statement needed to recover the rank-one low-energy penalty, and derive a quantitative global estimate transferring the source-Hamiltonian promise gap to the final weighted Hodge Laplacian.

Result: For fixed efficiently computable $k(n)$ and inverse-polynomial $\gamma(n)$, weighted gapped clique homology is $\mathsf{QMA}_1(\mathcal G)$-hard. The previously known $\mathsf{QMA}$-containment argument is logically independent of the gap and remains unaffected.

Conclusion: The hardness proof for weighted gapped clique homology is completed; the repair is not a local fix but requires new analysis of the local topology, filtered cochain complexes, and overlapping gadget interactions.

Abstract: The computational complexity of simplicial homology has been studied since the question was raised by Kaibel and Pfetsch, and clique homology was later shown to be $\mathsf{QMA}_1$-hard by Crichigno and Kohler. King and Kohler subsequently introduced the weighted, spectrally gapped version considered here and developed a reduction intended to establish the same hardness result in this setting.
  We give a complete proof of the hard direction. The earlier King-Kohler argument contains a gap in the analysis of the nontrivially folded local gadgets, so the required topological and spectral properties have to be re-established on the clique complexes actually produced by the reduction. This is not a local repair of the published proof: the argument requires a new analysis of the local topology, the filtered cochain complexes, and the interaction of many overlapping gadgets.
  Our proof uses exact finite certificates for the local combinatorial data and relative homology to determine the effect of each gadget on the encoded qubit space. We then prove the finite-dimensional filtered-Hodge statement needed to recover the corresponding rank-one low-energy penalty and derive a quantitative global estimate that transfers the source-Hamiltonian promise gap to the final weighted Hodge Laplacian. Consequently, for fixed efficiently computable $k(n)$ and inverse-polynomial $γ(n)$, weighted gapped clique homology is $\mathsf{QMA}_1(\mathcal G)$-hard. The previously known $\mathsf{QMA}$-containment argument is logically independent of the gap and remains unaffected.

</details>


### [86] [Lifting Multiplicity in Randomized Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03586)
*Yale Fan,J. P. Marceaux*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces "lifted randomized benchmarking," an intrinsic method to handle multiplicity in randomized benchmarking without fitting multi-exponential decays, remaining robust to SPAM errors, and demonstrated on two-qubit trapped-ion gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard randomized benchmarking reliably assesses quantum processor fidelity only for groups whose process matrices are free of multiplicity. Many groups formed from native gates exhibit multiplicity, making robust conclusions from their data difficult.

Method: Lifted randomized benchmarking generalizes key elements of character and synthetic randomized benchmarking to benchmark a target group with respect to an enlarged group, intrinsically eliminating multiplicity and avoiding multi-exponential decay fitting while remaining robust to state preparation and measurement errors.

Result: Numerical examples show the method efficiently and reliably estimates the fidelity of two-qubit gates relevant to experimental trapped-ion systems.

Conclusion: The techniques may extend beyond randomized benchmarking to quantum tomography, learning, and characterization of error-correcting codes.

Abstract: Randomized benchmarking comprises a suite of standard techniques for assessing the operational fidelity of quantum processors. These techniques can be reliably applied to groups with process matrix representations that are free of multiplicity. However, many groups that are naturally formed from native gates do have multiplicity, and it is difficult to draw robust conclusions from the resulting data. In this work, we present a new and intrinsic solution to the problem of multiplicity that completely eliminates the need to fit multi-exponential decays while still remaining robust to errors in state preparation and measurement. Our procedure, which we call lifted randomized benchmarking, generalizes key elements of both character and synthetic randomized benchmarking to benchmark a target group of quantum gates with respect to an enlarged group. We illustrate in numerical examples that our method can efficiently and reliably estimate the fidelity of two-qubit gates relevant to experimental trapped-ion systems. These techniques may find applications beyond randomized benchmarking to quantum tomography and learning, as well as the characterization of error-correcting codes.

</details>


### [87] [Quantum Fire with Delegated Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03593)
*Rohit Chatterjee,Ananta Mukherjee,Vir Pathak,Supartha Podder*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces delegatable quantum fire, a primitive where cloning of quantum "flame" states requires a separate key, enabling access control. Two classical-oracle-model constructions are given: one with a classical cloning secret key, and one (torch-fire) with non-splittable quantum cloning keys ("torches"), achieving a rigorous bound that m torches enable at most m noncommunicating parties to clone fresh challenge flames.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior quantum fire constructions have cloning as a public, keyless operation: any holder of a flame can clone it. This is unsuitable for access-control applications where an issuer wants to permit cloning only by designated quantum servers while denying it to other flame holders. Hence a notion of cloning that is gated by a separate key is needed.

Method: The authors define delegatable quantum fire, in which cloning requires a separate key. They provide two constructions in the classical-oracle model, relying on public classical oracles queryable in quantum superposition. The first uses a classical secret key for cloning, and any user holding the full key can clone. The second, torch-fire, uses quantum cloning keys ("torches") that enable cloning yet remain unaffected by the cloning process and cannot be split or delegated for additional cloning. Security is analyzed against an efficient adversary that, given m torches, may jointly process its resources, distribute arbitrarily entangled registers among parties, and then attempt to let more than m noncommunicating parties each clone a fresh, independently issued challenge flame.

Result: Two constructions are obtained. The classical-secret-key construction allows cloning by any user possessing the entire key. The torch-fire construction satisfies the stated bound: given m torches, an efficient adversary cannot, except with negligible probability, enable more than m noncommunicating parties to each clone a fresh, independently issued challenge flame, even with joint preprocessing and arbitrary entanglement distribution.

Conclusion: Delegatable quantum fire is achievable, allowing issuers to gate cloning capability via either classical secret keys or non-splittable quantum torches. The torch-fire construction yields a quantitative, entanglement-resistant bound on cloning capacity, showing that cloning rights cannot be amplified beyond the number of torches held. Both results hold in the classical-oracle model with quantum-superposition queries.

Abstract: Quantum fire is a recently introduced cryptographic primitive consisting of efficiently preparable quantum states, called \emph{flames}, that admit efficient cloning but resist efficient telegraphing, namely reconstruction via classical communication without preshared entanglement. In all prior constructions of quantum fire, cloning is a public operation that requires no separate key and every holder of a flame state can clone it. For applications to access control, however, an issuer may wish to delegate cloning to designated quantum servers while withholding this capability from other flame holders. To address this, we introduce \emph{delegatable quantum fire}, in which cloning requires a separate key.
  We give two constructions in the classical-oracle model. Our first construction uses a classical secret key which enables cloning, and any user with the entire key may clone successfully. Our second construction, which we call \emph{torch-fire}, uses quantum cloning keys, called \emph{torches}, which enable cloning while remaining unaffected in the process but cannot otherwise be split or delegated to enable additional cloning. An efficient adversary given $m$ torches cannot, except with negligible probability, enable more than $m$ noncommunicating parties to each clone a fresh, independently issued challenge flame. The adversary may jointly process its resources before separating the parties and distribute arbitrarily entangled registers among them. Both constructions are in the oracle model, relying on public classical oracles that allow queries in quantum superposition.

</details>


### [88] [Fault-tolerant and fully addressable unitary logical gates via round-robin sparsification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03594)
*Kaavya Sahay,Cody Jones*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces explicit low-depth unitary circuits that enable fully addressable, fault-tolerant two-qubit Clifford gates for a subset of 2D translationally invariant qLDPC codes, constructed via geometric sparsification of round-robin circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: qLDPC codes offer efficient encoding, but their utility is maximized only if individual logical qubits can be manipulated, i.e., addressable gates are needed. Existing approaches lack explicit low-depth circuits realizing such addressable and fault-tolerant gates.

Method: The authors design transversal unitaries by applying a geometrically structured sparsification procedure to round-robin circuits, yielding low-depth unitary circuits for two-qubit Clifford gates on a subset of 2D translationally invariant qLDPC codes.

Result: They validate the protocol by proposing and numerically simulating addressable CZs across toric, cyclic hypergraph-product, color, gross, and directional codes, showing fault-tolerant memory-like logical error rate scaling.

Conclusion: The protocol provides generally applicable, explicit, low-depth, fully addressable, and fault-tolerant two-qubit Clifford gates for a broad class of 2D translationally invariant qLDPC codes.

Abstract: For quantum low-density parity check (qLDPC) codes, the ability to manipulate individual logical units of information, i.e. the use of addressable gates, enables maximal utility of their efficient encoding. We introduce explicit low-depth unitary circuits that realize fully addressable and fault-tolerant two-qubit Clifford gates for a subset of two-dimensional translationally invariant qLDPC codes. Our transversal unitaries are created by applying a geometrically structured sparsification procedure to round-robin circuits. We verify the general applicability of our protocol by proposing and numerically simulating a range of addressable CZs between toric, cyclic hypergraph-product, color, gross, and directional codes, demonstrating fault-tolerant memory-like logical error rate scaling.

</details>


### [89] [The exact LCU sampling overhead of collective diagonal unitaries: resonances and a continued-fraction dichotomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03595)
*Zhihui Wang,Sujit Roy,Manil Maskey,Rahul Ramachandran*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper determines the minimal sampling overhead Γ for implementing the quadratic collective phase e^{-iγK²} as an LCU of single-qubit rotation layers, showing its growth is governed by the continued fraction of γ/π and establishing optimality of known Fourier-based and Kerr-optics decompositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quadratic collective phase is a key primitive across constrained quantum optimization (cardinality penalty), spin squeezing (one-axis twisting), and bosonic simulation (Kerr phase), but its exact/approximate cost as an LCU of single-qubit layers was not sharply quantified. The work seeks the minimal overhead and to characterize when it is small, polynomial, or exponential.

Method: Model the gate as ancilla-free sampling of single-qubit rotation layers (LCU) with overhead Γ; derive lower bounds via certificate-lifting arguments and tight two-sided "master formula" analyses; characterize dependence on register size n and on the number-theoretic properties (continued fraction, bad approximability) of γ/π; compare to existing Fourier-based LCU and fractional-revival/Kerr decompositions.

Result: Rational angles γ/π = p/q (lowest terms) cost exactly Γ = q for every n ≥ 2q−2; cost is Θ(n) iff γ/π is badly approximable; a master formula (proved two-sided near low-denominator rationals) gives the cost from the nearest rational. Arbitrary single-qubit gates cannot improve the permutation-symmetric case. Without symmetry, cost can be exponential: for K = Σ 2^j x_j, the m-qubit squaring phase costs at least 2^{0.557m−O(1)}, exceeding the 2^{m/2} cut-bound ceiling. The Fourier LCU of Carrera Vazquez et al. (Γ ≤ n+1) is optimal up to a constant at typical angles at fixed n, with the fractional-revival decomposition being the unique optimizer at rational angles (independent of n). In bosonic simulation, the Kerr-gate phase-shifter decomposition is the unique optimum over phase shifters, finite-cost only at rational γ/π.

Conclusion: The minimal LCU overhead for the quadratic collective phase is controlled by the continued fraction of γ/π, with rational, badly-approximable, and general angles giving distinct polynomial/exponential cost regimes. Existing Fourier-based and Kerr-optics decompositions are shown to be optimal (up to constants or uniquely), unifying the cost of this gate across quantum optimization, spin squeezing, and bosonic simulation.

Abstract: We consider the cost of implementing the quadratic collective phase $e^{-iγK^2}$, with $K$ a collective observable of evenly spaced spectrum, such as the permutation-symmetric Hamming weight. This gate is the cardinality-penalty layer of constrained quantum optimization, the one-axis-twisting gate of spin squeezing and the Kerr phase of a bosonic mode. Instead of two-qubit gates, it can be sampled as a linear combination of single-qubit rotation layers (an LCU) at an overhead $Γ$. We determine the minimal overhead. Ancilla-free sampling of the layers reproduces the target's outcome probabilities up to this factor, and no smaller factor works for every input; when $K$ is permutation symmetric, arbitrary single-qubit gates do no better. The minimal overhead's growth with the register size $n$ is set by the continued fraction of $γ/π$: rational angles $πp/q$ in lowest terms cost exactly $Γ=q$ for every $n\ge2q-2$; the cost is $Θ(n)$ if and only if $γ/π$ is badly approximable; and a master formula, proved two-sided near low-denominator rationals, reads it off the nearest one. Without the symmetry the cost over single-qubit gates can be exponential: for the binary-weighted $K=\sum_j2^jx_j$ the squaring phase of Fourier arithmetic on $m$ qubits costs at least $2^{0.557m-O(1)}$, beyond the $2^{m/2}$ ceiling of every cut bound, by a new certificate-lifting argument. The Fourier-based LCU of Carrera Vazquez, Egger and Woerner (2026), which guarantees $Γ\le n+1$, is optimal up to a constant factor at typical angles at fixed $n$; at rational angles the unique optimizer is the fractional-revival decomposition of Kerr optics, a recipe independent of $n$. In bosonic simulation, the Kerr-gate phase-shifter decomposition of Upreti, Quesada and Chabaud (2026) is the unique optimum over phase shifters, and a finite-cost one exists only at rational $γ/π$.

</details>


### [90] [Efficient fidelity simulation of high-rate magic distillation circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03605)
*Xiao Xiao,Dominik Hangleiter,J. Pablo Bonilla Ataides,Rohan Mehta,Varun Menon,Mikhail D. Lukin,Michael J. Gullans*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops exact and efficient classical benchmarking methods for fault-tolerant circuits composed of X, CNOT, and diagonal Clifford-hierarchy gates (including teleportation gadgets with feedforward), avoiding logical measurement simulation. The algorithm scales polynomially in physical qubits and circuit depth, applies to IQP sampling and magic state preparation, and is demonstrated by optimizing high-rate magic state distillation factories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Clifford gates are essential to escape classical simulability (per the Gottesman-Knill theorem), making error-corrected circuits with such gates crucial for fault-tolerant quantum algorithms. However, precisely evaluating the fault-tolerant performance of these circuits is difficult because classically simulating them is hard, especially when logical measurements are involved.

Method: They avoid simulating logical measurements and instead devise exact, efficient classical methods for benchmarking circuits built from X, CNOT, and diagonal gates in the Clifford hierarchy, covering state and gate teleportation gadgets with feedforward. For third-level diagonal gates, the algorithm efficiently samples syndrome statistics and estimates output logical fidelity and expectation values of certain Clifford observables (including all Pauli observables). Syndrome expectation evaluation is extended to fourth-level diagonal gates. Complexity is polynomial in the number of physical qubits n and circuit depth T, and independent of the number of logical qubits.

Result: The benchmarking algorithm efficiently samples syndrome statistics and estimates logical fidelity and Clifford observable expectation values for circuits with third-level diagonal gates, with syndrome expectation extending to fourth-level gates. Scaling is polynomial in n and T, independent of logical qubit count. Applications include IQP sampling and magic state preparation/cultivation, and the methods were used to optimize high-rate magic state distillation factories based on three copies of a [[27,3,3]] tricycle code.

Conclusion: The work provides exact, efficient classical benchmarking tools for realistic non-Clifford fault-tolerant circuits without simulating logical measurements. These tools enable practical optimization of magic state distillation factories and support applications in IQP sampling and magic state cultivation.

Abstract: Non-Clifford gates are essential for avoiding classical simulability under the Gottesman-Knill theorem. An error-corrected circuit with non-Clifford gates will therefore be an important element for realizing useful fault-tolerant quantum algorithms. However, accurately understanding the fault-tolerant performance of such circuits faces challenges due to the classical hardness of simulating these circuits. We avoid simulating logical measurements and develop exact and efficient classical methods for benchmarking circuits composed of X, CNOT, and diagonal gates in the Clifford hierarchy, including state and gate teleportation gadgets with feedforward. For circuits with third-level diagonal gates, our benchmarking algorithm efficiently samples syndrome statistics and estimates the output logical fidelity and expectation values of certain Clifford observables, including all Pauli observables. The evaluation of syndrome expectation values also extends to circuits with fourth-level diagonal gates. Our algorithm scales polynomially in the number of physical qubits n and circuit depth T, independent of the number of logical qubits. The benchmarking applications include IQP sampling and magic state preparation/cultivation. We demonstrate the practical utility of our simulation algorithms by using them to optimize high-rate magic state distillation factories based on three copies of a [[27,3,3]] tricycle code.

</details>


### [91] [The QICK Box: A Modular RF Front-End System for Quantum Control and Readout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03609)
*L. Arnaldi,D. Martin,S. Sussman,S. Uemura,K. Treptow,N. Wilcer,A. Colón Cesaní,O. Seidel,K. Anyang,G. Bratrud,F. Abouzahr,J. Christiansen-Salameh,D. Baxter,G. Cancelo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The QICK Box is an open-source quantum control system with up to 16 outputs, 8 inputs, and 8 DC biases, achieving (91.7 ± 0.4)% single-shot readout fidelity on a six-transmon device without a quantum amplifier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QICK was introduced in 2022 as a standalone open-source control system for quantum information science. Since then it has been migrated to new hardware platforms and expanded to support diverse qubit types (superconducting, atomic, ion-trapped, spin defects), quantum sensors, and quantum networks, with software and firmware customized to minimize latency and maximize control performance and throughput. This white paper is motivated by the need to detail the hardware and performance of the resulting QICK Box.

Method: The QICK Box provides up to 16 outputs, up to 8 inputs, and 8 20-bit DC biases. Modular output and input daughtercards offer signal chains optimized either for fast DC-coupled signals or for RF signals up to 10 GHz. Power levels, step attenuation, and bandpass filtering are software-controlled. Performance is evaluated via single-shot readout measurements of a six-transmon device at the Quantum Underground Instrumentation Experimental Testbed (QUIET) at Fermilab.

Result: Single-shot readout of a six-transmon device achieved (91.7 ± 0.4)% readout fidelity, limited by T1 decay during readout. The single-shot readout signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) was 4.4 ± 0.1 without a quantum amplifier.

Conclusion: The QICK Box is suitable for high-fidelity quantum control and measurement applications. Advances in firmware and software will be described in a separate paper.

Abstract: The QICK (Quantum Instrumentation Control Kit), first introduced in 2022, is a standalone open source control system for quantum information science. Since then, QICK has been migrated to new hardware platforms, expanded its functionality for applications with different types of qubits (superconducting, atomic, ion-trapped, and spin defects), as well as quantum sensors, and quantum networks. QICK is a highly optimized control kit, with a library of functions that can be used for more than one application. In particular, the software and firmware are customized to minimize latency and maximize control performance and throughput. In this white paper, we describe hardware details and performance of the QICK Box. The QICK Box features up to 16 outputs, up to 8 inputs, and 8 20-bit DC biases. Modular output and input daughtercards give the choice of signal chains optimized for fast DC-coupled signals or RF signals up to 10 GHz. Power levels, step attenuation, and bandpass filtering are controlled by software. We demonstrate the system's performance through single-shot readout measurements of a six-transmon device at the Quantum Underground Instrumentation Experimental Testbed (QUIET) at Fermilab, achieving (91.7 +/- 0.4)% readout fidelity limited by T1 decay during the readout. The single shot readout signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) is 4.4 +/- 0.1 without a quantum amplifier, confirming that the QICK Box is suitable for high-fidelity quantum control and measurement applications. Advances in firmware and software will be described in a separate paper.

</details>


### [92] [Symmetry-preserving quantum compilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03624)
*Maryam Mudassar,Zhi-Yuan Wei,Alexander Schuckert,Michael J. Lawler,Michael J. Gullans,Daniel Gottesman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a fault-tolerant compilation framework that exactly preserves SU(2) symmetry, circumventing a number-theoretic obstruction via measurement and feed-forward, and demonstrates large resource savings and qualitatively corrected physics in Heisenberg-model transport simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Clifford+T synthesis can break continuous symmetries even when the target time evolution preserves them, so compiled fault-tolerant simulations may lose essential physical features of the original system. A compilation method is needed that preserves symmetries exactly.

Method: The authors develop a fault-tolerant compilation framework that preserves SU(2) symmetry exactly. They characterize the number-theoretic obstruction preventing symmetry-preserving unitary Clifford+T circuits from approximating arbitrary SU(2)-symmetric evolutions, and circumvent it using measurement and feed-forward. The resulting synthesis confines compilation errors to the symmetry-preserving operator algebra instead of introducing symmetry-breaking perturbations. The framework is extended to gauge symmetries in lattice gauge theories and to particle-number and spin symmetries in fermionic models.

Result: Simulating transport in the 1D Heisenberg model, at a matched T-count of 54 T gates per two-site Trotter step, standard synthesis yields an incorrect transport exponent while the symmetry-preserving compilation reproduces the expected exponent. When resources are allocated to reach equal accuracy in estimating the exponent, the construction reduces T-count by roughly a factor of five.

Conclusion: Symmetry-preserving fault-tolerant compilation can qualitatively change the effects of synthesis error while substantially reducing the resources required for quantum simulation.

Abstract: Fault-tolerant quantum simulation requires compiling symmetry-preserving time evolutions into discrete gate sets. Standard Clifford+T synthesis can break continuous symmetries even when the target evolution preserves them, causing compiled simulations to lose essential features of the original system. We develop a fault-tolerant compilation framework that preserves SU(2) symmetry exactly. We characterize the number-theoretic obstruction preventing symmetry-preserving unitary Clifford+T circuits from approximating arbitrary SU(2)-symmetric evolutions and circumvent it using measurement and feed-forward. The resulting synthesis confines compilation errors to the symmetry-preserving operator algebra rather than introducing symmetry-breaking perturbations.
  We demonstrate the algorithm by simulating transport in the one-dimensional Heisenberg model. At a lower matched T-count, for instance, 54 T gates per two-site Trotter step, standard synthesis yields an incorrect transport exponent, whereas our symmetry-preserving compilation reproduces the expected exponent for the same expected T-count. If instead each approach is allocated whatever resources are necessary to estimate this exponent to the same accuracy, our construction reduces the T-count by approximately a factor of five. We further extend the framework to gauge symmetries in lattice gauge theories and to particle-number and spin symmetries in fermionic models relevant to quantum chemistry and the Fermi-Hubbard model.
  These results show that symmetry-preserving fault-tolerant compilation can qualitatively change the effects of synthesis error while substantially reducing the resources required for quantum simulation.

</details>


### [93] [Discriminating Lindbladian Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03629)
*Robert Salzmann,Ludovico Lami,Martin B. Plenio,Susana F. Huelga*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes fundamental limits for discriminating two quantum Markovian dynamics (Lindbladians), characterizing the optimal type-II error (Stein) exponent as the largest instantaneous increase of quantum relative entropy, with finiteness given by an algebraic criterion. Temporal resolution emerges as a resource: some dynamics allow zero type-II error at finite total time, others require resolution time tending to zero, achieved via quantum Zeno protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum hypothesis testing has been extensively studied for quantum states and channels, but the fundamental limits of discriminating quantum Markovian dynamics generated by Lindbladians remain unexplored, particularly regarding the roles of total exposure time and temporal resolution under adaptive control.

Method: The authors allow adaptive choices of evolution durations and arbitrary instantaneous controls between them, and characterize the optimal type-II error exponent (Stein exponent) as the largest instantaneous increase of quantum relative entropy. They provide an algebraic criterion for its finiteness in terms of the Lindbladians and employ quantum Zeno protocols to achieve the discrimination.

Result: An infinite exponent is equivalent to a vanishing type-II error probability at sufficiently large finite total time for every positive type-I tolerance. Some dynamics admit a finite protocol with zero type-II error, while others require increasingly fine temporal control: the error vanishes as the minimum allowed evolution duration (resolution time) tends to zero at fixed sufficiently large total time, but stays bounded away from zero at every fixed positive resolution time. When the null hypothesis is the identity semigroup, an infinite asymptotic error exponent implies that at fixed finite total exposure time the type-II error can be made arbitrarily small while keeping type-I error exactly zero—impossible in the classical setting. Qubit discrimination of identity semigroup from generalized amplitude damping exemplifies all three regimes.
