Conclusion: Temporal resolution is established as a genuine resource for discriminating quantum Markovian dynamics, with quantum Zeno protocols enabling discrimination advantages that have no classical counterpart, particularly the possibility of arbitrarily small type-II error at exactly zero type-I error.

Abstract: The fundamental task of quantum hypothesis testing, which consists in discriminating quantum objects, has been extensively studied for quantum states and channels. Here, we investigate the fundamental limits of discriminating an arbitrary pair of quantum Markovian dynamics generated by Lindbladians, focusing on the roles of total exposure time and temporal resolution. We allow adaptive choices of evolution durations and arbitrary instantaneous controls between them. We characterise the optimal type-II error exponent (Stein exponent) as the largest instantaneous increase of quantum relative entropy, and give an algebraic criterion for its finiteness in terms of the Lindbladians. Remarkably, an infinite exponent is equivalent to a vanishing type-II error probability at sufficiently large finite total time, for every positive type-I tolerance. While some dynamics admit a finite protocol with zero type-II error, others require increasingly fine temporal control. For the latter, the error vanishes as the minimum allowed evolution duration, or resolution time, tends to zero at fixed sufficiently large total time, but remains bounded away from zero at every fixed positive resolution time. We achieve this using quantum Zeno protocols, establishing temporal resolution as a resource for discrimination. When the null hypothesis is the identity semigroup, an infinite asymptotic error exponent has a stronger consequence: at a fixed finite total exposure time, the type-II error can be made arbitrarily small while keeping the type-I error exactly zero. This is impossible in the corresponding classical setting. Discriminating the identity semigroup from generalised amplitude damping on a qubit provides examples of all three regimes: a finite asymptotic error exponent, zero type-II error attainable at positive resolution time, and vanishing type-II error requiring the resolution time to tend to zero.

</details>


### [94] [Entanglement of purification for Werner states: canonical purification and nonadditivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03630)
*Zahra Baghali Khanian,Amir-Reza Negari*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves that the von Neumann entanglement of purification (EoP) is nonadditive, resolving the longstanding additivity question at Rényi index α=1. For two-qubit Werner states in the range 1/64 ≤ f ≤ 1/4, the canonical purification is shown to be globally optimal, with its entanglement (reflected entropy) equal to the exact EoP, while the regularized EoP lies strictly below the reflected entropy for 0 < f < f* (f* ≈ 0.017). This establishes nonadditivity for 1/64 ≤ f < f*, witnessed by separable states requiring no underlying entanglement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The additivity of entanglement of purification is a fundamental open question in quantum information theory. Prior work [1] established Rényi nonadditivity for α<1 using classical two-qubit states, but the case α=1 (von Neumann EoP) remained unresolved. This paper aims to settle this question.

Method: The authors prove canonical optimality for two-qubit Werner states W(f) with singlet weight 1/64 ≤ f ≤ 1/4, showing the canonical purification achieves the exact entanglement of purification. The proof generalizes the technique in [1] that established Rényi nonadditivity for 0 ≤ α < 1 using classical two-qubit states. They then compare the regularized EoP with the reflected entropy.

Result: Canonical purification is globally optimal for the given range, so its entanglement (reflected entropy) equals the exact EoP. However, the regularized EoP is strictly below the reflected entropy for 0 < f < f* where f* ≈ 0.017. Together, this proves nonadditivity throughout 1/64 ≤ f < f*.

Conclusion: The von Neumann entanglement of purification is nonadditive, resolving the additivity question at α=1. Notably, nonadditivity is witnessed by separable states with no underlying entanglement, echoing the classical-state phenomenon observed for α<1.

Abstract: We prove that the von Neumann entanglement of purification is nonadditive. For two-qubit Werner states $W(f)$ with singlet weight $1/64\le f\le1/4$, we show that canonical purification is globally optimal: its entanglement, also known as reflected entropy, gives the exact entanglement of purification. In contrast, the regularized entanglement of purification lies strictly below the reflected entropy for $0<f<f_\ast$, where $f_\ast\approx0.017$. Together, these results establish nonadditivity throughout $1/64\le f<f_\ast$. Our canonical-optimality proof generalizes the proof in [1], which established Rényi nonadditivity for $0\leα<1$ using classical two-qubit states. We thus resolve the additivity question at $α=1$. Remarkably, nonadditivity is again witnessed by separable states and no entanglement in the underlying states is required.

</details>


### [95] [Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Nuclear Tensor Norms and Multipartite Separability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03637)
*Martino Bernasconi,Giulio Malavolta*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper gives a deterministic d^{O(k)}-time algorithm for constant-additive approximations of a multilinear tensor optimization problem with bounded Frobenius norm and convex local factor sets, yielding the first polynomial-time constant-accuracy algorithms for weak membership in the nuclear-norm unit ball of high-order tensors and for multipartite quantum separability in Frobenius norm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilinear tensor optimization under Frobenius-norm constraints underlies fundamental problems such as weak membership in the nuclear-norm ball of high-order tensors and multipartite quantum separability. Prior to this work, no polynomial-time algorithms achieving constant accuracy were known for these tasks, and extending such results to quantum input models given only state copies remained open.

Method: The problem is cast as a cooperative multiprover game, combined with a recursive spectral compression procedure, to design a deterministic algorithm running in time d^{O(k)} for constant additive approximations. The authors also show that related ideas admit a quantum implementation when the input state is provided through copies rather than an explicit classical description, yielding a Frobenius-norm separability testing algorithm.

Result: A deterministic d^{O(k)}-time algorithm for constant additive approximations is obtained. As applications, the first polynomial-time constant-accuracy algorithms are given for weak membership in the nuclear-norm unit ball of high-order tensors and for multipartite quantum separability in Frobenius norm. In the quantum setting, separability testing is solved using poly(k) copies and poly(k log d) time.

Conclusion: Viewing tensor optimization through a cooperative multiprover-game lens with recursive spectral compression enables efficient constant-accuracy algorithms for high-order tensor and quantum separability problems, and the approach transfers to quantum input models where only copies of the state are available.

Abstract: We study a multilinear optimization problem for tensors with bounded Frobenius norm, where each of the $k$ local factors of dimension $d$ is chosen from a convex set. We give a deterministic algorithm running in time $d^{O(k)}$ for constant additive approximations. As applications, we obtain the first polynomial-time algorithms at constant accuracy for weak membership in the nuclear-norm unit ball of high-order tensors and for multipartite quantum separability in Frobenius norm. Our approach views tensor optimization as a cooperative multiprover game and combines this perspective with a recursive spectral compression procedure. We then show that related ideas admit a quantum implementation when the input state is given through copies, rather than an explicit classical description. In these settings we obtain an algorithm to solve Frobenius-norm separability testing using $\text{poly}(k)$ copies and $\text{poly}(k\log d)$ time.

</details>


### [96] [Clarifications on the Experimental Status of Real Quantum Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03644)
*Timothée Hoffreumon,Mischa P. Woods*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This note rebuts attempts to recover the experimental claim of Renou et al. that a bilocal network experiment can falsify real quantum theory without falsifying quantum theory, arguing the proof falls short of a Bell-like standard and relies on an experimentally untestable premise about independent preparations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The author previously proved that real quantum theory cannot be experimentally falsified without also falsifying quantum theory, directly contradicting the central claim of Renou et al. This note aims to examine and address the seemingly natural attempts to rescue that experimental conclusion.

Method: Conceptual and logical analysis of the proposed bilocal network experiment, evaluating its assumptions and comparing its standard of proof with that of Bell-type nonlocality arguments.

Result: The results, though based on nonlocality experiments, are shown to be far from a Bell-like level of proof; the proposed experiment is argued to require an experimentally untestable premise that independent preparations must be represented as product states.

Conclusion: Attempts to recover the Renou et al. conclusion fail, because the experiment depends on an untestable assumption and does not achieve Bell-level rigor; additional out-of-scope concerns are also discussed.

Abstract: In our previous paper on the matter, we proved that real quantum theory (RQT) cannot be experimentally falsified without the experiment falsifying quantum theory (QT) as well. Our result is in direct contradiction to the central claim of an article by Renou et al., published in Nature [600, 625--629 (2021)], reporting a bilocal network experiment that could falsify real quantum theory without falsifying quantum theory.
  The purpose of this note is to address several seemingly natural attempts to recover the experimental conclusion of Renou et al. We first explain how these results, although based on nonlocality experiments, are far from a Bell-like level of proof. Then we explain why the proposed experiment requires an experimentally untestable premise about independent preparations necessarily being represented as product states. Finally, we discuss some concerns that lie outside the scope of both results.

</details>


### [97] [Exponential quantum space advantage in random data streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03645)
*Adam Bouland,Matthew Ding,Siddhartha Jain*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves two unconditional quantum space advantages in the random-order streaming model: exponential advantage for the Code Intersection problem, and polynomial-to-exponential advantage for Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) via a streaming Decoded Quantum Interferometry algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum advantages in space complexity are less understood than time advantages, and the streaming model provides a clean setting to establish unconditional separations. The authors aim to demonstrate that quantum computers can achieve exponential space savings even for simple random-order streaming tasks, providing provable advantages without unproven assumptions.

Method: For Code Intersection, the lower bound uses density-restoring partitions (Göös, Gur, Jain, Li, STOC 2025) combined with a convex potential inspired by Raz (J.ACM 2018) and Garg-Raz-Tal (STOC 2018). For OPI, they develop a streaming version of the Decoded Quantum Interferometry algorithm achieving the target satisfaction fraction with O(d log n) qubits.

Result: Code Intersection admits exponential quantum space advantage in random-order streaming. For OPI with degree d and n evaluation points, achieving 1/2 + Ω(√(d/n)) satisfied constraints requires Ω(n) classical bits but only O(d log n) qubits, yielding provable advantage in the low-rate regime (n ≫ d), up to exponential in certain parameter settings.

Conclusion: Random-order streaming enables unconditional exponential quantum space advantages, both for Code Intersection and, in low-rate regimes, for OPI. The framework combines density-restoring partitions with convex potentials and a streaming DQI algorithm.

Abstract: We show two unconditional quantum space advantages in the random-order streaming model. First, we show the Yamakawa--Zhandry Code Intersection problem admits exponential quantum advantage in the streaming model when its inputs are streamed in random order. This means quantum computers exhibit exponential space advantage even when simply receiving $(x,f(x))$ pairs for a uniformly random function $f$ in a uniformly random order. Our lower bound is shown using density-restoring partitions as in the work of Göös, Gur, Jain, and Li (STOC 2025) combined with a convex potential, similar to the work of Raz (J.ACM 2018) on parity learning and its generalization by Garg, Raz, and Tal (STOC 2018).
  Second, we use our framework to show quantum space advantage for the Optimal Polynomial Intersection (OPI) problem in certain regimes via a streaming version of the Decoded Quantum Interferometry algorithm (Nature 2025; arXiv:2510.10967). In particular, we show that for degree $d$ and $n$ evaluation points, attaining $1/2 + Ω(\sqrt{d/n})$ fraction of satisfied OPI constraints via streaming requires $Ω(n)$ classical bits of memory but only $O(d\log n)$ qubits. This yields provable quantum advantage in a "low-rate" regime when the number of evaluation points is much larger than the degree, with a space advantage that can be as large as exponential in certain parameter settings.

</details>


### [98] [Reaching the Limits of Ground-State Metrology with Many-Body Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03653)
*Erik L. André,Víctor Izquierdo,Ricard Puig,John Calsamiglia,Martí Perarnau-Llobet*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work establishes the physical conditions for many-body ground-state probes to saturate the static (Fθ ≲ N²/Δ²) and dynamical (Fθ ≲ N²τ²) limits of quantum Fisher information, showing that short-range systems require closing spectral gaps (e.g., ν(d+z)=1 at second-order critical points) while all-to-all interacting models achieve optimality even when gapped.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ground-state metrology aims to estimate an unknown parameter θ of an N-body Hamiltonian through measurements on its ground state, and while two fundamental precision limits (static ground-state bound and dynamical Heisenberg limit) are known, the physical requirements needed to actually reach them with realistic many-body probes remain unclear.

Method: The authors analytically derive the conditions for saturating the static bound Fθ ≲ N²/Δ² and the dynamical bound Fθ ≲ N²τ², examining probes with short-range versus long-range (all-to-all) interactions. They connect the dynamical bound to ground-state preparation time via local adiabatic protocols and test their predictions on paradigmatic models: the transverse-field Ising chain, the XXZ chain, a squeezing Hamiltonian, and the two-mode Bose-Hubbard model.

Result: Saturating the static limit demands a spectral gap closing with N for short-range systems, yielding a universal critical-exponent condition ν(d+z)=1 at second-order quantum critical points (approachable arbitrarily closely, as shown for the XXZ chain), with cat-like ground states at first-order transitions providing an alternative route. All-to-all interacting models instead attain optimality even in gapped regimes. The optimal inverse-gap profile for the dynamical bound can be reached through local adiabatic protocols.

Conclusion: The results provide a theoretical foundation for designing local many-body ground-state sensors that operate at the ultimate limits of quantum precision, identifying specific many-body probes capable of approaching both the static and dynamical bounds.

Abstract: We investigate the physical requirements to reach the ultimate limits of ground-state metrology -- i.e., the estimation of an unknown parameter $θ$ of an $N$-body Hamiltonian via measurements on its ground state -- using many-body probes featuring either short-range or long-range interactions. We characterize the conditions to saturate two fundamental limits of the quantum Fisher information $F_θ$: the static ground-state bound $F_θ\lesssim N^2/Δ^2$, where $Δ$ is the spectral gap, and the dynamical Heisenberg limit $F_θ\lesssim N^2 τ^2$, where $τ$ is the total protocol duration. To saturate the static limit, we argue that short-range interacting systems require a spectral gap that closes with $N$. At second-order quantum critical points this leads to a universal condition on the critical exponents, $ν(d+z)=1$, which can be approached arbitrarily closely, as we show for the XXZ chain; cat-like ground states at first-order transitions provide an alternative route to static optimality. In contrast, we find that all-to-all interacting models can achieve optimality even in gapped regimes. Furthermore, reaching the dynamical bound is tightly related to the ground-state preparation time; we demonstrate that its optimal, inverse-gap profile can be obtained via local adiabatic protocols. By analyzing paradigmatic systems -- the transverse-field Ising chain, the XXZ chain, a squeezing Hamiltonian, and the two-mode Bose-Hubbard model -- we identify specific many-body probes capable of approaching both limits. Overall, our results provide a theoretical basis for the design of (local) many-body ground-state sensors operating at the ultimate limits of quantum precision.

</details>


### [99] [A Basis-Aware Approach to Quantum Sampling of the Fermi-Hubbard Ladder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03654)
*Adam Leicester,Zixu Wang,Jack Mandell,Yangyang Xu,Osama Muhammad Raisuddin,Haimeng Zhang,Jian Shi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work studies how the choice of one-body orbital representation affects the compressibility and sampling efficiency of sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) for the half-filled Fermi-Hubbard ladder, and shows that no single basis is universally compact, motivating co-design of representation, circuit, and reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sample-based quantum diagonalization can cut classical cost by diagonalizing only configurations found via quantum sampling, but its efficiency hinges on how compactly the target state is represented in the sampled basis. The paper asks how this compressibility depends on the chosen orbital representation, interaction strength, and hopping anisotropy.

Method: The authors perform sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization on the half-filled Fermi-Hubbard ladder using physically motivated one-body orbital rotations. They compare position, momentum, bonding/antibonding, and leg-mode representations across varying interaction strengths and hopping anisotropy, using statevector SKQD to quantify sampling efficiency and hardware calculations on systems up to 120 qubits.

Result: The most compressible basis varies systematically with interaction strength and hopping anisotropy: momentum sampling is favored in the weakly interacting regime, while geometry- or interaction-adapted representations become advantageous as the dominant energy scale changes. Near intermediate interaction and isotropic hopping, even the best representation remains poorly compressible. Basis choice strongly affects sampling efficiency in statevector SKQD. Hardware runs up to 120 qubits retain qualitative signatures of this basis dependence but show increasing particle-number leakage and reconstruction sensitivity with system size.

Conclusion: Scalable sample-based diagonalization requires co-design of the sampled representation, the quantum circuit, and the classical reconstruction procedure, since compressibility is strongly basis-, interaction-, and geometry-dependent.

Abstract: Sample-based quantum diagonalization can reduce the classical cost of many-body calculations by restricting diagonalization to configurations identified through quantum sampling, but its efficiency depends on how compactly the target state is represented in the sampled basis. We investigate this dependence for sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization of the half-filled Fermi-Hubbard ladder using physically motivated one-body orbital rotations. Across position, momentum, bonding/antibonding, and leg-mode representations, the most compressible basis varies systematically with interaction strength and hopping anisotropy, with momentum sampling favored in the weakly interacting regime and geometry- or interaction-adapted representations becoming advantageous as the dominant energy scale changes. Near intermediate interaction and isotropic hopping, however, even the most favorable representation remains poorly compressible. Statevector SKQD calculations further show that basis choice strongly affects sampling efficiency. Hardware calculations on systems containing up to 120 qubits retain qualitative signatures of this basis dependence while revealing increasing particle-number leakage and reconstruction sensitivity with system size. These results show that scalable sample-based diagonalization requires co-design of the sampled representation, quantum circuit, and classical reconstruction procedure.

</details>


### [100] [Breaking the cubic barrier for the inverse-free Solovay-Kitaev algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03655)
*Adam Bouland,Victor Shyaka,Chenyi Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper improves inverse-free quantum compiling exponents, achieving ≈2.138 for Pauli gates with an irrational qubit rotation and ≤2.988 for the general qubit inverse-free case, surpassing the information-theoretic bound of 3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Solovay-Kitaev algorithm compiles unitaries in polylog(1/ε) time for inverse-closed gate sets, and Bouland and Giurgicǎ-Tiron extended this to arbitrary gate sets without inverses. However, their inverse-free exponent is high (over 8.62 for a qubit), while information theory (Oszmaniec, Sawicki, Horodecki) shows an inverse-free exponent of 3 is possible in any dimension. This raises the question of whether the Bouland–Giurgicǎ-Tiron exponent can be improved and whether any inverse-free exponent below 3 (surpassing information-theoretic arguments) is achievable.

Method: The authors first address a special case: Pauli gates supplemented by an irrational qubit rotation. They fuse ideas from prior algorithms of Kuperberg and Sardharwalla–Cubitt–Harrow–Linden to construct an efficient "Pauli golf" compilation routine. They then extend these ideas to the general inverse-free qubit case, using a novel form of approximate "aiming" of unitary corrections to improve compilation efficiency.

Result: For Pauli gates plus an irrational qubit rotation, the algorithm achieves an exponent at most ≈2.138. For the general inverse-free qubit case, the algorithm achieves an exponent at most 2.988. Both results surpass the information-theoretic bound of 3.

Conclusion: The paper provides two examples of quantum compiling algorithms surpassing information-theoretic arguments, demonstrating that substantial efficiency gains are possible for inverse-free compilation.

Abstract: The Solovay-Kitaev algorithm states that it is possible to efficiently compile unitaries to accuracy $ε$ in $\text{polylog}(1/ε)$ time using any universal gate set. While Solovay and Kitaev's initial work addressed inverse-closed gate sets, recently Bouland and Giurgicǎ-Tiron showed it is possible to efficiently compile arbitrary gate sets without inverses. However, the exponent of their compilation algorithm is high. For example the inverse-free exponent for a qubit is over $8.62$, while information theoretically it is known that an inverse-free exponent of $3$ is possible in any dimension by a result of Oszmaniec, Sawicki, and Horodecki. This leads to a natural question: can one improve the exponent in Bouland and Giurgicǎ-Tiron's algorithm? And are there any cases where the inverse-free exponent is below 3, thus surpassing existing information-theoretic arguments?
  To answer this question, we first show that a special case of the inverse-free compiling problem -- namely Pauli gates supplemented by an irrational rotation of a qubit - admits a highly efficient compilation algorithm with an exponent at most $\approx 2.138$.
  Our algorithm fuses ideas from prior algorithms of Kuperberg and Sardharwalla, Cubitt, Harrow and Linden to construct an efficient ``Pauli golf'' compilation routine. We then extend these ideas to the general inverse-free case for a qubit, where we give a compiling algorithm with exponent at most $2.988$. This result uses a novel form of approximate ``aiming'' of unitary corrections to improve the compilation efficiency. Our results give two examples of quantum compiling algorithms surpassing information-theoretic arguments, and demonstrate that there are many efficiency gains possible for inverse-free compilation.

</details>


### [101] [Super-Exponential Advantage of Squeezed Light in Phase Estimation under Discrete Phase Randomisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03657)
*Lorcan O. Conlon,V Vijendran,Aritra Das,Yu-Xin Wang,Erfan Abbasgholinejad,Anthony J. Brady,Jacob Bringewatt,Sean R. Muleady,Syed M. Assad,Alexey V. Gorshkov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a super-exponential separation in quantum Fisher information between squeezed vacuum states and coherent states for a specific phase estimation task, both in noiseless and noisy settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It is commonly believed that quantum resources offer at most a quadratic (Heisenberg) advantage in phase estimation sensitivity under reasonable prior-information assumptions. The authors challenge this by identifying a task where the advantage is far larger, motivating a re-examination of the limits of quantum metrology.

Method: The authors consider a channel implementing a phase shift of 2πk/M, where k is a uniformly random unknown integer, plus a small unknown phase of interest, under a mean photon number constraint per channel use. They compare the quantum Fisher information (QFI) of a squeezed vacuum state against that of coherent states and any state obtainable from a coherent state via phase-covariant channels, and complement the QFI analysis with non-asymptotic estimation protocols. They also analyze noise, optical loss, and thermal noise.

Result: At mean photon number 1 per channel use, the QFI ratio grows as Ω[(M/√2e)^M]. At mean photon number M/log(M), squeezed vacuum QFI is Ω(M^{3/2}/√log(M)) while coherent state QFI is at most exp[−M log log(M)+O(M)]. Non-asymptotically, with M/log M photons per channel use, an explicit squeezed-vacuum protocol achieves accuracy O(1/M) using O(√(M log M)) channel uses, whereas any coherent state protocol needs at least exp[M log log M−O(M)] channel uses. Under noise the ratio can still grow super-exponentially, and under fixed optical loss and thermal noise, choosing n̄=M²/(log M)^{3/2} lets squeezed vacuum with heterodyne detection reach O(1/M) accuracy with sub-polynomially many channel uses, while the classical family needs super-polynomially many.

Conclusion: The quadratic (Heisenberg) limit is not universal: for this phase estimation task, squeezed vacuum states provide a super-exponential advantage in QFI and in channel-use complexity over coherent states, and this advantage persists under noise, optical loss, and thermal noise.

Abstract: A paradigmatic task in quantum metrology is that of phase estimation. For this task it is commonly believed, under reasonable assumptions about prior information, that a quadratic advantage in sensitivity is the maximum possible advantage offered by the use of quantum resources. In this work, we consider a channel that implements a phase shift by an angle $2πk/M$, where $k$ is a uniformly random unknown integer, in addition to a small unknown phase shift of interest. We show that, subject to a constraint on the mean photon number per channel use, there is a super-exponential separation between the quantum Fisher information (QFI) attainable using a squeezed vacuum state and that attainable using coherent states. This advantage holds over any state obtained from a coherent state via phase covariant channels. Specifically, when the mean photon number per channel use is 1, the ratio of their QFI grows as $Ω[(M/\sqrt{2}e)^M]$. For a mean photon number per channel use of $M/\log(M)$, the squeezed vacuum QFI is $Ω(M^{3/2}/\sqrt{\log(M)})$, whereas for a coherent state it is at most $\exp[-M\log\log(M)+O(M)]$. We complement this QFI analysis with non-asymptotic estimation results. For a mean photon number per channel use of $M/\log M$, an explicit squeezed-vacuum protocol estimates the phase to accuracy $O(1/M)$ using $O(\sqrt{M\log M})$ channel uses, whereas every coherent state protocol requires at least $\exp[M\log\log M-O(M)]$ channel uses. We consider the effects of noise and show that the ratio of their QFIs can still grow super-exponentially in $M$. Finally, under fixed optical loss and thermal noise, choosing $\bar n=M^2/(\log M)^{3/2}$ allows squeezed vacuum with heterodyne detection to achieve accuracy $O(1/M)$ using sub-polynomially many channel uses, whereas every protocol in the specified classical family requires super-polynomially many.

</details>


### [102] [A note on tomography of states with low stabilizer rank](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03658)
*Arkopal Dutt*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper provides a tomography protocol for n-qubit quantum states with stabilizer rank k, running in poly(n, 2^k, 1/ε) time, enabling efficient handling when k = O(log n), by establishing an improved bound on stabilizer extent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum state tomography generally scales exponentially with the number of qubits, but states with low stabilizer rank k admit compact stabilizer decompositions that may permit more efficient characterization. The previous best bound on stabilizer extent was k^{O(k)}, which limited the efficiency of tomography protocols based on that quantity.

Method: The authors first prove that the stabilizer extent ξ(|ψ⟩), defined as the minimal ℓ₁ norm of the coefficients in any stabilizer decomposition of |ψ⟩, is bounded by 2^{O(k)}, improving the earlier k^{O(k)} bound. They then leverage this tighter bound and combine it with an existing tomography protocol designed for states with bounded stabilizer extent to construct a tomography schedule for stabilizer-rank-k states.

Result: The protocol performs tomography of n-qubit states promised to have stabilizer rank k up to trace distance ε > 0 in poly(n, 2^k, 1/ε) time. Consequently, states with k = O(log n) can be tomographed efficiently.

Conclusion: The improved stabilizer extent bound of 2^{O(k)} directly yields an efficient tomography protocol for low stabilizer rank states, extending tractable state characterization to the regime k = O(log n).

Abstract: In this note, we consider tomography of $n$-qubit quantum states $|ψ\rangle$ that are promised to have stabilizer rank $k$ i.e., admit a stabilizer decomposition of $|ψ\rangle = \sum_{i=1}^k c_i |φ_i\rangle$ where each $|φ_i\rangle$ is a stabilizer state. We give a tomography protocol for such states up to trace distance $\varepsilon > 0$ running in $\textsf{poly}(n,2^k,1/\varepsilon)$ time and thus can handle $k=O(\log n)$ efficiently. This follows from first showing that the stabilizer extent $ξ(|ψ\rangle)$, which is the minimal $\ell_1$ norm over the coefficients $\{c_i\}$ of any stabilizer decomposition of $|ψ\rangle$, can be bounded as $ξ(|ψ\rangle) \leq 2^{O(k)}$ and improving upon the previous best bound of $k^{O(k)}$. The protocol is then obtained by utilizing a tomography protocol for states with bounded stabilizer extent.

</details>


### [103] [Quantum Codeword Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03659)
*John Blue,Zachary E. Chin,Vlad S. Oros,Isaac L. Chuang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quantum sensor register coupled to persistent quantum memory can decode a weak-signal classical codeword more reliably than any classical-memory receiver with the same qubit budget, achieving exact correction of transmission errors and vanishing decoding error even under stochastic bit flips.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper studies the fundamental advantage of quantum memory over classical memory for identifying an unknown classical codeword delivered through a sequence of weak phase interactions. Each received bit controls an identical Z-rotation of angle θ on every sensor qubit, and the total collected signal is weak (Qθ → 0). The central question is whether a persistent quantum memory between received bits enables a genuinely better receiver than any receiver restricted to classical memory under the same qubit budget Q and angle θ, including in the presence of transmission errors.

Method: The receiver model allows auxiliary qubits within a total qubit budget Q, arbitrary control unitaries, and entanglement among all qubits during each reception step. The paper compares sensors coupled to a persistent quantum memory against receivers with only classical memory between received bits. For a family of [n,k] binary simplex codes with even k and n = 2^k − 1, the sensor register is coupled to a persistent quantum memory of the same size, with Q = Θ((t+1) log n) and θ = 2π/(k√(n+1)). The analysis also considers independent bit flips with fixed probability p < 1/16 under the same θ and a qubit budget Q = O(log n log log n).

Result: The paper establishes that a sensor coupled to persistent quantum memory identifies the original codeword more reliably than any receiver with the same Q and θ but only classical memory, even when bit flips occur. For the simplex-code family, exact correction of up to t ≤ (k−2)/4 transmission errors is achieved using Q = Θ((t+1) log n) and θ = 2π/(k√(n+1)). For t = o(√k), every comparable receiver with only classical memory has average success probability (over uniformly chosen codewords) tending to zero. Under independent bit flips of fixed probability p < 1/16, the persistent-quantum-memory receiver achieves decoding error tending to zero as n → ∞ with Q = O(log n log log n), whereas every otherwise equivalent classical-memory receiver has vanishing average success probability.

Conclusion: Persistent quantum memory provides an asymptotic, unbounded advantage over classical memory for codeword identification through weak phase interactions, both for exact error correction of a bounded number of transmission errors and for stochastic bit-flip models. The advantage holds under the same qubit budget and interaction angle, separating the two memory paradigms in the weak-signal regime.

Abstract: Consider the task of identifying an unknown classical codeword delivered to a register of quantum sensor qubits through a sequence of weak phase interactions that each encode one bit. Suppose that each received bit controls an identical $Z$-rotation of angle $θ$ applied to every sensor qubit; receivers may also use auxiliary qubits within a total qubit budget $Q$, with arbitrary control unitaries and entanglement among all qubits allowed during each reception step. When the total collected signal is weak ($Qθ\rightarrow 0$), we establish that a sensor coupled to a persistent quantum memory can identify the original codeword more reliably than any receiver with the same $Q$ and $θ$ but only classical memory between received bits, even when bit flips occur during transmission. In particular, for a family of $[n,k]$ binary simplex codes with even $k$ and $n=2^k-1$, we achieve exact correction of up to $t\leq(k-2)/4$ transmission errors by coupling the sensor register to a persistent quantum memory of the same size, using $Q = Θ((t+1)\log n)$ and $θ=\frac{2π}{k\sqrt{n+1}}$; for $t=o(\sqrt k)$, every comparable receiver with only classical memory instead has average success probability (over uniformly chosen codewords) tending to zero. Furthermore, under independent bit flips of any fixed probability $p<1/16$, our receiver with persistent quantum memory achieves decoding error tending to zero as $n\to\infty$ using $Q=O(\log n\log\log n)$ total qubits and the same $θ$. In contrast, every otherwise equivalent receiver with only classical memory has vanishing average success probability under the same stochastic error model.

</details>


### [104] [Lie Algebraic Uncertainty Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03661)
*Xiaoli Hu,Naihuan Jing,Jian Zhang,Yunlong Xiao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a covariance-based formulation of quantum uncertainty relations, deriving an infinite family of state-independent optimal bounds for Hermitian and non-Hermitian operators expressed in closed form via Lie algebra and representation theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal state-independent uncertainty bounds are currently known analytically only in a few isolated cases. There is a need for a general, systematic framework that replaces state-dependent optimization with a direct algebraic calculation.

Method: The authors introduce a covariance-based formulation of quantum uncertainty. For operators forming a basis of a Lie algebra, the optimal bound is derived in closed form from the algebra and its representation. Within each weight space, the uncertainty is shown to take a fixed value.

Result: The framework yields an infinite family of uncertainty relations valid for both Hermitian observables and non-Hermitian operators. The optimal bound is obtained via direct algebraic calculation rather than optimization over quantum states, and the inequality reduces to a conservation law within each weight space.

Conclusion: Commutation relations together with their realization on a quantum state jointly determine the minimum quantum uncertainty of incompatible operators. By linking uncertainty to symmetry and representation theory, the framework provides a route to identifying intrinsic limits on quantum information processing via the underlying Lie algebra.

Abstract: Uncertainty relations express the fundamental incompatibility of quantum observables, yet optimal state-independent bounds are known analytically in only a few cases. Here we introduce a covariance-based formulation of quantum uncertainty that yields an infinite family of relations for both Hermitian observables and non-Hermitian operators. For operators forming a basis of a Lie algebra, we derive the optimal bound in closed form from the algebra and its representation, replacing optimization over quantum states with a direct algebraic calculation. Within each weight space, uncertainty takes a fixed value, turning the inequality into a conservation law. These results show how commutation relations and their realization on a quantum state jointly determine the minimum quantum uncertainty associated with incompatible operators. By connecting uncertainty to symmetry and representation theory, the framework offers a route to uncovering intrinsic limits on quantum information processing through its underlying Lie algebra.

</details>


### [105] [BARC codes: general polynomial framework for coherent-state superposition codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03663)
*Praneel Gore,Davit Aghamalyan,Varun Narasimhachar,Andrew Tanggara*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces BARC codes: an algebraic framework for bosonic quantum error-correcting codes whose states are superpositions of coherent-state constellations constrained by symmetries of solution sets of complex polynomial systems, with bounds on minimum geometric separation and explicit single-mode examples benchmarked against spherical and cubature codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bosonic quantum error correction needs code constructions resilient to photon-gain and photon-loss errors. Existing coherent-state constellation schemes lack a systematic algebraic structure that ties symmetry constraints to approximate Knill-Laflamme conditions, and there is limited understanding of geometric separation bounds determining noise resilience.

Method: The authors build an algebraic framework where code states are finite superpositions of coherent-state constellations constrained by symmetries of solution sets to multivariate complex polynomial systems. Constraints are tied to photon-gain and photon-loss errors, imposing approximate Knill-Laflamme conditions. For equally-weighted constellation points, upper bounds on minimum geometric separation are derived. In the single-mode case, degree-two polynomial solution sets are characterized via orthogonal group symmetry, yielding explicit ellipsoidal and hexagonal BARC codes.

Result: Upper bounds on minimum geometric separation are obtained for equally-weighted constellations, quantifying noise resilience. Explicit ellipsoidal and hexagonal BARC codes are constructed in the single-mode case via orthogonal group symmetry characterization of degree-two solution sets. Representative instances are benchmarked against spherical and cubature codes using entanglement fidelity with optimal recovery under pure-loss noise.

Conclusion: The BARC framework provides a symmetry-driven algebraic construction of bosonic codes with approximate Knill-Laflamme conditions for gain/loss errors, offering explicit single-mode families and a benchmark against existing constellation-based codes.

Abstract: We introduce an algebraic framework for constructing a new class of quantum error-correcting codes, bosonic algebraically-restricted constellation (BARC) codes. The code states are finite superpositions of coherent-state constellations constrained by symmetries of solution sets to multivariate complex polynomial systems. The constraints are associated with photon-gain and photon-loss errors, thereby imposing approximate Knill--Laflamme conditions for such errors. For equally-weighted constellation points, we derive upper bounds on the minimum geometric separation between points, indicating how noise-resilient the code is. In the single-mode case, we characterize degree-two polynomial solution sets using an orthogonal group symmetry to obtain explicit ellipsoidal and hexagonal BARC codes. We benchmark representative instances of these families against spherical and cubature codes using the entanglement fidelity with optimal recovery under pure-loss noise.

</details>


### [106] [Optimal kernel functions for linear combination of Hamiltonian simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03669)
*Harriet Apel,Bjorn K. Berntson*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes an asymptotically optimal LCHS framework: for a broadened class of admissible kernel functions, it identifies the query-cost-minimizing kernel and proves the minimum cost has asymptotic expansion (2e/π)(log(1/ε) − log log(1/ε) + o(1)), improving the empirically obtained coefficient by ~2.1×.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear combination of Hamiltonian simulation (LCHS) is an asymptotically optimal framework for quantum simulation of linear non-unitary dynamics, but its efficiency depends on the choice of kernel function. Prior work by Low and Somma found an optimally scaling coefficient only empirically via numerical minimization over a restricted family. A rigorous characterization of the optimal kernel and its true cost over a broader admissible class was needed.

Method: The authors define a broadened class of admissible kernel functions via natural complex-analytic conditions and establish an LCHS approximation theorem for this class. Under the standard time-independent block-encoding access model, they explicitly construct, for each target error ε, the unique kernel minimizing the query-cost functional. They derive the asymptotic expansion of the minimum, and construct a spectrally convergent quadrature with explicit bounds on operator error and LCU subnormalization. The analysis relies on Hardy space theory and convex analysis.

Result: The minimum query-cost functional has the asymptotic expansion (2e/π)(log(1/ε) − log log(1/ε) + o(1)) as ε↓0. The provably optimal leading coefficient 2e/π improves by approximately 2.1× over the coefficient obtained empirically by Low and Somma. The asymptotic improvement is shown to persist after discretization into a finite LCU suitable for implementation.

Conclusion: LCHS admits a provably optimal kernel within a broad complex-analytic admissible class, with a query cost achieving an asymptotic coefficient of 2e/π—substantially better than previously known empirical results—and this optimality is preserved under practical LCU discretization.

Abstract: Linear combination of Hamiltonian simulation (LCHS) provides an asymptotically optimal framework for the quantum simulation of linear non-unitary dynamics. The LCHS approach approximately implements the Peano--Baker propagator through an integral representation determined by a kernel function. We establish an LCHS approximation theorem for a broadened class of admissible kernel functions, defined by natural complex-analytic conditions. In the standard time-independent block-encoding access model, we explicitly construct, for each target error $\varepsilon$, the unique kernel function in this class minimizing the query-cost functional and show that the minimum of this functional has the asymptotic expansion $\tfrac{2\mathrm{e}}π\big(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}-\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+o(1)\big)$ as ${\varepsilon\downarrow0}$. The provably optimal leading coefficient $2\mathrm{e}/π$ improves by a factor of approximately 2.1 on the coefficient obtained empirically by Low and Somma through numerical minimization over their family of optimally scaling kernel functions. We construct a spectrally convergent quadrature with explicit bounds on operator error and linear combination of unitaries (LCU) subnormalization, proving that this asymptotic improvement persists after discretization into a finite linear combination of unitaries suitable for implementation. Our results are based on Hardy space theory and convex analysis.

</details>


### [107] [Classical Algorithms for Bipartite Quantum Max-Cut on Dense Expanders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03670)
*Stuart Wayland,Zackary Jorquera,Alexandra Kolla*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A polynomial-time classical randomized algorithm is given for Quantum Max-Cut (spin-1/2 antiferromagnetic Heisenberg model) on dense balanced bipartite expanders, estimating ground energy and edge correlations to additive error ε.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum Max-Cut is a well-studied local Hamiltonian problem whose classical complexity in the bipartite case remains unresolved. This work aims to resolve this gap for a natural class of dense bipartite expander graphs.

Method: A Markov chain is constructed over perfect matchings of the complete bipartite graph, where each matching corresponds to a product of singlets. Expansion of the underlying graph is used to prove that polynomially many steps and samples of this chain converge to the ground state, yielding a polynomial-time randomized algorithm.

Result: The algorithm estimates the ground energy and any ground state edge correlation to additive error ε in time polynomial in n and 1/ε. The class covered includes G(n,n,p) with high probability for every fixed p>0.

Conclusion: The bipartite Quantum Max-Cut problem becomes classically tractable on dense balanced bipartite expanders, suggesting that the hardness of the general bipartite case, if present, must arise from sparser or non-expanding structures.

Abstract: Quantum Max-Cut is a well-studied local Hamiltonian problem for which the classical complexity of the bipartite case remains open. We give a polynomial-time classical randomized algorithm for the spin-$\tfrac12$ antiferromagnetic Heisenberg model, equivalently Quantum Max-Cut, on dense balanced bipartite expanders. This class includes $G(n,n,p)$ with high probability for every fixed $p>0$. The algorithm estimates the ground energy and any ground state edge correlation to additive error $\varepsilon$ in time polynomial in $n$ and $1/\varepsilon$. We construct a Markov chain on perfect matchings of the complete bipartite graph, each matching representing a product of singlets, that converges to the ground state, and use the expansion of the graph to show that polynomially many steps and samples of this chain suffice.

</details>


### [108] [Quantum simulation of field-tunable spin spectroscopy of the quantum magnet Cs2CoCl4 on a trapped-ion quantum computer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03671)
*Elias Kokkas,Nora Bauer,Justin Provazza,Mark E. Nowakowski,Kathleen Hamilton,Gilles Buchs,Andrew Sornborger,Travis S. Humble,Ananth Kaushik,Martin Roetteler*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Using IonQ's 36-qubit processor and 420 two-qubit-gate circuits, the authors recover the excitation spectra and spin dynamics of a 36-site Heisenberg XXZ chain (modeling Cs2CoCl4), matching DMRG benchmarks with correlation/SSIM >0.9 and NMSE <0.15, and validate against inelastic neutron scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collective excitation spectra of quantum magnets offer a direct test of quantum computers for strongly correlated matter, but hardware noise restricts circuit depth, raising the question of how much magnetic information survives circuit compression.

Method: A 36-site Heisenberg XXZ chain in a transverse field is simulated on IonQ's 36-qubit Forte Enterprise processor using circuits of only 420 two-qubit gates. The workflow combines an ancilla-free local kick protocol with classical tensor network compression to encode both ground-state preparation and time evolution, alongside error mitigation.

Result: Reconstructed dynamical structure factors capture the evolution from a broad two-spinon continuum to a dispersive magnon-like branch near the expected critical field. A field-driven sign reversal of nearest-neighbor spin correlations is observed. Recovered spectra agree closely with DMRG (Pearson correlation and SSIM above 0.9, NMSE below 0.15) and are compared with inelastic neutron scattering measurements.

Conclusion: Circuit compression and error mitigation enable noisy quantum processors to recover both collective excitations and local magnetic correlations, offering a quantitative benchmark for quantum spectroscopy and a basis for extending these methods to more complex magnetic systems.

Abstract: The collective excitation spectra of quantum magnets provide a direct test of quantum computers as tools for studying strongly correlated matter. Hardware noise limits the circuit depths available for these calculations, leaving the question of how much magnetic information can survive circuit compression. Using IonQ's 36 qubit Forte Enterprise processor, we simulate the magnetic excitation spectra and spin dynamics of a 36 site Heisenberg XXZ chain describing the quantum magnet Cs2CoCl4 in a transverse field using quantum circuits containing just 420 two qubit gates. The workflow combines an ancilla free local kick protocol with classical tensor network compression, encoding both ground state preparation and time evolution. The reconstructed dynamical structure factors capture the evolution from a broad two spinon continuum toward a dispersive branch with magnon character near the expected critical field. We observe an interesting field driven sign reversal of the nearest neighbor spin correlations which shows a complementary signature of this magnetic evolution. Our error mitigation methods bring the reconstructed total spectra into close agreement with density matrix renormalization group calculations, with Pearson correlation and structural similarity above 0.9 and normalized mean squared error below 0.15. We also perform comparisons with experimental inelastic neutron scattering measurements to connect the recovered excitations to the material's magnetic response. Our results demonstrate that circuit compression and error mitigation allow noisy quantum processors to recover both collective excitations and local magnetic correlations providing a quantitative benchmark for quantum spectroscopy and a basis for extending these methods to more complex magnetic systems.

</details>


### [109] [Ordering-Aware Theory of Trotter Error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03672)
*Zhenning Liu,Zhi-Yuan Wei,Michael Foss-Feig,Alexey V. Gorshkov,Andrew M. Childs*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies how the ordering of Hamiltonian terms affects the accuracy of product-formula (Trotter) simulations of quantum many-body dynamics, deriving an efficient ordering-sensitive error bound and an optimization method that yields more accurate simulations than standard brickwall circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Product formulas are a basic tool for simulating quantum many-body dynamics, but a fundamental degree of freedom in their application—the ordering of Hamiltonian terms—has remained largely unexplored. The authors aim to quantify ordering effects on simulation accuracy and exploit them to design more accurate product-formula simulations.

Method: The authors develop an efficiently computable, ordering-sensitive bound on the finite-step-size Trotter error for geometrically local Hamiltonians, valid for arbitrarily high-order product formulas. They then use the local structure of the error bounds and estimates to optimize ordering efficiently, reducing the long-chain problem to a minimum-mean-weight-cycle problem on a weighted de Bruijn graph.

Result: For translation-invariant one-dimensional chains, the bounds are close to exact second-, fourth-, and sixth-order Trotter errors in small-system tests, and on large-scale systems they improve on previous higher-order analytical bounds by orders of magnitude. For random translation-invariant chains, sequential (staircase-like) orderings consistently outperform standard brickwall circuits; under restricted circuit depth, the optimizer favors wave-like parallel-sequential orderings, which preserve much of the accuracy advantage over brickwall circuits.

Conclusion: These results establish ordering as a useful degree of freedom for both understanding and improving product-formula simulation.

Abstract: Product formulas are a basic tool for simulating quantum many-body dynamics, but a fundamental degree of freedom in their application---the ordering of Hamiltonian terms---has remained largely unexplored. Here we systematically study such ordering effects, quantifying their impact on simulation accuracy and exploiting them to design more accurate product-formula simulations. We develop an efficiently computable, ordering-sensitive bound on the finite-step-size Trotter error for geometrically local Hamiltonians for arbitrarily high-order product formulas. For translation-invariant one-dimensional chains, our bounds are close to exact second-, fourth-, and sixth-order Trotter errors in small-system tests, while on large-scale systems they improve on previous higher-order analytical bounds by orders of magnitude. We further use the local structure of the error bounds and estimates to optimize ordering efficiently, reducing the long-chain problem to a minimum-mean-weight-cycle problem on a weighted de Bruijn graph. For random translation-invariant chains, sequential (staircase-like) orderings consistently outperform standard brickwall circuits; when circuit depth is restricted, the optimizer instead favors wave-like parallel-sequential orderings, which preserve much of the accuracy advantage over brickwall circuits. These results establish ordering as a useful degree of freedom for both understanding and improving product-formula simulation.

</details>


### [110] [Quantum Simulation on Riemannian Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03673)
*Dylan Herman,Jacob Watkins,Guneykan Ozgul,Jiayu Shen,Brandon Augustino,Junhyung Lyle Kim,Shouvanik Chakrabarti*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops quantum algorithms for simulating the Schrödinger equation on Riemannian manifolds, using Laplace–Beltrami kinetic operators via (1) a global spectral method with efficient eigenbasis transforms for specific manifolds and (2) a local spectral method on multiple charts applicable to any compact manifold, with applications to optimization and field-theory simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum simulation has largely targeted flat-space or fixed-geometry dynamics; extending it to the Schrödinger equation on Riemannian manifolds governed by the Laplace–Beltrami operator is needed for geometry-aware physical simulation and for Riemannian optimization tasks such as Quantum Hamiltonian Descent.

Method: Two algorithm families: (1) a global spectral method identifying efficient transforms to the Laplace–Beltrami eigenbasis, yielding explicit efficient simulations on tori and spheres (standard metrics), simplices (Wright–Fisher metric), truncated positive orthants and invertible affine images (log-barrier Hessian metric), and ℓ_p balls (Duffy-map-induced metric); (2) a coherent simulation of local spectral methods across multiple charts, analyzed in the continuum with conditions for a polynomial spectral cutoff, plus a discretization analysis for tensor products of constant-dimensional manifolds.

Result: Explicit, efficient quantum simulation algorithms are obtained for the listed manifolds under the global spectral approach; the chart-based local spectral method is shown (in the continuum) to admit a polynomial spectral cutoff under stated conditions, with a discretization analysis for tensor-product manifolds. Optimization results include a generalization and convergence analysis of Quantum Hamiltonian Descent for geodesically convex functions, yielding explicit algorithms on the sphere and simplex, and a Riemannian generalization of the Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm. Field-theory applications include simulation of certain spatially discretized field theories, e.g. a variant of the nonlinear sigma model.

Conclusion: Quantum simulation of the Riemannian Schrödinger equation is feasible via both global and local spectral methods, with rigorous cutoff and discretization guarantees and concrete instantiations on several manifolds, enabling geometry-aware applications in optimization and physical simulation.

Abstract: We investigate algorithms for the quantum simulation of the Schrödinger equation on a Riemannian manifold, where the kinetic operator is defined by the Laplace--Beltrami operator corresponding to the metric. Our first algorithms are based on a global spectral method based on the identification of an efficient transform to the eigenbasis of the Laplace--Beltrami operator. We use this method to provide explicit, efficient, quantum simulation algorithms for the Riemannian Schrödinger equation on tori and spheres with their standard metrics, simplices with the Wright--Fisher metric, truncated positive orthants and their invertible affine images with the log-barrier Hessian metric, and $\ell_p$ balls with a metric induced by the Duffy map. Our second algorithm is based on a coherent simulation of local spectral methods on multiple charts, and is in principle applicable to any compact manifold. We first analyze this algorithm in the continuum and derive conditions under which a polynomial spectral cutoff suffices. We also provide a discretization analysis of a polynomial spectral cutoff for tensor-products of constant-dimensional manifolds. Finally, we consider applications of these methods to optimization and physical simulation. For optimization, we provide results including a generalization and convergence analysis of Quantum Hamiltonian Descent for geodesically convex functions that leads to explicit algorithms on the sphere and simplex, and a Riemannian generalization of the Real-Space Adiabatic Algorithm. For physical simulation, we show that our algorithms can simulate certain spatially discretized field theories, including a variant of the nonlinear sigma model.

</details>


### [111] [Efficient capacity-achieving entanglement generation with application to pure-loss Bosonic channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03674)
*Avijit Mandal,Christophe Piveteau,Henry D. Pfister,Joseph M. Renes*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work constructs an efficient entanglement generation scheme achieving the coherent information for a broad class of channels, and the quantum capacity for degradable channels including truncated pure-loss Bosonic channels—the first explicit, efficiently decodable protocol reaching the energy-constrained quantum capacity of the pure-loss Bosonic channel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite well-established ultimate limits on quantum communication rates over noisy channels, very few efficient coding schemes achieve these limits—unlike classical communication, where polar codes and LDPC codes efficiently achieve classical capacity. This gap motivates the search for efficient quantum coding schemes.

Method: The authors build an efficient entanglement generation scheme using polar codes for two classes of classical-input, quantum-output (CQ) channels: (1) those with commuting outputs, decoded via classical polar decoding algorithms; and (2) those whose outputs are heralded mixtures of pure states, decoded via the belief propagation with quantum messages (BPQM) algorithm.

Result: The scheme achieves the coherent information for a broad class of channels. For degradable channels in this class—including truncated versions of the pure-loss Bosonic channel—it achieves the quantum capacity. It is the first explicit, efficiently decodable protocol achieving the energy-constrained quantum capacity of the pure-loss Bosonic channel.

Conclusion: Efficient quantum coding approaching fundamental limits is feasible: polar codes combined with appropriate decoders (classical polar decoding and BPQM) yield an explicit, efficiently decodable entanglement generation scheme reaching coherent information broadly and quantum capacity for degradable channels such as truncated pure-loss Bosonic channels.

Abstract: While the ultimate limits on the rate of quantum communication over noisy channels are well-established, very few efficient coding schemes achieving these limits are known. This contrasts sharply with transmitting classical data over classical channels, where polar codes and low-density parity-check codes both efficiently achieve the classical capacity of any noisy channel. In this work we construct an efficient entanglement generation scheme which achieves the coherent information for a broad class of channels. For degradable channels in the class, which includes truncated versions of the pure loss Bosonic channel, the resulting scheme achieves the quantum capacity. The construction makes use of polar codes for two kinds of classical-input, quantum-output (CQ) channels: those with commuting outputs and those whose outputs are heralded mixtures of pure states. The former case is amenable to decoding by classical polar decoding algorithms, the latter by the belief propagation with quantum messages (BPQM) algorithm. To our knowledge, this construction is the first explicit and efficiently decodable protocol that achieves the energy-constrained quantum capacity of the pure-loss Bosonic channel.

</details>


### [112] [How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love the Redfield Equation: Completely-Positive Resummation of Non-Markovian Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03676)
*Greg Kaplanek,Jason Pollack*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper compares the Redfield (TCL2) equation with an exponentiated Magnus resummation of the same second-order perturbative map, showing they agree to second order but differ via time-ordering at higher orders. A weak-coupling criterion is derived guaranteeing Redfield evolution stays close to a CPTP map, even when strongly non-Lindbladian, illustrated with qubit examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Redfield equation with time-dependent coefficients is one of the simplest perturbative descriptions of open quantum system dynamics, but its approximate status is subtle: perturbation theory fixes the reduced dynamics order-by-order in the system-environment coupling, while solving the Redfield equation yields a resummation that need not preserve complete positivity. The authors aim to clarify when and why Redfield evolution remains reliable despite being non-Markovian and non-Lindbladian.

Method: The authors compare Redfield evolution with an exponentiated (Magnus) resummation of the same second-order perturbative map, which is completely positive and trace preserving (CPTP) under broad physical conditions. They also construct a simple CPTP resolvent resummation to demonstrate the non-uniqueness of resummations of the same perturbative map. The analysis focuses on the differences arising from time-ordering corrections at higher orders, and a weak-coupling criterion is derived. Two finite-dimensional (qubit) examples illustrate the criterion.

Result: Redfield and Magnus prescriptions agree to second order but differ through time-ordering corrections at higher orders. The constructed CPTP resolvent resummation illustrates the non-uniqueness of resummations. A weak-coupling criterion is exhibited that guarantees Redfield evolution, even when non-Markovian, remains close to a CPTP map. The criterion is demonstrated in two qubit examples.

Conclusion: The results clarify when, and why, Redfield evolution can remain reliable even when it is highly non-Lindbladian, providing a weak-coupling criterion for its proximity to a CPTP map.

Abstract: The Redfield equation with time-dependent coefficients (or TCL2 equation) gives one of the simplest perturbative descriptions of open quantum system dynamics derived from a microscopic theory. Its status as an approximation is nevertheless subtle: while perturbation theory fixes the reduced dynamics order-by-order in the system-environment coupling, solving the Redfield equation yields a particular resummation that need not preserve complete positivity. Here we compare Redfield evolution with an exponentiated (Magnus) resummation of the same second-order perturbative map, which is completely positive and trace preserving (CPTP) for a broad class of physical conditions. We also construct a simple CPTP resolvent resummation, illustrating the non-uniqueness of resummations of the same perturbative map. The Redfield and Magnus prescriptions agree to second order but differ through time-ordering corrections at higher orders. We exhibit a weak-coupling criterion which guarantees that Redfield evolution, even when non-Markovian, remains close to a CPTP map. We illustrate the criterion in two examples, focusing on finite-dimensional (qubit) systems. Our results clarify when, and why, Redfield evolution can remain reliable even when it is highly non-Lindbladian.

</details>


### [113] [Exact Recovery for Non-Abelian Surface Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03677)
*Alison Warman,Nathanan Tantivasadakarn,Sakura Schafer-Nameki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops an exact, deterministic recovery protocol for non-Abelian topological surface codes based on the quantum double D(G) of any finite group G, using an orthogonal error basis fixed by a gauge-fixing procedure and charge/flux transfer circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact recovery for non-Abelian topological surface codes is challenging because errors supported on closed loops on the lattice or dual lattice create redundancy in the error basis due to stabilizers, and existing recovery approaches are largely limited to Abelian groups.

Method: The authors determine an orthogonal error basis composed of group multiplication and irreducible representation operators. They introduce a gauge-fixing procedure to remove redundancy from stabilizers for loop-supported errors and obtain a complete orthogonal error basis on the code space. Assuming predetermined neutral correctable error clusters as input, they construct charge and flux transfer circuits that move errors onto ancillas, which are then projected out.

Result: An exact and deterministic recovery protocol is constructed for surface codes of any finite group G, including non-Abelian groups.

Conclusion: The proposed gauge-fixing and transfer-circuit framework yields a complete orthogonal error basis and enables exact, deterministic error recovery for non-Abelian quantum double surface codes for arbitrary finite groups.

Abstract: We study exact recovery for non-Abelian topological surface codes based on the quantum double $D(G)$ of any finite group $G$. We determine an orthogonal error basis, that is comprised of group multiplication and irreducible representation operators. Whenever the errors are supported on closed loops on the lattice or dual lattice, there is a redundancy in the basis, due to stabilizers. To remedy this, we introduce a gauge-fixing that results in a complete and orthogonal error basis on the code space. Assuming as input predetermined, neutral correctable error clusters, we construct charge and flux transfer circuits that move the errors onto ancillas that then get projected out. This is an exact and deterministic recovery protocol, applicable to surface codes for any finite, in particular non-Abelian, group $G$.

</details>


### [114] [Low-Overhead Quantum Error Correction with Boundary-Connected Planar Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03682)
*Oscar Higgott,Hasan Sayginel,Francisco J. H. Heras,Zhiyang He,Tomas Jochym-O'Connor,Andrew W. Senior,Lei M. Zhang,Thomas Edlich,James S. Spencer,Matt McEwen,Craig Gidney,Johannes Bausch,Pushmeet Kohli,Hartmut Neven*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: By wiring sparse, static boundary connections between planar Euclidean modules, the authors build modular hyperbolic surface and color codes (from new semi-hyperbolic code families) that cut physical qubit overhead over 10x versus the surface code, and project ~30x reduction at rate 1/16, distance >=22, with co-designed decoders, walking logic circuits, and a compact modular extractor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical quantum computers need quantum error correction, but the dominant planar surface code imposes substantial physical qubit overhead. A solution is needed that retains the fabrication advantages of planar modules while achieving high encoding rates and distances.

Method: Partition the processor into manageable flat Euclidean planar modules joined by non-local, sparse, static boundary ("seam") connections. Construct modular hyperbolic surface and color codes based on new families of semi-hyperbolic codes. Use modular syndrome extraction circuits with neural network and matching decoders; co-design low-weight, highly symmetrical logical bases, fault-tolerant walking circuits implementing logic via code automorphisms, and a modular extractor system.

Result: Circuit-level simulations show >10x physical qubit overhead reduction vs. the surface code, even at elevated inter-module seam error rates. Projected larger systems reach k/n=1/16, d>=22, exceeding rate and distance of the [[288,12,18]] two-gross bivariate bicycle code while using mostly nearest-neighbor gates inside planar modules, implying >30x overhead reduction vs. surface codes. The modular extractor is ~4.5x smaller than the code.

Conclusion: Modular hyperbolic and color codes built from planar modules with sparse static inter-module wiring enable dense, high-rate fault-tolerant memory, unlocking high-rate code efficiency without sacrificing planar-module fabrication advantages, and can serve as dense storage in a universal fault-tolerant architecture.

Abstract: Realizing practical quantum computers requires quantum error correction, but the most widely implemented approach, the planar surface code, demands a substantial physical qubit overhead. Here, we demonstrate that partitioning quantum processors into manageable, flat modules with non-local inter-module connections provides a natural solution. By wiring sparse, static boundary connections between Euclidean planar modules, we construct modular hyperbolic surface and color codes, based on new families of semi-hyperbolic codes. Using modular syndrome extraction circuits and efficient neural network and matching decoders, our circuit-level simulations show that this modular memory design can reduce the physical qubit overhead by tenfold or more compared to the surface code, even under elevated inter-module seam error rates. Projecting to larger system sizes, we find modular codes with encoding rate $k/n=1/16$ and distance $d\geq 22$, exceeding both the rate and distance of the $[[288,12,18]]$ two-gross bivariate bicycle code while using mostly nearest-neighbor gates within planar modules, and implying an over $30\times$ overhead reduction relative to surface codes. We develop fault-tolerant walking circuits for implementing logic via code automorphisms, which we co-design with low-weight, highly symmetrical logical bases and a modular extractor system that is $\approx 4.5\times$ smaller than the code itself. Combining these methods, our modular memory can be used as dense storage in a universal fault-tolerant architecture, unlocking the efficiency of high-rate codes without sacrificing the fabrication advantages of planar modules.

</details>


### [115] [Modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality for 1D non-commuting Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03683)
*Ángela Capel,David Pérez-García,Matteo Scandi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves a modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality (MLSI) with constant $\Omega(1/\log n)$ for the heat-bath and regularised heat-bath dynamics of a finite-range, non-commuting 1D spin chain Hamiltonian, implying rapid mixing of these Gibbs samplers to the Gibbs state in relative entropy and trace norm with mixing times $\mathcal{O}(\log n \cdot \log(n/\varepsilon))$. It is claimed to be the first MLSI proof in the non-commuting regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establishing rapid mixing of Gibbs samplers is central to quantum many-body and algorithmic applications, but existing logarithmic Sobolev inequality techniques largely apply to commuting or classical settings. The non-commuting regime lacks such guarantees, motivating a proof of MLSI and consequent fast mixing for heat-bath dynamics on generic finite-range 1D spin chains.

Method: The proof combines weak quasi-factorisation of relative entropy, decay-of-correlation estimates, a uniform conditional local gap, and a buffered comparison between conditional entropy and quadratic energy. The residual bounded entropy defect is then removed using the global spectral gap. The analysis covers quasi-local heat-bath dynamics, its regularised variant, and allows spatially inhomogeneous interactions.

Result: An MLSI is proven with constant $\Omega(1/\log n)$ for both heat-bath and regularised heat-bath dynamics on finite-range, non-commuting 1D spin chains. This yields rapid convergence to the Gibbs state in relative entropy and trace norm with mixing time $\mathcal{O}(\log n\cdot\log(n/\varepsilon))$, valid even for spatially inhomogeneous interactions.

Conclusion: The work provides the first proof of an MLSI in the non-commuting regime, thereby guaranteeing efficient (polylogarithmic) mixing of quasi-local Gibbs samplers for 1D finite-range non-commuting Hamiltonians.

Abstract: Given a finite-range, non-commuting Hamiltonian on a 1D chain of finite spins, we prove a modified logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant $Ω(1/\log n)$ for the heat-bath dynamics, a quasi-local Gibbs sampler, and for the regularised heat-bath dynamics. This implies rapid mixing of both samplers towards the Gibbs state in relative entropy and trace norm, with mixing times of order $\mathcal{O}(\log n\cdot \log(n/ε))$. The interactions may be spatially inhomogeneous. The proof combines weak quasi-factorisation of relative entropy, decay-of-correlation estimates, a uniform conditional local gap, and a buffered comparison between conditional entropy and quadratic energy. The resulting bounded entropy defect is removed using the global gap. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first proof of MLSI in the non-commuting regime.

</details>


### [116] [Single-Shot Error Correction at Optimal Spacetime Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03684)
*Kishor Bharti,Tobias Haug,Derek Khu,Andrew Tanggara*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This work shows that the optimal spacetime cost for storing quantum information under independent erasures can be achieved with an explicit noisy error-correction circuit and efficient decoding, given long-range connectivity and fast classical processing, using quantum Tanner codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work proved a spacetime cost lower bound of Ω(S(K+log(S/ε))) for storing K logical qubits for S steps with error at most ε under independent erasures, and gave a matching construction—but that construction assumed ideal error correction. It remained open whether the same scaling is achievable with realistic, noisy error-correction circuits and efficient decoding.

Method: The authors design an explicit noisy error-correction circuit where each memory step consists of one round of syndrome extraction followed by a fixed number of parallel classical decoder steps, reducing residual error enough that later faults remain correctable. They use quantum Tanner codes and count all state-preparation, gate, measurement, and wait locations. The construction assumes long-range qubit connectivity and fast, reliable classical processing.

Result: The same scaling Ω(S(K+log(S/ε))) is achieved with a noisy circuit and efficient decoding, adding only a logarithmic overhead shared by all stored qubits. The construction works for sufficiently weak but otherwise general circuit noise, including faults during syndrome extraction and correlated faults. For independent erasures it matches the best known lower bound up to constant factors, achieving exponentially small failure probability per memory step while keeping each step's cost linear in the code size.

Conclusion: Reliability adds only a logarithmic overhead, and the optimal spacetime scaling is attainable with realistic noisy error correction. The analysis also extends to certain constant-depth logical Clifford operations.

Abstract: Recent work established that, for independent erasures, storing $K$ logical qubits for $S$ time steps with error at most $\varepsilon$ requires a spacetime cost of $Ω(S(K+\log(S/\varepsilon)))$. A matching construction was also given, but assumes ideal error correction.
