Here we show that the same scaling can be achieved with an explicit noisy error-correction circuit and efficient decoding, counting all state-preparation, gate, measurement, and wait locations, provided that the hardware supports long-range qubit connectivity and fast, reliable classical processing. Thus, reliability adds only a logarithmic overhead shared by all stored qubits. The construction works for sufficiently weak but otherwise general circuit noise, including faults during syndrome extraction and correlated faults, and for independent erasures it matches the best known lower bound up to constant factors. We attain this scaling using quantum Tanner codes, with each memory step consisting of one round of syndrome extraction followed by a fixed number of parallel classical decoder steps that reduce the residual error enough to keep later faults correctable, regardless of memory size, storage time, or target accuracy. Together, this gives exponentially small failure probability per step while keeping the cost of each memory step linear in the code size. The same analysis also extends to certain constant-depth logical Clifford operations.

</details>


### [117] [Efficient Block Encoding of Structured Hamiltonians by Separating Where and What](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03686)
*Alessandro Summer,François Jamet*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper constructs permute-act-unpermute block encodings of structured Hamiltonians using CSWAP networks that exploit support geometry, achieving T-counts scaling with system size rather than term count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault-tolerant block encodings of structured Hamiltonians are costly in non-Clifford resources during SELECT. Existing approaches often rely on translational symmetry or factorised coefficients to reduce T cost, which limits applicability. The work aims to reduce T cost by decoupling the "where" an operator acts from "what" is applied, leveraging support geometry.

Method: Permute-act-unpermute circuits with CSWAP networks: a selected support is moved to a fixed target register, a shared local circuit is applied, then the permutation is undone. For fixed-size supports, T count scales with system size. Minimum CSWAP count for two-site supports is established within address-controlled site-transposition networks. A bridged CSWAP retains and repairs a temporary AND across the target action. Pauli terms compile to 8SN+O(log N) T gates with O(log N) permutation work qubits.

Result: For Pauli terms, circuits use 8SN+O(log N) T gates with O(log N) permutation work qubits (S=1 nearest-neighbour, S=3/2 all-to-all pairs). Bridged CSWAP halves T count of a matched forward-inverse CSWAP pair when repair is Clifford, at cost of one retained work qubit. Heisenberg ring: T count reduced by factor approaching 3 at large system size. Five-orbital Anderson impurity model: reduction about 1.7 at large bath sizes. Comparisons use lowest-cost compiled baselines with unchanged block-encoding normalisation and comparable peak ancilla counts.

Conclusion: Exploiting support geometry via permute-act-unpermute CSWAP networks and bridged CSWAP substantially reduces non-Clifford (T) cost of block-encoding structured Hamiltonians, without requiring translational symmetry or factorised coefficients.

Abstract: Fault-tolerant block encodings of structured Hamiltonians can reduce their non-Clifford cost in SELECT by selecting where an operator acts separately from what is applied. We construct permute-act-unpermute circuits whose CSWAP networks exploit the support geometry: a selected support is brought to a fixed target register, a shared local circuit acts there, and the permutation is undone. For supports of fixed size, the $T$ count of SELECT then grows with the system size rather than with the number of terms, without requiring translational symmetry or factorised coefficients. For two-site supports, we establish and attain the minimum CSWAP count within address-controlled networks of site transpositions. Compiled for Pauli terms, these circuits use $8SN+O(\log N)$ $T$ gates with $O(\log N)$ permutation work qubits, where $N$ is the number of system qubits and $S$ is determined by the support geometry, with $S=1$ for nearest-neighbour interactions and $S=3/2$ for all-to-all pairs. We also introduce a bridged CSWAP that retains and repairs a temporary AND across the target action. When the repair is Clifford, it halves the $T$ count of a matched forward-inverse CSWAP pair at the cost of one retained work qubit. For a Heisenberg ring, the complete block-encoding query reduces the $T$ count by a factor approaching 3 as the system size grows. For a five-orbital Anderson impurity model, which combines nearest-neighbour and all-to-all support geometries, the reduction is about 1.7 at large bath sizes. Both comparisons use the lowest-cost compiled baselines considered, at unchanged block-encoding normalisation and comparable peak ancilla counts.

</details>


### [118] [Universal Bounds for Out-of-Distribution Unitary Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03690)
*Joachim Favre,Armando Angrisani,Zoë Holmes*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper builds a general framework for out-of-distribution quantum dynamics learning by characterizing state ensembles through the first and second moments of their input distribution, defining bias and expressivity that jointly govern generalization between arbitrary pure-state training and testing distributions for unitary dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning the action of an unknown unitary on one family of states may reveal its behavior elsewhere, but existing results (e.g., locally scrambling and product-to-Haar bounds) are fragmented and loose. A principled understanding of when and why out-of-distribution generalization occurs for quantum dynamics is missing.

Method: The authors develop a framework based on the first and second moments of the input state distribution. These moments define a notion of bias and expressivity of an ensemble, which together control generalization between arbitrary pure-state training and testing distributions. They analyze tensor products of ensembles, single-qubit risks, and derive bounds and no-go results for channel learning.

Result: The framework shows that training on random real states suffices to learn a unitary's action on random complex states; tensor products of unbiased pure-state ensembles preserve expressivity; single-qubit risks are independent of bias. It recovers earlier locally scrambling results as loose special cases, yields tight product-to-Haar bounds, and provides no-go results and limited generalization bounds for learning certain channels.

Conclusion: Bias and expressivity of an ensemble, captured by first and second moments, govern out-of-distribution generalization in quantum dynamics learning. The results highlight the distinctive role of unitarity in enabling such generalization, while no-go results delimit what is achievable for general channels.

Abstract: How much can the action of an unknown unitary dynamics on one family of states reveal about its action elsewhere? We develop a general framework for out-of-distribution quantum dynamics learning based on the first and second moments of the input distribution. These define a notion of the bias and expressivity of an ensemble, which together control generalization between arbitrary pure-state training and testing distributions. Our framework shows that only studying the action of a unitary on random real states can learn its action on random complex states, that tensor products of unbiased pure-state ensembles preserve expressivity, and that single-qubit risks are independent of bias. It also recovers earlier locally scrambling results as loose special cases and yields tight product-to-Haar bounds. We conclude with a series of no-go results and limited generalization bounds for learning certain channels, which further serve to underscore the distinctive role of unitarity in enabling out-of-distribution generalization.

</details>


### [119] [An operational characterization of finite-dimensional quantum theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03694)
*Lionel J. Dmello,Xiangling Xu,Marc-Olivier Renou,David Gross*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper shows that finite-dimensional quantum theory can be self-tested: a finite set of two-body correlations, combined with stability under iterated teleportation, uniquely singles out quantum theory among probabilistic theories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundations of quantum mechanics aim to identify operational constraints that characterize physical theories. Bell inequalities characterize classical probability theory, and Tsirelson's bound is a first step for quantum mechanics. The dual question addressed here is whether one can certify that all correlations predicted by quantum theory are actually realizable.

Method: The authors construct a finite number of two-body correlations satisfying two conditions: (1) they are realized by the theory, and (2) they remain stable under iterated teleportation. For systems of the form (C^d)^{⊗n}, Condition (1) can be verified with poly(d,n) measurement settings. Condition (2) is formulated as a hierarchy of tests, one for each number N of teleportation steps.

Result: The only probabilistic theory that both realizes these correlations and does so stably under iterated teleportation is quantum theory. Condition (1) is efficiently verifiable with poly(d,n) measurement settings, and Condition (2) yields a hierarchy of tests indexed by teleportation steps N.

Conclusion: Finite-dimensional quantum theory can be self-tested in a meaningful sense. In particular, one can certify the existence of violations of Bell inequalities larger than any that have been directly observed.

Abstract: A key goal in the foundations of quantum mechanics is to identify operational constraints characterizing physical theories. Bell inequalities do so for classical probability theory, while Tsirelson's bound provides a first step for quantum mechanics. Here, we address the dual question: Can one certify that all correlations predicted by quantum theory are actually realizable? In this work, we construct a finite number of two-body correlations such that the only probabilistic theory that (1) realizes them, and (2) does so in a way that is stable under iterated teleportation, is quantum theory. For $(\mathbb{C}^d)^{\otimes n}$, Condition (1) can be verified using $\operatorname{poly}(d,n)$ measurement settings. Condition (2) may be understood as a hierarchy of tests, one for each number $N$ of teleportation steps. There is thus a sense in which finite-dimensional quantum theory can be self-tested. In particular, one can certify the existence of Bell inequality violations larger than any that have been directly observed.

</details>


### [120] [Maximum-Entropy Extension of Quantum Correlation Functions from Short Real-Time Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03696)
*Filippo Maria Gambetta,Sabrina Yue Wang,Raul A. Santos*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a principled method to extend short-time quantum correlation functions and reconstruct their spectral densities by treating them as covariance sequences of stationary stochastic processes, applying Burg's maximum-entropy principle to obtain a positive semi-definite all-pole autoregressive extension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sufficient spectral resolution of quantum correlation functions typically requires long-time evolution, which is difficult to achieve in both classical and quantum simulations. There is a need for a method that yields accurate spectral reconstruction from limited (short-time) data.

Method: The authors exploit the connection between quantum correlation functions and covariance sequences of stationary stochastic processes, then apply Burg's maximum-entropy principle to find the least-committal extension consistent with the measured correlation values. This corresponds to an all-pole autoregressive model whose coefficients solve Yule-Walker equations; the method extends to both scalar and matrix-valued correlation functions (including structured or continuous spectra).

Result: The extension is positive semi-definite by construction, which guarantees spectrum positivity and automatic satisfaction of the total spectral weight sum rule. The matrix generalization allows trading simulation time for additional measured correlation functions, enabling accurate time-series extension and spectral reconstruction from short-time data, even under noise.

Conclusion: A principled maximum-entropy, autoregressive framework enables reliable extension and spectral reconstruction of quantum correlation functions from short-time data, with guaranteed positivity and applicability to scalar and matrix-valued cases.

Abstract: Correlation functions are central to understanding the behavior of quantum systems, with their spectra giving direct access to quasiparticle and collective excitations. However, the long-time evolution required for sufficient spectral resolution is challenging to reach for both classical and quantum simulations. In this work, we introduce a principled approach for extending time series of quantum correlation functions and reconstructing their spectral densities, based on their connection with covariance sequences of stationary stochastic processes. This connection enables us to apply Burg's maximum-entropy principle to find the least-committal extension compatible with the measured values of the correlation function, which corresponds to an all-pole autoregressive model whose coefficients solve a set of Yule--Walker equations. The resulting extension is positive semi-definite by construction, guaranteeing the positivity of the spectrum and the automatic satisfaction of its total spectral weight sum rule. The approach applies to both scalar and matrix-valued correlation functions, including those with highly structured or continuous spectra. The generalization to the matrix case makes it possible to trade simulation time for additional measured correlation functions, enabling accurate time-series extension and spectral reconstruction from short-time data, even in the presence of noise.

</details>


### [121] [On The Complexity of Redundancy-Free Quantum Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03697)
*Matthew B. Hastings,Alexander Schmidhuber*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies redundancy-free Hamiltonians (those with vanishing trace for any product of terms in which at least one term appears an odd number of times), motivated by Hamiltonian Decoded Quantum Interferometry (DQI). It shows the corresponding partition-function hardness transitions at a quadratically lower temperature, controlled by β²d, giving an efficient classical algorithm for small β²d, NP-hardness for large β²d, a subexponential quantum algorithm for preparing thermofield states at small β²d, and QMA-completeness for ground-state energy estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Redundancy-free Hamiltonians arise naturally in Hamiltonian Decoded Quantum Interferometry, where state preparation is split into a classical decoding step and a step preparing the thermofield double state of such a Hamiltonian. Understanding the hardness of these Hamiltonians is therefore needed both for intrinsic interest and to characterize the complexity of the state-preparation step in Hamiltonian DQI.

Method: The authors analyze redundancy-free Hamiltonians, including sums of products of Paulis with no relations, characterizing complexity via the parameter β²d where d is the degree of the anticommutation graph. They introduce an "anticommutation glass," in which frustration arises purely from anticommutation relations, and develop classical and quantum algorithms, including a subexponential-time quantum algorithm for thermofield state preparation at small β²d and a polynomial-time approach based on the Feiguin-Klich Hamiltonian.

Result: For small β²d, an efficient classical algorithm approximates the partition function; for large β²d, approximating the partition function is NP-hard. A subexponential-time quantum algorithm prepares the thermofield state for small β²d. The Feiguin-Klich Hamiltonian yields a polynomial-time algorithm for preparing thermofield double states of general Hamiltonians on an interaction graph of degree d for β ≲ 1/d. Estimating the ground-state energy of redundancy-free Hamiltonians is QMA-complete.

Conclusion: Redundancy-free Hamiltonians become easy at a quadratically lower temperature than general Hamiltonians, with complexity governed by β²d, establishing both classical easiness/hardness thresholds and quantum algorithmic results that clarify the complexity of the state-preparation step in Hamiltonian DQI.

Abstract: We consider a class of redudancy-free Hamiltonians, which are those where the trace vanishes for any product of Hamiltonian terms in which at least one term appears an odd number of times. This includes Hamiltonians which are sums of products of Paulis with no relations between them. These Hamiltonians arise naturally in Hamiltonian Decoded Quantum Interferometry, which separates the hardness of preparing quantum states into two steps: a classical decoding step, and a step preparing the thermofield double state of such a redundancy-free Hamiltonian.
  We focus on the hardness of these Hamiltonians, both for intrinsic interest and to understand the complexity of the state preparation step in Hamiltonian DQI. We present several results showing that the problem becomes easy at quadratically lower temperature than for a general Hamiltonian, with the complexity at given inverse temperature $β$ depending on $β^2 d$, where $d$ is the degree of the anticommutation graph. We give an efficient classical algorithm for small $β^2 d$ to approximate the partition function, while for large $β^2 d$ we show that approximating the partition function is NP-hard. To this end, we introduce what we call an anticommutation glass, where frustration arises purely from anticommutation relations. We give a subexponential time quantum algorithm to prepare the thermofield state for small $β^2 d$, and present some results toward a polynomial time algorithm based on the Feiguin-Klich Hamiltonian. As a result of possible independent interest, we show that this Hamiltonian leads to a polynomial time algorithm for preparing thermofield double states of general Hamiltonians on an interaction graph of degree $d$ for $β\lesssim 1/d$. Finally, we show that estimating the ground state energy of redundancy-free Hamiltonians is QMA-complete.

</details>


### [122] [Exponential lower bounds on the fermionic Gaussian rank of magic states and the bosonic coherent state rank of Fock states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03699)
*Oliver Reardon-Smith*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proves the first superconstant lower bounds on the fermionic-Gaussian rank and approximate rank of magic states, showing Ω(1.4^k) terms are required, and exactly determines the coherent state border rank of bosonic Fock states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical simulation algorithms for quantum mechanics have superpolynomial runtime components linear in the number of "free" state terms needed to decompose tensor products of "magic" states. The ranks of these decompositions (stabilizer, fermionic-Gaussian ranks) are poorly understood: only quadratic lower bounds exist for stabilizer rank, no bounds beyond constants for fermionic-Gaussian rank, despite exponential upper bounds being known. This gap limits understanding of simulation complexity.

Method: The authors analyze fermionic Gaussian decompositions of the tensor product |M⟩^{⊗k} of the standard 4-qubit magic state (the state consumed to implement a swap gate in fermionic linear optics). They prove lower bounds on both the exact rank and the δ-approximate rank, with the latter bounded by the exact rank bound multiplied by a factor (1−δ²). The exact rank results extend to any product of k fixed parity non-Gaussian states. Additionally, they determine the coherent state border rank of an n-mode bosonic Fock state with m_j bosons in mode j, proving it equals ∏_j(1+m_j), and derive approximate coherent state rank lower bounds.

Result: Any fermionic Gaussian decomposition of |M⟩^{⊗k} requires Ω(1.4^k) terms. Essentially matching bounds are proved for the δ-approximate rank, lower bounded by the same quantity multiplied by (1−δ²). The coherent state border rank of an n-mode bosonic Fock state with m_j bosons in mode j is exactly ∏_j(1+m_j), confirming a conjecture from Ref. [1]; approximate coherent state rank lower bounds follow as the same quantity times a function of the approximation fidelity.

Conclusion: The work establishes the first nontrivial lower bounds on fermionic-Gaussian rank and its approximate variant, extends exact-rank results to products of fixed parity non-Gaussian states, and resolves a conjecture on coherent state border rank with accompanying approximate-rank bounds, demonstrating broad applicability of the method to classical simulation complexity.

Abstract: Recent algorithms for classical simulation of quantum mechanics have runtime whose superpolynomial component is given by a linear dependence on the number of terms required to write large tensor products of certain "magic" states as superpositions of "free" states (which may be stabilizer states, fermionic Gaussian states or others). Surprisingly little is known about the number of terms in such decompositions, called ranks (e.g. the stabilizer rank, fermionic-Gaussian rank etc.). For complexity theoretic reasons they are expected to grow exponentially in the number of tensor factors but, while exponential upper bounds are known for both the stabilizer and fermionic-Gaussian rank of magic states; the best known lower bounds on the stabiliser rank are quadratic, and no bounds on the fermionic-Gaussian rank are known beyond fixed constants. In this work we prove that any fermionic Gaussian decomposition of $\lvert M\rangle^{\otimes k}$ consists of $Ω(1.4^k)$ terms. Here $\lvert M\rangle$ is the most standard magic state for fermionic linear optics: the $4$ qubit state that may be consumed to implement a swap gate. We also prove essentially matching bounds on the $δ$-approximate rank of the same state, lower bounding it by the same quantity that bounds the exact rank, multiplied by a factor of $1-δ^2$. Our results on exact fermionic Gaussian rank apply directly to any product of $k$ fixed parity non-Gaussian states, although the same is not true of the approximate rank. Finally, we prove that the coherent state border rank of an $n$-mode bosonic Fock state with $m_j$ bosons in mode $j$ is exactly $\prod_{j}(1+m_j)$, answering a conjecture of Ref. [1] and obtain lower bounds on the approximate coherent state rank given by the same quantity multiplied by a function of the fidelity of the approximation, emphasizing the broad applicability of the method we employ.

</details>


### [123] [Scalable Passive QRAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03700)
*Siddhartha Jain,Alexander M. Dalzell,Connor T. Hann*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A blueprint for a passive, scalable physical QRAM operation whose per-query energy cost is O(log N), the first construction with polylog N energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To show that the physical QRAM operation can be implemented in a scalable, passive manner and is feasible in principle.

Method: Evolve a pre-manufactured, time-independent 4-local Hamiltonian with O(N) terms, where each qubit participates in at most 4 terms, for an n-qubit address over N = 2^n memory bits.

Result: Energy cost per QRAM query is O(log N), the first construction to use polylog N energy; runtime per query is O(log^2 N).

Conclusion: The proposed construction demonstrates the feasibility in principle of a scalable, passive physical QRAM with polylogarithmic energy and O(log^2 N) runtime.

Abstract: We present a blueprint for implementing the physical QRAM operation in a scalable, passive manner, proving its feasibility in principle. Our construction is passive because for an $n$-qubit address over $N = 2^n$ memory bits the energy cost per QRAM query is $O(\log N)$, and to our knowledge it is the first construction to use $\operatorname{polylog} N$ energy. The runtime for each query is $O(\log^2 N)$. It is achieved by evolving a pre-manufactured time-independent 4-local Hamiltonian with $O(N)$ terms where every qubit participates in at most 4 terms.

</details>


### [124] [Unitary complexity in polynomial space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03705)
*William Kretschmer,Ewin Tang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: If quantum commitments exist, then either the unitary synthesis problem is not solvable in polynomial time, or BPP ≠ NEXP; a general lemma links unitaryPSPACE synthesis to classical oracles and NEXP search, and new robust definitions for unitaryP and unitaryPSPACE are proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Showing unconditionally that quantum commitments exist would require resolving at least one of two longstanding complexity-theoretic open problems. The paper aims to expose this barrier and to fix conceptual problems in prior definitions of unitary complexity classes.

Method: The main result follows from a general lemma stating that every unitary in unitaryPSPACE either cannot be synthesized efficiently relative to any classical oracle, or can be synthesized efficiently with an oracle for NEXP search problems. The authors also propose new definitions of unitaryP and unitaryPSPACE, considering both garbage-erasing and garbage-allowing implementations, and prove robustness properties such as an entrywise-computability characterization of unitaryPSPACE.

Result: The lemma implies that quantum commitments existing forces either no polynomial-time unitary synthesis or BPP ≠ NEXP. It also shows certain oracle separations involving unitaryPSPACE would imply breakthrough classical lower bounds such as NC ≠ NP. The new definitions are robust: the two implementation notions can be viewed as special cases of each other, and unitaryPSPACE equals the set of unitaries whose entries are computable to arbitrary precision in polynomial space. Generic garbage erasure holds in unitaryPSPACE, though it provably fails relative to unitary oracles.

Conclusion: Quantum commitments cannot be shown to exist unconditionally without answering at least one major open complexity question. The proposed definitions of unitaryP and unitaryPSPACE resolve key conceptual issues from prior work, yield elegant proofs, and exhibit strong robustness properties.

Abstract: We show that if quantum commitments exist, then either there is no polynomial-time solution to the unitary synthesis problem, or $\mathsf{BPP} \neq \mathsf{NEXP}$. Thus, showing unconditionally that quantum commitments exist would require answering at least one of two longstanding open questions in complexity theory. We prove our main result as a consequence of a more general lemma, which shows that every unitary in $\mathsf{unitaryPSPACE}$ either cannot be synthesized efficiently relative to any classical oracle, or can be synthesized efficiently with an oracle for $\mathsf{NEXP}$ search problems. Our lemma has other noteworthy consequences, including that certain oracle separations involving $\mathsf{unitaryPSPACE}$ would imply breakthrough classical lower bounds such as $\mathsf{NC} \neq \mathsf{NP}$.
  Along the way, we propose new definitions for the unitary complexity classes $\mathsf{unitaryP}$ and $\mathsf{unitaryPSPACE}$. Our changes address the biggest conceptual issues with definitions suggested in prior work, and lead to elegant proofs. We study both implementations that erase garbage and implementations that allow it, because we cannot rule out the possibility that the two definitions differ. Nevertheless, we show that both definitions can be viewed as special cases of each other. We also showcase many other ways in which our definitions are robust. For example, we show that $\mathsf{unitaryPSPACE}$ has an equivalent characterization as the set of unitary transformations whose entries can be computed to arbitrary precision in polynomial space. Consequently, we deduce that $\mathsf{unitaryPSPACE}$ can generically erase garbage, a result that provably fails relative to unitary oracles.

</details>


### [125] [Quantum estimation, channel orders, and private capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03706)
*Christoph Hirche*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper links entropic channel orders to recent zero-private-capacity constructions via binary estimation and measured χ² divergence, showing that complete measured-χ² ordering (and its quantum refinements) characterizes when signed lifts and activation exist, thereby explaining why complete comparison prevents activation with antidegradable helpers while regularized comparison does not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent examples showed that zero private capacity does not imply antidegradability, and that two zero-private-capacity channels can jointly transmit private information (activation). Existing entropic channel orders bound capacities by comparing what a receiver and its environment learn, but their connection to these new constructions, and the reason activation occurs, remained unexplained.

Method: Connect entropic orders to the constructions through binary estimation, whose minimum mean-square error is governed by measured χ² divergence. Use a new integral representation showing how measured χ² on a qubit extension recovers quantum χ², enabling passage from complete measured-χ² ordering to complete quantum-χ², relative-entropy, and less-noisy ordering. Apply a signed lift and introduce Hermitian-smoothed measured-χ² ordering to characterize existence of such lifts, including with a quantum reference. Derive a finite-code reliability–secrecy bound from environmental dominance in binary estimation at every blocklength. Analyze noisy Werner–Holevo channels and extend Pauli half-erasure activation to all 1/2 ≤ p < 1 plus a new four-level family, using reference-assisted measured-χ² witnesses.

Result: Hermitian-smoothed measured-χ² ordering characterizes when signed lifts exist, even with a quantum reference. A finite-code reliability–secrecy bound follows from environmental dominance at every blocklength. For noisy Werner–Holevo channels, sharp private-capacity and antidegradability thresholds are determined and exact complementary capacities computed in a nondegradable range. Pauli half-erasure activation is extended to all 1/2 ≤ p < 1, with the same range established for a new four-level family. Reference-assisted measured-χ² witnesses show why the activating constructions lack complete comparison.

Conclusion: Complete channel comparison prevents activation with antidegradable helpers, whereas regularized comparison alone does not. Measured-χ² ordering and its Hermitian-smoothed variant provide the operative framework characterizing zero private capacity, signed lifts, and activation boundaries across several channel families.

Abstract: Recent examples have shown that zero private capacity need not imply antidegradability and that two channels with zero private capacity can nevertheless transmit private information together. Existing entropic channel orders compare what a receiver and its environment can learn and thereby bound capacities. We connect these orders to the recent constructions through binary estimation, whose minimum mean-square error is governed by measured $χ^2$ divergence. A new integral representation shows how measured $χ^2$ on a qubit extension recovers quantum $χ^2$. It lets us pass from complete measured-$χ^2$ ordering to complete quantum-$χ^2$, relative-entropy, and less-noisy ordering. Zhu and Wang used a signed lift to show that their qutrit channel has zero private capacity. We show that Hermitian-smoothed measured-$χ^2$ ordering characterizes when such lifts exist, even with a quantum reference. Environmental dominance in binary estimation at every blocklength yields a finite-code reliability--secrecy bound. These results explain why complete comparison prevents activation with antidegradable helpers whereas regularized comparison alone does not. Building on that qutrit example, we establish this stability for a range of noisy Werner--Holevo channels, determine sharp private-capacity and antidegradability thresholds, and compute exact complementary capacities in a nondegradable range. By contrast, we extend Pauli half-erasure activation to all $1/2\le p<1$ and establish the same range for a new four-level family. Reference-assisted measured-$χ^2$ witnesses show why these activating constructions lack complete comparison.

</details>


### [126] [How to Build Pseudorandom Unitaries in Microcrypt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03711)
*Aditya Gulati,Dakshita Khurana,Kabir Tomer*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Relative to a classical oracle, pseudorandom unitaries (PRUs) can exist without quantum-computable one-way functions, demonstrated via five random diagonal phase layers interleaved with Hadamard transforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establish whether PRUs imply quantum-computable one-way functions, a fundamental question in quantum cryptography. Prior worst-case classical-oracle evidence (Kretschmer, Qian, Tal, STOC 2025) was limited; this work strengthens the separation to QMA-aided adversaries.

Method: Prove that U = F5 H F4 H F3 H F2 H F1 with five independent random diagonal phase layers forms a strong PRU secure under adaptive, controlled access to the unitary, its inverse, transpose, and conjugate. Then instantiate the phase layers with a classical random oracle O, yielding security against uniform BQP adversaries with coherent access to Boolean completions of classical-input QMA^PH^O decision problems.

Result: The construction remains a strong PRU even against adversaries that can invert quantum-computable one-way functions and solve efficiently verifiable one-way puzzles relative to O. This provides a black-box separation showing PRUs do not imply quantum-computable one-way functions.

Conclusion: Classical-oracle evidence shows PRUs do not imply quantum-computable one-way functions, substantially strengthening prior classical-oracle results. The simple, efficient circuit querying a random function suggests concrete implementation via the random-oracle heuristic.

Abstract: We provide the first evidence relative to a classical oracle that pseudorandom unitaries (PRUs) can exist without quantum-computable one-way functions.
  To obtain this result, we first prove that five independent random diagonal phase layers interleaved with Hadamard transforms \[ U=F_5HF_4HF_3HF_2HF_1 \] form a strong PRU with security under adaptive, controlled access to the unitary, its inverse, transpose, and complex conjugate.
  Our main result is that, when the phase layers are implemented using a classical random oracle O, the construction remains secure against uniform BQP adversaries with coherent access to suitable Boolean completions of classical-input $QMA^{PH}^{O}}$ decision problems. Such an adversary can invert (even quantum-computable) one-way functions and solve efficiently verifiable one-way puzzles defined relative to O. Our result therefore provides classical-oracle evidence that PRUs do not imply quantum-computable one-way functions, in the form of a black-box separation.
  We substantially strengthen the classical-oracle result of Kretschmer, Qian, and Tal (STOC 2025) by obtaining PRUs secure against these QMA-aided adversaries. Our construction also consists of a simple, efficient circuit that queries a random function, suggesting a route to concrete implementation via the random-oracle heuristic.

</details>


### [127] [SpiderCSS: Scalable Fault-Tolerant CSS State Preparation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03714)
*Boldizsár Poór,Andrey Boris Khesin,Sarah Meng Li,Benjamin Rodatz,John van de Wetering,Richie Yeung*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: SpiderCSS is a scalable compilation pipeline that uses ZX-calculus and fault-equivalent rewrites to generate highly optimized, provably fault-tolerant state preparation circuits for arbitrary CSS codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault-tolerant preparation of logical states is a critical primitive for large-scale fault-tolerant quantum computing, yet existing scalable and heuristic methods produce circuits with high depth, large qubit footprint, and worse logical error and acceptance rates.

Method: Starting from an idealized CSS state specification, the authors use the ZX-calculus with fault-equivalent rewrites to build a preparation diagram that preserves fault tolerance. From this diagram, SpiderCSS assembles a fault-tolerant CSS-state preparation circuit via a modular construction that is CNOT-minimal within each component and across component interconnections. Restricting to fault-equivalent rewrites makes the final circuit provably fault-tolerant without expensive verification.

Result: Monte Carlo simulations across various CSS codes up to distance 15 show SpiderCSS scales efficiently and produces circuits with substantially reduced depth and qubit footprint compared to existing scalable and heuristic methods, while achieving an average 33.7% decrease in logical error rate and a 9.5% increase in acceptance rate.

Conclusion: SpiderCSS offers an efficient, verifiably fault-tolerant compilation approach for CSS-state preparation that outperforms prior scalable and heuristic methods in depth, qubit footprint, logical error rate, and acceptance rate.

Abstract: Fault-tolerant preparation of logical states is a critical primitive for the realization of large-scale fault-tolerant quantum computing. This paper introduces SpiderCSS, a scalable compilation pipeline that generates highly-optimized, fault-tolerant state preparation circuits for arbitrary Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes. Starting from an idealized specification of a CSS state, we use the ZX-calculus and fault-equivalent rewrites to construct a diagram that prepares the given state while preserving the fault tolerance of the initial ideal ZX-diagram. From this diagram, SpiderCSS constructs a fault-tolerant CSS-state preparation circuit using a modular construction that is CNOT-minimal both within each component and also in the way these components are connected together. By only using fault-equivalent rewrites, the final circuit is provably fault-tolerant without needing expensive verification. Monte Carlo simulations across a variety of CSS codes up to distance 15 show that SpiderCSS scales efficiently and constructs circuits with substantially reduced depth and qubit footprint compared to existing scalable and heuristic methods, while simultaneously achieving an average decrease of 33.7% in logical error rate and an increase of 9.5% in acceptance rate.

</details>
